Gen AI for Developers: Hero to Architect

Part 2 of a series — depth over breadth, methodology over taxonomy. Ready to scale to a whole org? Continue to Part 3: Architect to Leader.

Who This Is For

You've read v1 (or you have equivalent breadth: you understand tokens, context windows, RAG basics, and what an LLM actually is). Now you're picking up an agentic coding tool — Claude Code, Kilo Code, or Gemini CLI — and you want to wield it well, not just survive it.

This guide is for developers at that exact inflection point: past the "what is this" stage, not yet at the "I work this way automatically" stage. The goal is to close that gap.

Assumes v1. If you haven't read the Zero to Hero guide yet, start there. This guide builds on that mental model without re-explaining it.

Prerequisites

  • v1 mental model — tokens, context windows, LLM basics, RAG concept, what an agent is. You don't need to have memorized it; you need to have internalized the shape of it.
  • Basic git and CLI fluency — you can clone a repo, run commands in a terminal, and read a diff without stress.
  • Comfort installing tools — you can follow a README to install a CLI tool, set an API key, or add a VS Code extension without needing step-by-step hand-holding.
  • No specific language required — the worked example uses Python, but the methodology applies to any stack. Adapt freely.

Framing Rules

These rules apply throughout every chapter. Understanding them up front prevents confusion when you notice patterns in how examples and tool coverage are presented:

1. Concept-first, then tool-specific. Every concept gets a tool-agnostic mental-model treatment first, then implementation in the tools that have it.

2. Kilo Code primary in IDE contexts; Claude Code primary in terminal contexts; Gemini CLI as the free-tier-friendly terminal alternative. Avoids three-way fragmentation in every example.

3. Antigravity stays as a callout in Ch 5.9 and the decision matrix — no deep dive.

4. Every chapter ends with a "see also" cross-link grid to related chapters elsewhere in the guide.

5. The CLI worked example (gitlog-report) threads through Ch 6–11 as 1-page mini-walkthroughs at the end of each methodology chapter, then gets the full end-to-end treatment in Ch 17.

6. "Kilo Code primary, Claude/Gemini as alt" callouts appear next to any tool-specific example.

1. The Agentic Coding Loop

What you'll learn: how an agent actually works under the hood — the plan/act/observe cycle, what differs from chat, where it breaks, and how to read the loop in real time.

1.1 From chat to agent — the fundamental shift

The fundamental difference between chat and an agent comes down to one thing: who holds the hands.

What chat is

  • Ping-pong: you send one message, the model sends one message back, you respond.
  • The conversation accumulates but nothing happens unless you copy text out and do something with it yourself.
  • The model has no hands — it observes the world only through your keystrokes, and it acts on the world only through your clipboard.

What an agent does

  • You give it a goal — not a question — and it loops.
  • It inspects its environment (reads files, runs commands, browses docs), decides what to do next, executes, reads the results, and decides again.
  • Continues until it believes the goal is complete, hits a resource limit, or asks for your help.
  • The model does have hands, in the form of tool calls.

Why the shift matters

  • Your mental model has to change: in chat you are the executor — you parse the response and act.
  • In an agent loop the model is the executor; you are the goal-setter, reviewer, and interrupt handler.
  • The skills transfer poorly: "Just ask it better" is a chat reflex; "Give it a tighter spec and review the plan before it runs" is an agent reflex.
  • This guide is about building the second kind of muscle memory.
v1 contrast: v1's "What is an AI Agent" section is the map — it tells you what an agent is and where it fits. This chapter is the depth — it tells you what's actually happening inside the loop so you can steer it, stop it, and diagnose it when it goes wrong.

1.2 The loop: plan → act → observe → repeat

Every agentic turn, regardless of tool, follows the same four-phase structure. The phase durations vary (planning can be milliseconds or several thinking tokens; acting can be one file read or a dozen parallel tool calls) but the sequence is invariant.

Plan
What should I do next?
→
Act
Execute tool calls
→
Observe
Read tool results
→
Repeat
Until goal met or blocked

Plan

  • The model reads its full context — system prompt, conversation history, tool definitions, and any previous tool results.
  • Generates text that either answers directly or announces a tool call.
  • When extended thinking is enabled, this phase is longer and more deliberate; the model can reason about multi-step consequences before committing to an action.

Act

  • The model emits one or more tool calls (file reads, shell commands, web fetches, editor edits).
  • Most agents can fire multiple tools in parallel if they're independent.
  • The model is not running the tools itself; the host process (Claude Code, Kilo Code, Gemini CLI) intercepts the output, executes the tools, and appends the results.

Observe

  • Tool results come back into the context as a structured message.
  • The model now sees what happened: file contents, command output, error messages, anything the tool returned.
  • This is where ground truth enters — the model's plan might have been wrong, and the observation corrects it.

Repeat

  • The model decides whether the goal is met; if not, it plans the next action.
  • The loop continues until the model calls a "done" tool, the user interrupts, or the maximum turn limit is hit.
  • Understanding that this is a loop — not a one-shot completion — is the single most important mental model shift in this guide.

1.3 Anatomy of one agent turn

Within a single turn, here is the typical sequence of events. The model receives its context, may produce reasoning tokens (if extended thinking is on), then emits text and/or tool calls. Each tool call gets executed and its result injected back before the next generation step.

About this trace

  • Annotated JSONL-style trace of a real three-tool turn: the agent reads a file, edits it, and runs a test to verify.
  • Compressed representation of actual protocol messages — real tool calls are wrapped in slightly different structures depending on the SDK, but the semantic content is identical.
// Turn N — context arrives
{ "role": "user", "content": "Add a --verbose flag to the CLI parser in cli.py, then run the tests." }

// Model plans (may include thinking tokens, not shown here)
// Model acts — tool call 1: read
{ "type": "tool_use", "name": "Read", "input": { "file_path": "/repo/cli.py" } }

// Tool result 1 injected back
{ "type": "tool_result", "content": "...file contents of cli.py..." }

// Model acts — tool call 2: edit (based on what it just read)
{ "type": "tool_use", "name": "Edit",
  "input": {
    "file_path": "/repo/cli.py",
    "old_string": "parser = argparse.ArgumentParser()",
    "new_string": "parser = argparse.ArgumentParser()\nparser.add_argument('--verbose', action='store_true')"
  }
}

// Tool result 2 injected back
{ "type": "tool_result", "content": "Edit applied successfully." }

// Model acts — tool call 3: verify
{ "type": "tool_use", "name": "Bash",
  "input": { "command": "cd /repo && python -m pytest tests/test_cli.py -q" }
}

// Tool result 3 injected back
{ "type": "tool_result", "content": "3 passed in 0.42s" }

// Model completes the turn
{ "role": "assistant", "content": "Done. Added --verbose flag. All 3 tests pass." }

What this trace shows

  • Every act is immediately followed by an observe before the next act — the model cannot "fire and forget."
  • It waits for each result before deciding the next step (unless it fires parallel tool calls, in which case it waits for all of them together).
  • This sequential dependency is why agentic tasks are slower than chat: real I/O is happening at every step.
1
Prompt arrives

The full context — system prompt, conversation history, tool schemas, and all prior tool results — is assembled and delivered to the model as a single structured input document.

2
Model plans (optional extended thinking)

The model reads the context and reasons about the next action. When extended thinking is enabled, this phase produces internal chain-of-thought tokens before the visible response; otherwise planning is implicit in the generation.

3
Tool calls execute

The model emits one or more tool-call blocks. The harness (Claude Code, Kilo Code, Gemini CLI) intercepts them, runs the actual code or I/O, and captures the result. The model itself never runs tools directly.

4
Results return — loop continues

Tool results are injected back into the context as structured messages. The model re-evaluates: task done → emit final response; task incomplete → return to step 2 and plan the next action. The loop repeats until the goal is met or a stop condition is hit.

1.4 What the agent "sees" vs what you see

This is where most early-agent confusion lives. The model's actual input is a large, structured document: system prompt + conversation history + tool definitions + all tool results from this and previous turns. You see a terminal or an IDE panel. The two views are very different, and the mismatch is a reliable source of debugging headaches.

What's hidden in the model's view

  • The model might have a <system-reminder> block injected by the harness telling it about available skills, recent memory, or permission grants.
  • You don't see that unless you specifically ask to dump the context.
  • The model's "beliefs" about what tools it has, what files it has read, and what was previously said are all locked inside that context document — which grows silently with every turn.
Concrete example: Claude Code injects a <system-reminder> with the current list of approved permissions, deferred tools, and active skills at the top of each turn. If a skill was loaded three turns ago, the model still "sees" it and can invoke it — even though your terminal shows no sign of it. If you're confused why the model is doing something, the first debugging step is: what is actually in its context right now?

The practical implication

  • Agents can appear to behave inconsistently because their context has changed, not because the model is random.
  • A long conversation where tool results accumulate, old decisions drift in and out of the context window, and injected reminders come and go will produce behavior that looks non-deterministic to a user watching only the terminal.
  • Learning to reason about what is in the context — not just what you've typed — is a core skill.

1.5 Determinism, non-determinism, and the temperature knob

Temperature is the sampling parameter that controls how "creative" the model's output is. At temperature 0 (greedy), the model always picks the highest-probability next token. At higher values it samples more broadly, producing more varied output. This matters for agentic coding: you usually want low temperature for tool-call generation (you want the model to reliably call the right tool with the right arguments), but sometimes higher temperature for initial brainstorming or architecture ideation.

Important caveat: Claude Code and Gemini CLI do not expose temperature as a CLI surface-level parameter. It's fixed or invisible in those tools. The temperature dial matters when you're calling the underlying APIs directly — e.g., through the Anthropic SDK or the Google Gen AI SDK — where you control the sampling parameters yourself. For everyday CLI use, don't look for this knob; it isn't there.

Other sources of non-determinism

  • Temperature is only one source — parallel tool calls introduce ordering ambiguity: if two tools fire simultaneously and both modify state, the outcome depends on which result arrives first.
  • Race conditions in shell commands are real.
  • File system ordering, network latency in web-fetch tools, and even OS scheduling can affect which observation the model receives first.
  • When you see "flaky" agent behavior that can't be explained by temperature, look at the tool execution layer, not the model.

The practical rule

  • For agentic coding, treat the output as probabilistic but reviewable.
  • The model will usually do the right thing on the first attempt.
  • When it doesn't, the loop gives it a chance to self-correct via the observe phase — provided you've given it enough context to recognize the error.

1.6 The control problem

The loop runs until it stops. The three main stop conditions are: (1) the model calls a terminal/done tool signalling completion, (2) the max-turn or token-budget limit is hit, or (3) the user interrupts. Understanding these is essential for working safely with agentic tools — especially when the agent has permission to write files, run commands, or make network calls.

Human-in-the-loop patterns

  • Human-in-the-loop patterns are your primary control mechanism.
  • The simplest form: the agent surfaces a plan as text before executing it, and you approve or modify before it runs — the "plan-then-confirm" gate, built into most tools in some form.
  • Claude Code's Plan Mode produces a text proposal and waits; Kilo Code's Architect mode thinks before switching to Code mode for execution.
  • The gate does not prevent errors — it just moves them earlier, when they're cheaper to fix.

Granularity of permission

  • A subtler control pattern: granularity of permission.
  • You can grant "approve all file reads" while requiring approval for each shell command, or vice versa.
  • The right granularity depends on your trust in the task: for well-understood, reversible operations (reading files, writing new files), broad permission is fine.
  • For operations with external effects (network calls, git push, database writes), tight granularity keeps you in the loop.
The most common control failure: granting broad "auto-accept" and then leaving the room while the agent runs a long task. The agent can't tell you it's confused halfway through — it will keep going, making decisions you would have caught if you'd been watching. Stay close for the first few turns of any novel task.

1.7 Plan mode vs auto mode vs interactive

Different tools use different vocabulary for their execution modes. We use a three-mode taxonomy here as a pedagogical frame — this is our framing, not an industry standard that all tools share. Knowing the underlying concept lets you map any tool's vocabulary back to the same mental model.

  • Plan mode: The agent reasons and produces a plan but takes no actions. All tool calls are suppressed. Output is text only. Use this when you want to see the agent's thinking before anything touches your filesystem or shell.
  • Auto mode: The agent executes the full loop without asking for approval at each step. Maximum throughput, minimum friction. Use this for well-understood tasks on isolated branches.
  • Interactive mode: The agent pauses before each tool call (or before "risky" ones) and asks for your approval. Maximum control, higher friction. Use this for novel or high-stakes tasks.

None of these modes is objectively correct — the right choice depends on the task scope, the reversibility of the actions, and your confidence in the spec. A common pattern: start interactive to understand what the agent is doing, build confidence, then switch to auto for subsequent runs of the same pattern.

How the three modes map to each tool:
Mode Claude Code Kilo Code Gemini CLI
Plan /plan slash command, or cycle to Plan with Shift+Tab Architect mode — reasons, produces plan, does not execute No dedicated plan mode; use /chat for text-only turns
Auto auto-accept-edits toggle (cycle to it with Shift+Tab) or --dangerously-skip-permissions flag Code mode with auto-approve enabled --yolo flag; skips all permission prompts
Interactive Default mode — prompts before each tool; Tab to approve Ask or Debug mode; approves each action individually Default (no flag) — prompts before shell/file write tools

This three-mode taxonomy is a conceptual frame we use in this guide. Each tool's actual modal UI is more granular — the table above is a best-fit mapping, not a 1:1 equivalence.

1.8 Anatomy of a bad loop

Most agent failures aren't dramatic crashes — they're subtle drifts that compound over several turns until the output is wrong in ways that are hard to trace back. Knowing the failure archetypes lets you catch them early.

The rathole

  • The agent picks up on a tangent inside the task and goes deep on it, forgetting the original goal.
  • Classic example: asked to add a feature, it notices a linting error, fixes it, notices the linter flagged three more files, fixes those, and is now three directories deep in style cleanup while the feature is untouched.
  • Early-warning sign: tool calls whose file paths have diverged from the original scope.

Drift

  • The goal shifts gradually across many turns as the context window fills and early instructions lose effective weight.
  • The model isn't ignoring you — it's weighting more recent context more heavily and has "forgotten" the preamble.
  • Early-warning sign: output that technically satisfies the last thing you said but not the original intent stated five turns ago.

Premature claim-of-done

  • The model announces completion when the task is only partly finished.
  • This often happens when the model observes a partial success signal (tests pass, no errors printed) and treats it as full success.
  • Early-warning sign: the model says "Done" but the diff is smaller than expected, or the output doesn't include all the deliverables you asked for.
The 3-turn heuristic: If after three tool-call turns you can't see clear forward progress toward the original goal, stop the agent and re-prompt with a sharper scope. Don't let it continue hoping it will self-correct — it usually won't without fresh direction.

1.9 Mental model: agents are interns with very fast hands

The most useful mental model for working with agentic coding tools: imagine a junior engineer who is extremely fast, broadly knowledgeable, eager to help, and occasionally confidently wrong. They can read a thousand-line file in the time it takes you to type a command. They know most standard library APIs. They will attempt any task you give them without hesitation.

Their weaknesses

  • They need clear specs — vague instructions produce vague results.
  • They will make reasonable-sounding decisions about ambiguous edge cases without flagging the ambiguity to you — because they are fast and eager and don't know they should pause.
  • They can get lost in a large codebase without a map.
  • They can be confidently wrong about something domain-specific that isn't in their training data (your internal API, your company's deployment process, the specific invariants of your codebase).

The management style this predicts

  • Write specs as if you're briefing a smart engineer who doesn't know your system.
  • Review every diff before merging.
  • Give them small, bounded tasks rather than open-ended explorations.
  • Check in after three turns.
  • Don't be surprised when they do something sensible but not quite what you meant — that's not a model failure, that's a spec failure.

1.10 Common failure modes and early-warning signs

A structured catalog of the failure modes you'll encounter most often, and the signals that let you catch them before they compound.

Failure Mode Early-Warning Signs
Rathole tangent — agent pursues a subproblem and loses the main goal Tool call paths drift out of original scope; no forward progress on the stated deliverable after 2+ turns
Context drift — early instructions lose weight as context grows Output satisfies recent messages but contradicts the original spec; agent "forgets" constraints stated at session start
Premature done — claims completion on partial work Diff is smaller than expected; deliverables list partially unchecked; agent stops asking for more context
Overconfident hallucination — fabricates an API, path, or behavior References a function or import that doesn't exist; tests fail with AttributeError or ImportError on first run
Spec ambiguity explosion — multiple valid interpretations, agent picks one silently Output is technically correct but not what you meant; the delta between expectation and result is hard to explain
Loop non-termination — agent keeps trying and failing the same approach Identical or near-identical tool calls appear in successive turns; error message in tool result repeats without the root cause being addressed
Scope creep — agent "improves" things you didn't ask it to touch Diff includes files outside the feature scope; refactors appear alongside the requested change
Permission cascade — agent asks for, then uses, broader permissions than needed First tool call is a permission-requesting shell command; subsequent tool calls have broader blast radius than the task requires
Stale context assumption — agent plans based on file state it read turns ago, which has since changed Edit fails with "old_string not found"; agent seems confused about current file state after its own previous edits
Tool result misread — agent misinterprets a tool result (e.g., treats a warning as a success) Agent says "tests pass" but test output includes warnings or skipped tests; shell command exits 0 but prints error text

1.11 Hands-on: trace one real agent turn

The best way to build intuition for the loop is to watch it happen in real time with a trivial task. Use the following prompt in both Kilo Code and Claude Code, then compare the traces.

Try This

Prompt to use: "Create a file called hello.txt containing exactly the text 'hello world', then read it back and confirm the contents match."

This task requires exactly three tool calls: a Write (or Bash echo), a Read, and a text comparison. It's small enough to fit in one turn, real enough to show the full loop.

In Kilo Code: Open any project in VS Code, start a new Kilo Code chat in Code mode (not Architect), paste the prompt. Watch the tool call approvals. Notice: Kilo asks for approval before each write/bash call and shows you the full tool input before executing.

In Claude Code: Run claude in a terminal in any directory. Paste the same prompt. Notice: Claude Code shows the tool name and key parameters inline, asks for approval, and prints the result. The output format is different from Kilo but the underlying sequence is identical.

What to look for: (1) How many turns does each tool take? (2) Does the agent issue all three tool calls in one turn or spread them across multiple turns? (3) What does the "observe" phase look like — how does the result appear in the UI? (4) Does either tool fire a tool call you didn't expect?

After running this, you've seen the plan→act→observe loop in the flesh. Every subsequent task you give an agent is this same loop, just longer, with more tool calls and more observations. The complexity comes from scale and ambiguity — not from any new mechanics. The loop is always this.

Gemini CLI variant: Run gemini in the same directory (with a GEMINI_API_KEY set). The same prompt works. Gemini CLI's output format differs — it shows tool calls inline in the conversation stream — but the turn structure is identical. Compare all three traces side-by-side for the clearest picture of what's tool-specific and what's universal.
→ Ch 2. Context as Working Memory → Ch 3. Tools, Side Effects & the Agent's World → Ch 10. Safety, Review & Verification

2. Context as Working Memory

What you'll learn: how the context window actually works as the agent's RAM — token economy, prompt caching, compaction, memory systems, and the anti-patterns that waste both.

2.1 The context window, in plain terms

Every model has a finite context window — a block of token slots that holds everything the model can "see" at inference time: your system prompt, the conversation history, tool schemas, tool outputs, and the assistant's own prior responses. Think of it as RAM.

What the window holds

  • The CPU (the model) can only operate on what fits in RAM right now; anything outside it is simply invisible.
  • Current window sizes (verify current at time of reading): Claude Sonnet 4.7 and Opus 4.7 both offer a 200K-token default context with a 1M-token extended tier available on request; Gemini 3 Pro supports 1M tokens natively; GPT-5 offers its own large-context tier (verify the current ceiling).
  • Bigger windows don't mean free — everything in the window costs input tokens on every turn, even content that hasn't changed.

The binary nature of "in context"

  • "In context" is binary: content is either inside the window or it isn't.
  • There is no partial awareness, no fuzzy retrieval unless you build a RAG layer on top.
  • When the window fills, either content is dropped (truncation), summarized (compaction), or the session ends.

2.2 What actually costs tokens (it's not what you think)

Developers routinely underestimate how much of their context is consumed by infrastructure rather than their actual problem.

SourceTypical sizeNotes
System prompt500–8 000 tokensCLAUDE.md auto-load can be large; tool descriptions compound this
Tool schemas (all registered tools)2 000–15 000 tokensEach function definition with full JSON schema; Claude Code with MCP servers can spike high
Prior tool results (stdout dumps)500–50 000+ tokensFull grep output, test logs, build traces — easy to bloat here
Conversation historyGrows linearlyEvery past turn is re-sent on each new turn
Files read via Read toolVaries widelyA 500-line file ≈ 3 000–4 000 tokens; reading 10 files compounds fast
Cache miss on system promptFull system-prompt cost × 1.25First turn after a schema change pays a write premium (see 2.3)

The practical implication

  • On a long task in Claude Code, tool output and read-file content often dominate the context — not your messages.
  • Tools that return verbose JSON or full file contents on every call are the biggest budget leaks.
System prompt
Your CLAUDE.md, persona rules, and project conventions — loaded on every session start; keep it lean and stable to maximise cache hits.
Tool schemas
Every registered tool's JSON definition is sent on every turn. MCP servers with many tools can add 10 000+ tokens here without any user action.
Conversation history
Every past user and assistant message is re-sent on each new turn. Long sessions pay a compounding cost for older, often irrelevant turns.
Prior tool results
Grep output, test logs, build traces — all stay in context until compacted. A single verbose grep can add 5 000+ tokens that persist for the whole session.
Current message
Your actual prompt — usually the smallest contributor. Don't over-invest in brevity here; invest in trimming the categories above instead.
Model response
Output tokens are the most expensive category (often 3–5× the input rate). Verbose responses, long reasoning traces, and over-explained answers all cost here.

2.3 The token economy — input vs output vs cached

The three token categories

  • Providers charge differently for: uncached input, cached input, and output.
  • Cache reads are typically ~10× cheaper than uncached input.
  • Cache writes cost ~1.25× the base input rate on Anthropic — the write premium amortizes over subsequent cache hits.
  • Output tokens are the most expensive category — often 3–5× the per-token input rate.
Steady-state cost formula (per turn, after cache is warm): 
cost = (uncached_in × P_input)
     + (cached_in  × P_input × 0.1)   // cache read: ~10× cheaper
     + (output     × P_output)         // output: most expensive
First-write cost formula (cache cold or invalidated): 
cost = (cacheable_prefix × P_input × 1.25)  // write premium on Anthropic
     + (uncached_remainder × P_input)
     + (output × P_output)

Multipliers vary by provider. As of mid-2026: Anthropic charges 1.25× for cache writes and 0.1× for cache reads; Google (Gemini API) uses a different cache pricing model — context caching charges a per-token-per-hour storage fee for cached prefixes rather than a write-multiplier (verify exact pricing against ai.google.dev before writing — the model evolves); OpenAI prompt caching for GPT-5 is automatic at 0.5× for cached reads. Always verify against current provider pricing pages before building cost models.

Strategic takeaway

  • Maximize the fraction of each turn that lands in cached input.
  • The steady-state formula shows cache reads are cheap.
  • Invalidating the cache (by editing an early message or the system prompt) resets the clock and pays the write premium again.

2.4 Prompt caching — how it works, when it kicks in, what breaks it

How it works

  • Anthropic's prompt caching is prefix-based: the model caches the longest matching prefix of the input that was seen in a recent prior request (TTL: 5 minutes for standard, up to 1 hour with explicit cache-control headers — verify current TTL options against Anthropic docs, these evolve).
  • The cache key is the exact byte sequence of the prefix — any edit to the system prompt, any added tool schema, or any changed message before the cached region invalidates it entirely.

Patterns that maximize cache hits

  • Freeze the system prompt. Put stable content (persona, project conventions) at the top; put session-variable content (file paths, current branch) at the bottom or pass it as user messages.
  • Keep tool schemas stable. Don't register tools dynamically mid-session. A changed schema invalidates the entire prefix up to that point.
  • Use explicit cache-control breakpoints (Anthropic API: "cache_control": {"type": "ephemeral"}) to mark where the cacheable prefix ends. This tells the API exactly how much to cache rather than relying on automatic prefix matching.
  • Avoid prepending new content. Inserting new tool results early in the message list — rather than appending — breaks the prefix for everything that follows.

What Claude Code does automatically

  • Claude Code handles cache breakpoints automatically — it marks the system prompt and loaded files as cacheable. You benefit without configuring anything.
  • What you can do is avoid actions that bust the cache: editing CLAUDE.md mid-session, changing MCP server config, or using /clear when continuation would have preserved the warm cache.

2.5 Context compaction — what gets summarized, what gets thrown out

When the context fills, each tool has its own compaction mechanism:

ToolAuto-compact triggerManual commandWhat's preserved
Claude Code~80–90% context usage/compactRecent turns, pinned files, CLAUDE.md; older turns summarized
Gemini CLINear window limit/compressRecent turns, starred context; older turns summarized
Kilo CodeBuilt into session manager (automatic)No slash command; managed transparentlyActive file context and recent task state; older tool output dropped

Compaction is lossy

  • The summarization step condenses many tokens into fewer, but nuance is lost — specific function signatures, exact error messages, intermediate reasoning.
  • If you need to preserve something precisely across a compaction boundary, write it to a file or add it to your memory file (CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, etc.) before the compact runs.

What happens after /compact in Claude Code

  • The session continues with a synthesized summary in place of the raw history.
  • The model receives this as a user message prefixed with the compaction notice.
  • Your next turn can reference everything in the summary, but can't see the verbatim prior tool calls.

2.6 The cost of long sessions (and how to keep one going)

How long sessions degrade

  • Context fills with stale tool output, and cache invalidations compound.
  • The result is both slower (more tokens to process) and more expensive (cache misses, output re-explaining stale state).
  • A 4-hour Claude Code session on a complex codebase can hit 500K+ input tokens per turn late in the session.

Techniques to extend session quality

  • Pin key files early. Use /add or ask the agent to re-read critical files at the start of a new sub-task — they'll be near the front of the window and more likely to survive compaction.
  • Clear irrelevant tool output. If a large grep result or test log was useful but is now stale, ask the agent to note the key finding and discard the raw output. This keeps the window lean.
  • Spawn sub-agents for investigation. Use Claude Code's sub-agent mode (or Kilo Code's task delegation) for exploratory tasks (reading 20 files to answer a question). The sub-agent's context is separate; only the result lands in your main session.
  • Break naturally at task boundaries. Commit, note what's next, then /clear. Restart with a concise re-briefing. This costs a cache-write premium but avoids context bloat compounding across unrelated tasks.

2.7 When to start fresh vs continue — the /clear question

The decision of whether to clear context is not just economic — it affects the model's reasoning quality. Stale context that contradicts the current state of the codebase can cause the model to make decisions based on outdated information.

Decision tree: clear vs continue 
New topic / unrelated task?          → /clear (or New Task)
Same task, but context is stale?     → /clear, re-brief concisely
Mid-task, active files in context?   → Continue (preserve cache)
After compaction, still on-task?     → Continue (summary is good enough)
Long session, model confusing state? → /clear, re-brief

Tool-specific clear commands

  • Claude Code: /clear — wipes the conversation history, preserves CLAUDE.md and tool config.
  • Gemini CLI: /clear — same behavior, wipes conversation.
  • Kilo Code: No /clear slash command. Use the New Task button in the side panel to start a fresh session. Alternatively, close and reopen the Kilo Code chat panel.

2.8 Reading the budget

Each tool surfaces token usage differently. Knowing how to read your spend in real time is essential for detecting runaway context growth early.

ToolCommand / locationWhat you see
Claude Code/costPer-turn spend: input, output, cached tokens + USD cost
Claude Code/contextBudget view: tokens used vs window size, % full
Claude Code (external)ccusage (npm: npx ccusage)Historical usage dashboard: daily/session spend, model breakdown — third-party tool, not bundled
Gemini CLIFooter bar (always visible)Running token count for the current session displayed in the terminal footer
Kilo CodeChat panel (top or bottom)Token counter and estimated cost shown inline in the Kilo Code chat UI

Reading the numbers

  • Use /cost after any turn where you suspect a large tool result landed in context.
  • If the cached-input fraction is dropping (more uncached input per turn), that's a signal the cache prefix has been invalidated — investigate what changed.

2.9 Memory systems vs context — CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, Kilo rules

Memory files are not in the context window until referenced. They are loaded at session start (or on demand) and inserted as part of the system prompt or early context. They persist across /clear; raw conversation history does not. This is the key architectural distinction: use memory files for durable project knowledge, and context for task-specific working state.

Memory file conventions across tools
ToolFile / locationLoaded when
Claude CodeCLAUDE.md (project root and ~/.claude/CLAUDE.md for global)Auto-loaded at every session start; also loads parent-directory CLAUDE.md files up the tree
Gemini CLIGEMINI.md (project root) and ~/.gemini/GEMINI.md for globalAuto-loaded at session start; same directory-walk behavior as Claude Code
Kilo Code.kilocode/rules/ directory (one or more .md files)Loaded at session start; rules files are concatenated into the system prompt

What to put in memory files

  • Put in: project conventions (language, framework, code style), key architectural decisions, file layout, personas and output format rules.
  • Do NOT put in: current task state, specific file contents (load them on demand instead), or anything that changes per-task.
  • Memory files that grow large burn system-prompt tokens on every session — keep them lean and focused.

2.10 Context anti-patterns

These are the patterns most responsible for context bloat, cache busting, and degraded session quality.

The anti-patterns to avoid

  • Paste-the-whole-file. You paste a 500-line module when you have a question about one function. Use grep or the Read tool with line ranges instead. Five targeted lines cost 30 tokens; the full file costs 3 500.
  • Paste-and-pray. You dump a full stack trace, log file, or JSON blob and ask the model to "figure out what's wrong." The model reads it, but now it's permanently in the context. Use grep, head, or a summarizing sub-agent to extract the signal first.
  • Log dump accumulation. Running tests 10 times without clearing test output. Each run appends several KB of logs. After 10 runs you have 50 K tokens of mostly-identical output. Clear between runs or capture only the diff.
  • Mid-session schema changes. Registering a new MCP server or changing tool config mid-session invalidates the prefix cache for the entire session. Make tool config changes before starting a session.
  • Abandoning sub-agents for context efficiency. Running deep investigation (reading 20 files to answer an architectural question) in the main session when a sub-agent could do the same work with isolated context, returning only the answer.
  • Editing early messages. Retroactively editing a user message or system prompt mid-session. This breaks the cache prefix and forces a full re-encode of all subsequent content.

2.11 Hands-on: measure your token usage on a real task

This exercise builds intuition for where your tokens actually go. Do it once on a non-trivial task and you'll have a mental model that lasts.

Exercise: token budget awareness
  1. Open a fresh Claude Code session on an existing project (/clear if needed).
  2. Ask it to: "Find all functions in this repo that take more than 4 arguments. List file, function name, arg count."
  3. After the response, run /cost. Note: input tokens, cached input tokens, output tokens, total USD.
  4. Now run the same prompt again without clearing. Run /cost again. Compare: the second turn should show a much higher cached-input fraction (the system prompt and tool schemas are now cached).
  5. Ask a follow-up: "Show me the full contents of the three worst offenders." Run /cost again. Note the jump in input tokens from the file reads.
  6. Run /compact. Run /cost after the next turn — note the drop in total input tokens and the cache-miss cost of the compaction summary entering as new content.
  7. Check /context before and after /compact to see the window usage drop.
What to look for: the ratio of cached vs uncached input; the spike when files are read; the post-compaction cache reset. Run npx ccusage after the session for a per-model cost breakdown.
→ Ch 4. Model Selection & Cost Engineering (caching deep dive) → Ch 13. Claude Code Deep Dive (CLAUDE.md) → Ch 14. Gemini CLI Deep Dive (GEMINI.md) → Ch 12. Kilo Code Deep Dive (rules)

3. Tools, Side Effects & the Agent's World

What you'll learn: how tool calls extend an agent's capabilities, the blast-radius spectrum from grep to rm -rf, permission models, sandboxes, and how to design an allowlist that lets the agent move fast without breaking things.

3.1 What is a "tool" to an agent

What a tool is

  • From the model's perspective, a tool is a JSON-schema-described function it may choose to invoke.
  • The runtime — Claude Code, Kilo Code, Gemini CLI — intercepts the model's tool-call output, executes the function, and injects the result back into context as a tool-result message.
  • The model never runs code itself; it emits structured intent, and the harness acts on it.

Every tool definition in the Anthropic Messages API shape carries three standard fields (name and input_schema are required; description is strongly recommended but technically optional — the model relies on it for selection):

{
  "name": "read_file",
  "description": "Read the full text of a file at the given path. Returns file contents as a string.",
  "input_schema": {
    "type": "object",
    "properties": {
      "file_path": {
        "type": "string",
        "description": "Absolute path to the file to read"
      }
    },
    "required": ["file_path"]
  }
}

Why description and schema matter

  • The description is not metadata — it is a natural-language instruction to the model about when and how to invoke the tool.
  • A weak description means the model misuses the tool or skips it entirely.
  • The input_schema is a JSON Schema object; the model's input will be validated against it before execution.
  • A schema that accepts a string where an array is needed creates ambiguous behavior at the boundary.

3.2 Built-ins you'll use daily — Read, Edit, Write, Bash, Grep, Glob

Read

  • Loads a file into context. Prefer it over a Bash cat because the harness tracks which files have been read — both for permission enforcement and to provide line-number prefixes that make Edit diffs unambiguous.
  • Always read before editing.

Edit

  • Makes a targeted string replacement in a file. Validates that the old string exists exactly once before applying the change.
  • The right tool for surgical modifications: changing a function signature, fixing a bug, updating a constant.
  • Because it sends only the diff, it is far cheaper in tokens than Write for large files.

Write

  • Creates or completely overwrites a file. Use it when producing a new file from scratch or when a complete rewrite is genuinely cheaper than a sequence of Edits.
  • Requires a Read first on existing files — the harness will refuse otherwise, protecting you from clobbering unread state.

Bash

  • Runs an arbitrary shell command — the most powerful and the most dangerous built-in.
  • Prefer narrow, read-only Bash invocations (grep, find, git log) during exploration, and reserve write/destructive commands for explicit intent.
  • The harness can be configured to require per-call approval for Bash while auto-approving Read/Edit.

Grep

  • Searches file contents by pattern. Backed by ripgrep, returning structured matches (file paths + line numbers) the agent can act on directly, with no shell to spawn.
  • Returns matches with file paths and line numbers ready for a follow-up Read.

Glob

  • Lists files matching a pattern — use it to discover structure (**/*.ts, src/**/index.{ts,tsx}) before deciding which files to read.
  • Strictly non-mutating and can always be auto-approved.
Read
When you need file contents with line-number context — always read before editing so the harness tracks state and Edit diffs stay unambiguous.
Edit
When you need a surgical string replacement in a known file — cheaper than Write for large files because it sends only the diff, not the whole content.
Write
When creating a new file from scratch or when a complete rewrite is cheaper than a sequence of Edits — requires a prior Read on existing files.
Bash
When you need to run a shell command the other tools can't express — prefer narrow, read-only invocations; reserve destructive commands for explicit intent.
Grep
When you need to find occurrences of a pattern across a codebase — returns file paths and line numbers ready for Read.
Glob
When you need to discover file structure before deciding what to read — strictly non-mutating, always safe to auto-approve.

3.3 How agents pick which tool to call

The three selection signals

  • The model selects a tool by weighing: the tool's description (primary signal), the shape of the input schema (does the data I have fit?), and prior turn context (what has already been read, what partial state exists).
  • A model that has just done a Read on a file will prefer Edit over Write for the same file because the context signal says "partial knowledge" matches Edit's stated contract.

Selection is inference, not lookup

  • If your tool description says "use this for X" and the model keeps using it for Y, the description is wrong or the schema is too permissive.
  • Descriptions that include explicit anti-patterns ("do not use this if the file is larger than 100 lines") are not pedantry — they are steering prompts baked into the tool manifest.
  • This becomes critical when you build your own MCP tools (Chapter 16): bad descriptions cause agent thrash that wastes tokens and time.

Prior context grows with every tool result

  • If the agent is mid-plan and has already called Bash to clone a repo, it will sequence subsequent calls assuming the working directory is now inside the repo.
  • Agent sessions with long tool-call chains become increasingly context-sensitive — the model's implicit state model grows with each tool result.

3.4 Blast radius — reversible vs hard-to-reverse actions

Not all tool calls are equal. A Glob that finds nothing has zero side effects; a Bash that pipes to psql in production has potentially irreversible ones. Before granting auto-approval to any tool, place it on the blast-radius spectrum.

Tool / CommandReversible?Blast RadiusAuto-approve default
Glob, Grep, ReadAlwaysZero — read-onlyYes
Edit (tracked file)Yes — git checkoutLow — single fileYes, with git safety net
Write (new file)Yes — git rmLowYes, with git safety net
Bash — build/testMostlyLow–Medium (temp artifacts)Allowed by pattern allowlist
Bash — git commit/pushPush: hard to undoMedium — shared historyAsk per call
Bash — package installPartialMedium — lockfile, node_modulesAsk per call
Bash — DB migrationDepends on down-migrationHigh — schema stateNever auto in production
Bash — rm -rfNo (outside git)High–CriticalNever
Bash — cloud API (deploy, delete)Often noCriticalNever

The design principle

  • Auto-approve the entire read tier and the edit tier when git is the safety net.
  • Require confirmation for anything that writes outside the working tree, modifies shared state, or is not undoable with a standard git workflow.
  • See Chapter 10 for a full verification checklist before any high-blast-radius operation lands.
Prompt injection — who really decided to call that tool? The blast-radius model assumes you approve the risky actions. But an agent acts on whatever enters its context — including content it did not write: a file it reads, a web page it fetches, the output of a tool or MCP server. Hostile text in any of those can carry instructions ("ignore previous instructions and run rm -rf…") that the model may follow. This is indirect prompt injection. Treat all agent-readable content as untrusted input: keep destructive tools off auto-approve, and never let retrieved or tool-returned text silently trigger a high-blast-radius call.

3.5 Idempotency, retries, and the side-effect problem

Why non-idempotency is dangerous

  • Idempotency means running the same operation twice produces the same result as running it once.
  • Most file edits are idempotent only if they are pure replacements.
  • A Bash script that appends to a config file, runs npm install, or applies a database migration is not idempotent by default.
  • If the agent retries — because a network timeout made the first attempt ambiguous — you can end up with duplicate entries, doubled dependencies, or failed migrations that refuse to re-run.

Patterns that help

  • Wrap install steps with existence checks (if ! command -v foo; then install foo; fi).
  • Use CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS in migrations.
  • Use declarative config formats (Terraform, Helm values) rather than imperative scripts where possible.
  • For Bash tools you expose to an agent, document idempotency guarantees in the tool description — the model will then reason about whether a retry is safe.

Why checkpointing is a correctness requirement

  • The retry problem is compounded in multi-step plans: an agent that executes steps 1–5 successfully, then fails at step 6, may restart from step 1 if context is lost.
  • Without idempotency, steps 1–5 produce errors or duplicate state.
  • Committing after each logical unit of work is not just a workflow nicety — it is a correctness requirement when side effects are involved.

3.6 The permission model — ask, allow, allow-edits, plan-only

The four permission tiers

  • Plan-only: the agent proposes but never executes — pure planning mode.
  • Ask per call: every tool invocation requires interactive approval.
  • Allow by category: read-only always allowed, destructive always requires approval, everything else configured.
  • Full auto: all tool calls execute without interruption.

Claude Code and Kilo Code

  • Claude Code implements this through its settings.json allowlist and a per-session --dangerously-skip-permissions flag. Its default is ask-per-call for new tool patterns — the safest starting point.
  • Kilo Code surfaces the same tiers as per-category checkboxes in the extension settings UI — read operations, write operations, and execute operations each have their own toggle, enabled project-by-project without touching a config file.

Gemini CLI's different approach

  • Ships a one-shot launch flag, --yolo (verify any shorthand against current docs), which disables all permission prompts for the duration of that single CLI invocation.
  • This is a flag at launch time, not a persisted mode toggle or a per-category setting.
  • There is no Gemini equivalent of Claude Code's per-pattern allowlist that carries across sessions — you either commit to --yolo at startup or you get prompts.
  • Choose carefully based on the risk profile of the session.

3.7 Per-project allowlists — .claude/settings.json, Kilo permission rules

Claude Code's layered hierarchy

  • Permissions resolve in order: global user settings → per-project .claude/settings.json → per-session overrides.
  • The project file is the right place for team-shared rules — commit it to the repo so everyone gets the same tool permissions automatically.

A minimal allowlist for a TypeScript project that wants read/edit auto-approved and all Bash requiring a pattern match:

{
  "permissions": {
    "allow": [
      "Read",
      "Edit",
      "Write",
      "Glob",
      "Grep",
      "Bash(npm run test)",
      "Bash(npm run build)",
      "Bash(git diff*)",
      "Bash(git log*)",
      "Bash(git status)"
    ],
    "deny": [
      "Bash(rm -rf*)",
      "Bash(git push*)"
    ]
  }
}

Kilo Code's approach

  • Does not use a file-based allowlist. Permission rules live in VS Code extension settings (the Kilo Code settings panel), stored in VS Code's settings.json at the workspace or user level.
  • Rules are configured via checkboxes per operation category — not by writing permission strings.
  • Kilo rules are not automatically shareable via git commit; they must be distributed through workspace settings or documented separately.
  • The checkbox UI is more approachable for individuals, but lacks Claude Code's granular pattern-matching for specific Bash commands.
Per-tool config conventions across harnesses
HarnessConfig surfaceGranularityVersion-controllable?
Claude Code.claude/settings.json (project) or ~/.claude/settings.json (user)Per tool + per Bash pattern (glob-style)Yes — commit the project file
Kilo CodeVS Code extension settings UI (checkboxes)Per category: read / write / executeVia workspace settings.json, not auto-committed
Gemini CLI--yolo / -y flag at launchAll-or-nothing per sessionN/A — flag, not persisted config

3.8 Auto-mode — when to use, when to fear

When auto-mode is the right choice

  • Isolated environments where blast radius is bounded by the sandbox (see 3.9).
  • Read-heavy exploration tasks where the worst outcome is a few extra file reads.
  • CI pipelines where there is no human in the loop anyway.
  • The speed gain is real — interactive approval breaks the model's flow and introduces latency that fragments multi-step plans.

When auto-mode is dangerous

  • Any tool family that writes outside the working tree, operations on shared or production infrastructure, sessions that include network-write tools (API calls, deployments, database writes).
  • The failure mode is not that the model makes a wrong choice once — it is that a single bad tool call in a 20-step plan can leave a mess that is expensive to unwind. The model cannot feel regret; it keeps going.

Rules of thumb by tool family

  • Read/Grep/Glob: always safe for auto.
  • Edit/Write: safe for auto when git is the safety net and the session is short enough that you will review the diff before committing.
  • Bash: safe for auto only when the allowed patterns are tightly scoped — test runners, linters, read-only git commands.
  • Network-write tools (MCP servers that POST to external APIs): should never be auto in anything resembling production.

3.9 The sandbox — container, devcontainer, VM, bare metal

The isolation spectrum

  • Bare metal: highest capability, highest blast radius.
  • Devcontainer/Docker: filesystem and process isolation, shared network by default.
  • Full VM: network isolation, snapshotable.
  • Ephemeral container per session: maximum safety, highest cost in setup time.
  • Each step up the isolation ladder reduces blast radius but also reduces the agent's ability to reach things it might need — local databases, test infrastructure, hardware interfaces.

Why devcontainer is the sweet spot for daily work

  • The agent gets a real filesystem, a real shell, and the project's full toolchain, but cannot write to your host filesystem or persist side effects across container teardown.
  • VS Code and Cursor ship devcontainer support out of the box; Claude Code can be run inside a devcontainer manually or via a pre-configured Docker image.
  • The cost is a 10–30 second startup penalty per session and the occasional "the tool I need isn't installed in the container" friction.

When to use ephemeral containers

  • The right choice for high-blast-radius automation — CI agents that run against feature branches.
  • A fresh container per session guarantees no state leaks between runs.
  • The agent cannot benefit from warm caches, installed dependencies, or prior build artifacts unless baked into the base image — design the base image around the agent's typical tool requirements, not developer convenience.

3.10 Tool-call observability — what just happened on disk?

Streaming JSON output per harness

  • Claude Code exposes tool-call events via --output-format=stream-json — a newline-delimited JSON stream of every event: tool invocations, tool results, model turns, and cost metadata.
  • Gemini CLI does not have an equivalent streaming JSON output flag; it logs to stderr in human-readable format, and machine-parseable output requires wrapping the CLI or using the API directly.
  • Kilo Code surfaces tool calls in the VS Code output panel as formatted log entries, not as a parseable stream.

Git as the most reliable audit trail

  • Configure the agent to commit after each logical step, and git log --oneline --stat becomes a tool-call summary.
  • For finer granularity, use hooks — Claude Code's hooks config block lets you fire shell commands on tool events (before/after Bash, after Edit, on session stop).
  • A one-line hook that appends to a session log gives you a persistent record without needing to capture the stream.

MCP server observability is a first-class concern

  • Each MCP server should log its own inputs and outputs.
  • When debugging an agent that called an MCP tool and produced a wrong result, the question is: did the model call the tool with the right input, or did the tool return the wrong output?
  • Without per-tool logs, you can only infer from context — with them, you can pinpoint in seconds.
  • Build logging into every MCP server you write (Chapter 16 returns to this).

3.11 Hands-on: design a tool allowlist for a real project

Take a Node.js API project with a PostgreSQL database. The agent should be able to read and edit source files freely, run tests and lints, and read git history — but should never push to remote, run migrations without approval, or call any cloud CLI. Here is the .claude/settings.json allowlist for that project:

{
  "permissions": {
    "allow": [
      "Read", "Edit", "Write", "Glob", "Grep",
      "Bash(npm run test*)",
      "Bash(npm run lint*)",
      "Bash(npm run build*)",
      "Bash(git diff*)",
      "Bash(git log*)",
      "Bash(git status)",
      "Bash(git add*)",
      "Bash(git commit*)"
    ],
    "deny": [
      "Bash(git push*)",
      "Bash(git rebase*)",
      "Bash(npm run migrate*)",
      "Bash(psql*)",
      "Bash(aws*)",
      "Bash(gcloud*)",
      "Bash(rm -rf*)"
    ]
  }
}

Kilo Code equivalent

  • Open Kilo Code settings → Permissions → enable "Read files" and "Edit/Write files" auto-approve; leave "Execute commands" as ask-per-call.
  • In the system prompt rules field, add: "Never run git push, psql, npm run migrate, or any cloud CLI command without explicit user confirmation."
  • Kilo's category granularity is coarser than Claude Code's pattern matching — the system prompt becomes the second enforcement layer for specific Bash commands.

Testing your allowlist

  • Run the agent on a throwaway branch, watch the approval prompts it triggers, and refine.
  • A good allowlist produces zero prompts for the 80% of operations that are routine and exactly one prompt for the 20% that carry real risk.
  • If you are getting prompts on every file read, the allowlist is too tight; if the agent is pushing to remote without asking, it is too loose.
→ Ch 10. Safety, Review & Verification → Ch 11. Multi-Agent & Parallel (worktrees) → Ch 16. MCP Servers — Using and Building

4. Model Selection & Cost Engineering

What you'll learn: pick the right model for each task, understand the three cost dials (capability/cost/latency), exploit caching and batching, and build a token budget that survives a feature-build cycle.

4.1 The model landscape now — Claude 4.7, GPT-5, Gemini 3, open-weight

The frontier as of mid-2026 has consolidated around four families, each with meaningfully different capability/cost/latency tradeoffs. The velocity of releases means any specific version numbering here may be stale within months — treat what follows as a structural map, not a spec sheet.

FamilyTop modelSpeed tierOne-line character
Anthropic ClaudeClaude 4.7 OpusMedium–slow on extended thinkingBest at long-horizon reasoning, safety-first design, large context coherence
OpenAI GPTGPT-5Fast (mini) to medium (full)Broadest plugin/tool ecosystem, strong at structured output, widely integrated
Google GeminiGemini 3 ProFlash is extremely fast; Pro is mediumStrongest at very-long-context recall, competitive free tier via AI Studio
Open-weightLlama 4, Qwen 3, DeepSeek R2Hardware-dependentZero inference cost, full local/private, capability gap closing fast

The open-weight tier

  • Llama 4 (Meta), Qwen 3 (Alibaba), and DeepSeek R2 are the three families worth tracking in 2026.
  • At Q4/Q8 quantization on a modern consumer GPU (RTX 4090 or Apple M3 Ultra), 70B-class models produce output that competes with 2024-era frontier on coding tasks.
  • The capability gap against today's frontier Opus/GPT-5 class is real but shrinking — and the cost gap is absolute zero once the hardware is amortized.
Claude Opus
Best for architecture, deep code review, complex multi-step reasoning, and any task where a wrong answer has high recovery cost.
Claude Sonnet
Best for daily coding, medium-complexity tasks, and interactive sessions — fast enough to stay in flow, capable enough for 90% of coding work.
Claude Haiku
Best for classification, intent parsing, short-form generation, and any pipeline step that runs thousands of times per hour.
GPT-5
Best when broadest ecosystem integration matters — most SaaS products and off-the-shelf agents default to the OpenAI API shape.
Gemini 3 Pro
Best for very-large-context tasks (500K–1M token fills) and free-tier experimentation via Google AI Studio.
Gemini 3 Flash
Best for real-time UI applications and high-throughput pipelines where low latency matters more than maximum reasoning depth.
Local (Ollama / LM Studio)
Best when data cannot leave the device (privacy, compliance, air-gap) or when high-volume throughput makes cloud per-token cost prohibitive.

4.2 The three dials — capability vs cost vs latency

The three dials

  • Capability: the model's ability to reason correctly on hard tasks — long context coherence, multi-step planning, subtle bug detection.
  • Cost: the dollar price of input and output tokens.
  • Latency: time-to-first-token and total generation time.
  • Turning one dial up typically turns at least one other down.

The pick rule

  • Use the cheapest model that can do the job correctly at the latency your workflow tolerates.
  • "Can do the job correctly" is the constraint that prevents you from always picking Haiku/Flash — if the model hallucinates on your task, the downstream debugging cost exceeds the model savings.
  • "At the latency your workflow tolerates" is the constraint that prevents you from always picking Opus/GPT-5 — if your task is synchronous and user-facing, a 20-second response is not acceptable regardless of how good it is.

Calibration exercise

  • Run the same 10-prompt benchmark across three model tiers on your actual workload.
  • Measure accuracy (does the output work without correction?), latency (wall clock from call to response), and cost (tokens in/out). Plot them.
  • You will almost always find a tier that dominates on cost/latency with acceptable accuracy loss — that is your workhorse model. The top tier becomes the specialist you reach for when the workhorse fails.

4.3 Claude family — Opus / Sonnet / Haiku and what each is for

Three distinct price/capability points

  • Opus is the architect model: use it for initial system design, complex refactors requiring whole-codebase reasoning, security analysis, and any task where a wrong answer has high recovery cost. Its extended thinking mode (section 4.8) is most valuable here.
  • Sonnet is the daily coder: fast enough for interactive use, capable enough for the 90th percentile of coding tasks, and priced to make routine feature work economically sensible.
  • Haiku is the volume model: use it for classification, intent parsing, short-form generation, embedding-style tasks, and any pipeline step that runs thousands of times per hour.
ModelBest forContext windowRelative cost tier (verify current)
Claude 4.7 OpusArchitecture, deep code review, complex reasoning200K (extended tiers available)High — typically ~10–15× Haiku
Claude 4.7 SonnetDaily coding, medium-complexity tasks200KMedium — typically ~3–5× Haiku
Claude 4.7 HaikuVolume tasks, classification, fast inline tasks200KLow — baseline reference tier

Pricing note

  • Anthropic's per-million-token prices have tiered structures that change with demand and model generation.
  • Opus 4.7's 1M-token context tier carries a different (higher) price than the standard 200K tier — the extended context capability is a separate offering.
  • Verify current pricing at docs.anthropic.com before budgeting — figures here are structural, not binding (verify current at time of reading).

4.4 GPT family — GPT-5, GPT-5 mini and what each is for

GPT-5 and GPT-5 mini

  • GPT-5 is OpenAI's current flagship: strong reasoning, excellent structured output (JSON mode and function calling have been a GPT strength since GPT-4), and the widest third-party integration surface — more SaaS products connect to the OpenAI API than any other provider.
  • GPT-5 mini is the cost-optimized tier, offering substantially lower token prices at the expense of reasoning depth on hard tasks. The mini tier is the right default for volume API workloads where GPT is the required provider.

Ecosystem breadth advantage

  • More harnesses, more LangChain integrations, more off-the-shelf agents are built against the OpenAI API shape.
  • If you are buying an AI coding tool rather than building one, GPT integration is the default.
  • If you are building your own agent, the API shape is largely compatible with Claude's via OpenAI-compatible adapters.
  • Verify current pricing at platform.openai.com/docs/pricing — the relative cost structure is similar to Claude (flagship ~10× mini) but exact figures shift frequently (verify current at time of reading).

4.5 Gemini family — 3 Pro, Flash and what each is for

Gemini 3 Pro and Flash

  • Gemini 3 Pro is notable for two things: the strongest long-context recall of any frontier model (handles 1M+ token contexts with less mid-context degradation than 2024-era competitors), and a competitive free tier through Google AI Studio.
  • Gemini Flash is the speed/cost tier — genuinely fast time-to-first-token, suitable for real-time UI applications and high-throughput pipelines. Flash is the model Gemini CLI uses by default for interactive sessions (verify current default against Gemini CLI docs).

The free tier

  • Google AI Studio allows Gemini API calls at no charge up to a rate limit threshold (verify current limits at ai.google.dev).
  • For a solo developer doing experimentation, prototyping, or light daily coding assistance, this can mean genuinely zero AI inference spend.
  • Free-tier rate limits make it impractical for production workloads or multi-agent parallelism.

Long-context advantage

  • If your workflow involves ingesting large codebases, lengthy documentation sets, or multi-file refactors, Gemini 3 Pro has measurably better recall at 500K–1M token fill levels than Claude or GPT equivalents as of mid-2026.
  • This changes the model-selection calculus for specifically large-context tasks.
  • Verify current Gemini pricing at ai.google.dev/pricing (verify current at time of reading).

4.6 Local models — Ollama, LM Studio, when they make sense

The two dominant runtimes

  • Ollama is CLI-first: ollama run llama4 downloads and runs a model with no GUI, exposes an OpenAI-compatible REST endpoint at localhost:11434, and integrates with any tool that accepts an OpenAI API base URL override.
  • LM Studio is GUI-first: a macOS/Windows/Linux desktop app with model browsing, quantization selection, and a built-in chat UI, also exposing a compatible local API endpoint.

When local inference makes sense

  • Privacy requirements: code, prompts, or data cannot leave the device — legal, compliance, or employer policy constraints.
  • Cost requirements: high-volume pipelines where per-token cloud costs exceed hardware amortization.
  • Offline requirements: air-gapped environments, travel, or unreliable connectivity.
  • Does not make sense when capability is the binding constraint — a 70B local model on consumer hardware will not match Opus or GPT-5 on genuinely hard reasoning tasks, and the capability gap matters for architecture-level decisions.

Hardware baseline

  • Apple Silicon M2 Pro or later (32 GB unified memory minimum for 70B Q4), or an RTX 4070 or better (12 GB+ VRAM for 7–13B, 24 GB for 70B Q4).
  • On Apple M3 Ultra, Llama 4 70B at Q4 produces ~30–50 tokens/second — fast for batch tasks, borderline acceptable for interactive use.
  • A cloud Haiku or Flash call is typically faster wall-clock if network latency is low. Local wins on privacy and sustained high-volume throughput.

4.7 BYO-model in Kilo Code — routing, fallbacks, mode-specific picks

Per-mode model routing

  • Each mode (Architect, Code, Ask, Debug, etc.) can be assigned a different model independently.
  • Architect mode should use a high-capability model — runs infrequently, output drives downstream work.
  • Ask mode (used dozens of times per session) should use a fast, cheap model.
  • Config surface: Kilo Code settings UI under the Modes section. Verify the exact config file path and schema (look for .kilocode/ directory or .roomodes file — verify against current Kilo Code docs, as the config format has evolved across versions).

Illustrative routing pattern

  • This is a suggested pattern, not Kilo Code's shipped default — your Kilo installation's actual defaults will differ.
  • Architect mode → Claude 4.7 Opus (deep reasoning, infrequent).
  • Code mode → Claude 4.7 Sonnet (fast, capable for routine coding).
  • Ask mode → Claude 4.7 Haiku or Gemini Flash (speed-optimized, high-frequency).
  • Debug mode might use Sonnet by default and escalate to Opus if the session produces more than 5 failed fix attempts.

Fallback routing

  • Fallback routing (cheaper model first, escalate on failure or low confidence) is not natively built into Kilo Code's mode system as of mid-2026.
  • Implement it at the orchestration layer: a custom mode whose system prompt instructs the model to explicitly mark tasks as requiring deep reasoning before the harness routes them.
  • Alternatively, use the Kilo orchestrator pattern (Boomerang Tasks) where a lightweight orchestrator delegates subtasks to specialist sub-agents running stronger models for hard subtasks only.
Model routing surfaces across the three major harnesses
HarnessHow to configure model per taskGranularity
Kilo CodeSettings UI → Modes → per-mode model selector; or edit .kilocode/ workspace config / .roomodes file (verify current path)Per mode — Architect, Code, Ask, Debug, etc.
Claude CodeCLAUDE.md model preference line or skill-specific SKILL.md model directive; CLI flag --model claude-... per invocationPer session or per skill; no native per-task routing without a wrapper
Gemini CLI--model gemini-3-pro or --model gemini-3-flash flag at launch (verify current flag names against Gemini CLI docs)Per CLI invocation; no mid-session switching

4.8 Thinking / extended thinking — what it buys, what it costs

What extended thinking is

  • Extended thinking (Claude's term) or "reasoning" mode (OpenAI's o-series framing): models perform explicit chain-of-thought deliberation before producing their final answer.
  • Thinking tokens are generated internally, typically billed at a reduced rate compared to output tokens — a hard problem might consume 10,000–30,000 thinking tokens before the visible response appears.
  • The latency cost is the more practical constraint: extended thinking adds 10–60 seconds to response time for hard problems.

When it pays vs when it wastes

  • Pays: multi-step logical problems with no obvious greedy path (algorithmic design, security threat modeling, root-cause analysis of complex bugs); tasks where the model has historically hallucinated on the first attempt; tasks where the cost of a wrong answer exceeds the cost of the extra thinking budget.
  • Wastes: well-defined mechanical tasks (generate a CRUD endpoint, rename a variable, write a migration); tasks with a clear single-step answer; any interactive session where 30-second latency breaks developer flow.

Practical budget guidance

  • Set a thinking budget cap (Anthropic's API: budget_tokens parameter — verify current parameter name).
  • A budget of 5,000–8,000 tokens handles most medium-hard coding problems without runaway cost.
  • Set it to 0 for routine tasks (the model will not invoke thinking). Reserve unlimited budget for the rare genuinely hard problem.
  • Track thinking token spend separately — it will surprise you in mixed-use sessions.

4.9 Prompt caching as a cost lever — beyond the basics

How caching works

  • The provider stores a prefix of your prompt on their inference infrastructure and skips re-processing it on subsequent calls.
  • Cache hit discount: historically 90% reduction in input token cost for cached prefixes on Claude (verify current discount at docs.anthropic.com).
  • The catch: the cache only hits if the prefix is byte-for-byte identical from call to call. Any character change invalidates the cache for all content after the change point.

Three rules for maximizing cache hit rate

  • Keep system prompts static: do not interpolate dynamic values (timestamps, session IDs, user names) into the system prompt; move dynamic content to the human turn.
  • Append, don't insert: if you must modify a long prompt between calls, add new content at the end rather than inserting it in the middle; insertion invalidates everything downstream.
  • Monitor hit rate explicitly: the Anthropic API returns cache_read_input_tokens and cache_creation_input_tokens in the usage object; track the ratio per session to validate your caching strategy is working.

Worked example: the scale of savings

  • A coding session with a 10,000-token system prompt running 40 turns: without caching → 40 × 10,000 = 400,000 input tokens at full price.
  • With caching and stable prefix: 1 × 10,000 cache-creation tokens + 39 × 10,000 cache-read tokens. If cache reads cost 10%, the session's system-prompt input cost drops by ~88%.
  • At scale, this is the single highest-leverage cost reduction available without changing models.

4.10 Batching, message batches, async wins

What batch APIs offer

  • Submit multiple API requests as a single job, processed asynchronously with a completion window (typically up to 24 hours).
  • In exchange for accepting async delivery, providers offer significant price reductions — historically ~50% discount on batch vs. synchronous pricing.
  • Verify current discount and SLA for each provider — Anthropic's Message Batches API, OpenAI's Batch API, and Google's equivalent each have their own current tiers (verify current at time of reading).

Right and wrong use cases

  • Right for: generating documentation across a large codebase (hundreds of files, no user waiting), running evals over a test set, processing logs or data, classification pipelines, and any task where latency is not user-facing.
  • Wrong for: any interactive session, any task in the critical path of a deployment, and any task where the next step depends on the result of the current one (sequential dependencies prevent batching).

Implementation notes for Claude Message Batches

  • The API accepts an array of request objects, each with its own custom_id for result matching. Results are polled or retrieved via a results endpoint after the batch completes.
  • There is no streaming within a batch — each request produces a complete response.
  • Error handling must account for partial failure: some requests may succeed while others fail; the custom_id map lets you retry only the failed items.
  • This is meaningfully different from a synchronous retry loop.

4.11 Context size vs effective usable context

Nominal vs effective context

  • Nominal windows: Claude 4.7 supports 200K tokens (with extended tiers), Gemini 3 Pro supports 1M+ tokens, GPT-5 supports 128K–1M depending on tier.
  • These numbers do not mean you can fill them and get uniform quality.
  • The "lost in the middle" problem — information placed in the middle of a very long context is retrieved less reliably than information at the beginning or end — is documented across all frontier models, though severity varies.

The degradation gradient

  • Rule of thumb (model-dependent, verify against current evals): performance begins to degrade past approximately 50–70% context fill for most coding tasks.
  • This is a gradient, not a cliff — the threshold depends on task type (factual retrieval degrades before reasoning), content structure (well-structured code is retrieved more reliably than prose), and model.
  • Gemini 3 Pro has demonstrated stronger long-range recall than 2024-era frontier models at high fill levels — a genuine architectural advantage for large-context use cases.

Practical consequences

  • For a 200K Claude session, treat ~120K–140K as your reliable working space.
  • If your actual content needs more: use retrieval (semantic search over the codebase, returning only relevant chunks) rather than whole-file loading; break the task into smaller subtasks with fresh context windows; or consider Gemini 3 Pro if the use case truly requires dense 500K+ context.
  • Never pad context with marginally relevant content — every token of irrelevant context increases the chance that relevant content is displaced or diluted.

4.12 The cost-per-feature mental model

The cost formula

  • cost = input_tokens_per_turn × turns × turns_per_feature × input_price + output_tokens_per_turn × turns × turns_per_feature × output_price
  • The turns-per-feature variable is the one most developers underestimate — a feature that looks like "write the CRUD endpoint" typically involves 8–15 turns: initial plan, scaffold, write route, write model, write tests, fix lint error, fix test failure, refactor for consistency, add validation, handle edge case, final review. Not one.

Worked example: one CRUD endpoint

  • Claude 4.7 Sonnet, 12 turns, average 4,000 input tokens/turn and 800 output tokens/turn.
  • At approximately $3/million input tokens and $15/million output tokens (verify at docs.anthropic.com): input ≈ $0.144; output ≈ $0.144; total ≈ $0.29 per endpoint.
  • With prompt caching hitting 80% of input tokens at a 90% discount: input drops to ~$0.04 — total with caching ≈ $0.18 per endpoint.

Scaling to a feature sprint

  • 20 endpoints + 10 service methods + 5 complex refactors: without caching ~$0.29 × 35 ≈ ~$10.
  • With caching and Sonnet routing (reserving Opus for the 5 complex refactors): closer to $4–6.
  • Routing Opus tasks to Sonnet when possible and batching non-interactive documentation generation could halve the total again.
  • Cost-per-feature thinking turns model selection from an abstract API preference into a concrete budget line item.

4.13 Decision tree — which model for which job

Use the following tree as a starting point. The right answers for your specific tasks will differ — calibrate against your own benchmarks after reading section 4.12.

Is the task interactive (user waiting)?Is it high-complexity reasoning?Is volume high (>100/day)?Recommended tier
YesYesNoOpus / GPT-5 (capability wins)
YesNoNoSonnet / GPT-5 mini / Flash (speed + capability balance)
YesNoYesHaiku / Flash / GPT-5 mini (speed + cost win)
NoYesNoOpus via Batch API (async, cost-optimized)
NoNoYesHaiku / Flash via Batch API (maximum throughput, minimum cost)
EitherEitherEither — privacy requiredLocal model (Ollama / LM Studio) — match size to task
  1. Start with task type: Is it a design/architecture decision, a mechanical code generation task, a classification/parsing task, or a documentation/explanation task?
  2. Apply the latency constraint: Is the user blocking on this response? If yes, the fastest acceptable-quality model wins over the cheapest.
  3. Apply the volume constraint: Will this run more than 100 times today? If yes, move one tier down from your initial pick and validate accuracy holds.
  4. Apply the context constraint: Does the task require >150K tokens of context? If yes, consider Gemini 3 Pro for its long-range recall advantage.
  5. Apply the privacy constraint: Does the content have legal or policy constraints on leaving the device? If yes, go local regardless of capability tradeoffs.
  6. Apply the cost constraint: Does your budget allow for the selected model across the expected volume? If no, add batching and/or caching, then re-evaluate.

4.14 Hands-on: budget a real feature build

Take last week's work — a specific feature or set of tasks you actually completed with an AI coding assistant. Reconstruct the session structure: how many distinct tasks, how many turns each, roughly how much context each turn carried. Use your harness's usage tracking (ccusage for Claude Code, the API usage dashboard for direct API use, Kilo Code's token counter in the status bar) to pull actual numbers if available.

Exercise steps
