  • Step 1 — list each distinct task from the week and classify it (design/architecture, mechanical coding, classification, documentation).
  • Step 2 — for each task, identify which model you used and estimate the token cost using the cost-per-feature formula from 4.12.
  • Step 3 — re-route each task to the cheapest model that would have produced acceptable output (use the decision tree from 4.13 as a guide).
  • Step 4 — identify which tasks could have used the Batch API (non-interactive, no sequential dependencies).
  • Step 5 — estimate the savings from re-routing + batching + caching, and express it as a percentage of actual spend.

What to expect

  • Most developers find 40–60% savings available without any quality loss — primarily from three sources: using Sonnet instead of Opus for tasks that did not require deep reasoning, batching documentation and test generation that ran synchronously, and stabilizing system prompts to enable cache hits.
  • The goal is not to squeeze every dollar — it is to build the habit of treating token spend as a real engineering variable, like memory or latency, with explicit tradeoffs that you control.
→ Ch 12. Kilo Code Deep Dive (modes & routing) → Ch 13. Claude Code Deep Dive (fast mode) → Ch 14. Gemini CLI Deep Dive (free tier)

5. IDE vs Terminal: Choosing Your Surface

What you'll learn: install and run the three primary agentic coding tools (Kilo Code, Claude Code, Gemini CLI), understand where each fits, place the wider field (Cursor, Aider, etc.) in context, and choose deliberately for the task in front of you.

5.1 The dominant idioms — IDE-resident vs terminal-resident

IDE-resident tools

  • Embed directly inside your editor — VS Code, Cursor, Windsurf, JetBrains. They see the file tree, open editor tabs, inline diagnostics, and the selection you've highlighted.
  • Interaction model is visual: proposed changes appear as inline diffs you accept or reject, context is injected automatically from the current file or workspace, and the feedback loop runs at the speed of a keypress.
  • For developers who already live inside an IDE all day, the cognitive overhead is nearly zero.

Terminal-resident tools

  • Live in your shell — invoked like any other CLI program, read context from the working directory and flags you pass, output streams to stdout.
  • Composable: you can pipe them, script them, run them in CI, invoke them headlessly from a Makefile, or orchestrate multiple agents across repos without ever opening a GUI.
  • The interaction model is conversational and scriptable rather than visual and point-and-click.

Neither is strictly superior

  • IDE-resident tools reduce friction for single-file or single-project edits where visual diff review matters.
  • Terminal-resident tools win on automation, reproducibility, and anything that needs to run without a human in the loop.
  • Most professional engineers end up using at least one from each column, picking the right surface for each class of task.

5.2 The three primary tools — Kilo Code, Claude Code, Gemini CLI

The three tools and what they represent

  • Kilo Code: open-source VS Code extension, model-agnostic, IDE-resident primary.
  • Claude Code: Anthropic's official CLI agent — runs in the terminal, reads your local codebase, executes multi-step tasks autonomously. Terminal-resident primary from the Claude ecosystem.
  • Gemini CLI: Google's open-source terminal agent backed by the Gemini model family, with a notable free tier via personal Google account. Terminal-resident primary from the Gemini ecosystem.

Why these three

  • Together they give you direct experience of both idioms, two distinct terminal-agent philosophies, two billing models, and two underlying model families.
  • After working through the hands-on sections and the deep-dives in Part 3, you will have enough first-hand context to evaluate any new tool that emerges against familiar reference points.

5.3 Kilo Code — what it is + first run

What Kilo Code is

  • A fork of Cline (formerly Claude Dev), maintained as a standalone open-source project. Installs as a VS Code extension.
  • Supports any model provider through API keys (Anthropic, OpenAI, Google, AWS Bedrock, Azure, local Ollama, and others).
  • Exposes multiple interaction modes: Code (autonomous file edits), Architect (planning and reasoning), Ask (Q&A without writes), Debug (targeted diagnostics).
  • The multi-model routing capability is the key differentiator: configure cheaper, faster models for Ask mode and reserve more capable models for Code and Architect.

Install and first run

  • Open VS Code → Extensions (Ctrl+Shift+X / Cmd+Shift+X) → search Kilo Code → Install.
  • On first launch a setup panel appears — choose your provider and paste your API key (or use BYO key). Once configured, open the Kilo Code panel from the sidebar activity bar.
  • First prompt: select Code mode, type "Add a brief docstring to every function in this file that is missing one." Kilo reads the active file, proposes a diff, and waits for your approval.
  • Watch the mode selector and the token counter in the status bar — these are the first signals for developing cost intuition.
┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Kilo Code  ▼ Code  │ Architect │ Ask │ Debug │
├──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Context: main.py (active) + workspace   │
│  Provider: Anthropic · claude-sonnet-4-7 │
│  [Send message...]                       │
└──────────────────────────────────────────┘

Detailed Kilo Code configuration, mode-routing strategies, and provider selection are covered in Ch 12.

5.4 Claude Code — what it is + first run

What Claude Code is

  • Anthropic's official agentic CLI, designed to operate autonomously in the terminal — reading files, writing code, running shell commands, searching the web, and executing multi-step plans with minimal hand-holding.
  • Understands your entire local codebase, not just the file currently open — particularly powerful for cross-cutting tasks like refactors, security reviews, and dependency upgrades.
  • Exposes a rich hooks system and a Model Context Protocol (MCP) integration layer that lets you attach external tools and data sources.

Install instructions can shift between releases (npm package vs native binary distributions have both appeared in the official docs). Always verify the current canonical installation method at docs.claude.com/claude-code before running any command you copy from a guide. At the time of writing the npm route is:

npm install -g @anthropic-ai/claude-code
claude login

First run

  • claude login opens a browser OAuth flow that ties the CLI to your Anthropic account.
  • First prompt in a project directory: cd my-project && claude, then type "Summarise this codebase in three paragraphs." Claude Code reads the tree, samples key files, and responds.
  • From there, try "Find all places where we call the external API without retry logic." — a task that would be tedious to do manually but runs in seconds.
  • Claude Code billing, Pro/Max plan details, and full feature coverage are in Ch 13.

5.5 Gemini CLI — what it is + first run

What Gemini CLI is

  • Google's open-source terminal agent, backed by the Gemini model family and featuring a 1 million token context window.
  • Headline differentiator: free tier for anyone with a personal Google account — approximately 1,000 requests per day at no charge (always verify current limits at the official repository and Google AI Studio, as free-tier quotas are subject to change).
  • Available at github.com/google-gemini/gemini-cli — check the repository for the current canonical package name and install method before running any command from a guide, as these have changed across releases.

At the time of writing, the npm route is:

npm install -g @google/gemini-cli
# or without a global install:
npx @google/gemini-cli

Authentication modes and first run

  • Personal Google account: run gemini, choose the personal login option, and complete the browser OAuth flow — this gives you the free tier.
  • API key: obtain a key from Google AI Studio (aistudio.google.com), set GEMINI_API_KEY, and run.
  • Vertex AI: for enterprise deployments with GCP credentials and data residency requirements.
  • First prompt: in any directory, type "List the top three security concerns in this codebase." The 1M context window means Gemini CLI can ingest very large repos without chunking.
  • Full feature coverage is in Ch 14.

5.6 IDE strengths vs terminal strengths

Where IDE tools shine

  • Inline diff review — each proposed change appears highlighted in the exact line it modifies — is genuinely faster than reading a patch in the terminal for single-file or small-cluster edits.
  • Zero context-setup cost: the tool already knows which file you have open, what your cursor is on, and what the compiler is currently complaining about.
  • For developers new to agentic coding, the IDE surface reduces the learning curve because the interaction feels like pair programming rather than CLI operations.

Where terminal tools compound over time

  • Scriptable: drive from shell scripts, Makefiles, GitHub Actions, or any orchestration layer without a GUI.
  • Compose with existing Unix tooling — pipe output to grep, chain with &&, background with &.
  • Run headlessly in CI pipelines for automated code review, security scanning, or documentation generation on every pull request.
  • Give the user more explicit control over context, which matters for large codebases where automated context injection can bloat token costs.

The practical heuristic

  • Reach for the IDE tool when you are actively editing and want to stay in flow.
  • Reach for the terminal tool when you want to automate, batch, run in CI, or operate across multiple repositories at once.

IDE-resident strengths

Visual diffs: Proposed changes appear inline in the exact line they modify — faster to review than reading a patch in the terminal, especially for single-file or small-cluster edits.

Inline edits: Accept or reject each change without leaving the editor; the feedback loop runs at keypress speed rather than CLI round-trip speed.

Tab context: The tool already knows which file is open, what the cursor is on, and what the compiler is currently flagging — zero setup cost for the most common in-flow tasks.

Best for: Active editing sessions, visual diff review, staying in developer flow, single-project work where context injection is automatic.

Terminal-resident strengths

Scriptable: Drive agents from shell scripts, Makefiles, GitHub Actions, or any orchestration layer — no GUI required, fully composable with Unix tooling.

Headless & CI: Run in CI pipelines for automated code review, security scanning, or doc generation on every pull request without a human in the loop.

Multi-session: Invoke multiple agents across repos simultaneously, background them with &, pipe output to grep, or chain with && — patterns impossible in an IDE panel.

Best for: Automation, batch tasks, cross-repo operations, CI pipelines, and any workflow that needs to run reproducibly without a human present.

5.7 Claude Code vs Gemini CLI — the terminal-vs-terminal choice

Both tools are terminal-resident agentic coding tools with significant capability overlap: both can read your codebase, write code, run shell commands, and execute multi-step tasks. The practical differences come down to model family, context window, billing model, and ecosystem integration.

Claude Code

  • Backed by Anthropic's Claude model family (Opus, Sonnet, Haiku). Strong reputation for following nuanced instructions precisely and producing clean, idiomatic code.
  • Integrates tightly with Anthropic's MCP ecosystem and exposes a powerful hooks system.
  • Billing is not free: requires either an Anthropic Pro or Max subscription, or an API key billed per token. There is no genuine free tier for production use.

Gemini CLI

  • Backed by Google's Gemini model family. Offers a 1 million token context window — significantly larger than Claude's current limits — a material advantage for ingesting entire large codebases at once.
  • Billing asymmetry is real and significant: genuine free tier via personal Google account, with approximately 1,000 requests per day at no charge (verify current limits at the official repo and AI Studio).
  • For learners, side projects, and low-volume automation: lower-friction entry point. For teams with existing Anthropic contracts or strong Claude-model preferences: Claude Code will often be the natural choice.

5.8 Working in multiple tools at once

Kilo Code + terminal agent (most common pairing)

  • Kilo Code for active editing + Claude Code (or Gemini CLI) for review and security scanning.
  • Workflow: Kilo Code makes a batch of changes in the IDE, you commit or stash them, then invoke Claude Code from the terminal: "Review the changes I just made for security issues, off-by-one errors, and missing error handling."
  • The terminal agent reads the diff independently — operating from a fresh, unanchored perspective rather than the same context frame that generated the code — and surfaces concerns the in-IDE tool missed.

Claude Code + Gemini CLI (model diversity as quality gate)

  • Claude Code for implementation + Gemini CLI for a second opinion on architecture.
  • Both are terminal-resident — run them sequentially in the same shell session, feeding the output of one as context to the other.
  • Using model diversity as a quality gate rather than relying on a single model's blind spots is one of the highest-leverage habits an agentic engineer can develop.

The write-conflict caveat

  • Establish a clear ownership rule: one tool writes, others read-only review.
  • Never let two agents write to the same files concurrently without a coordination layer.

5.9 The wider field — IDE-resident alternatives

Kilo Code is this guide's primary IDE-resident tool, but the IDE-resident category is crowded and rapidly evolving. The six tools below are the most-referenced alternatives at the time of writing. Each has a genuine user community and a distinct value proposition. This guide does not deep-dive them — the intent here is to give you enough orientation to evaluate them against the primary tools and your own workflow.

Cursor

What it is: A VS Code fork with AI features baked into the editor at a deep level — not an extension, but a purpose-built IDE. Offers tab-completion, inline edits, an Agent mode that runs multi-step tasks, and its own codebase indexing layer.

Who it's for: Developers who want the tightest possible AI–editor integration and are willing to use a distinct application rather than an extension inside an existing VS Code install.

Key strength: Deep editor-level integration means AI suggestions appear faster and with richer context than most extension-based tools. The Composer/Agent mode can make sweeping multi-file changes with a single prompt.

Why not deep here: Cursor is a commercial product with its own subscription pricing and model routing. It diverges from upstream VS Code and extension compatibility can vary. This guide focuses on Kilo Code for its open-source, BYO-key model-agnostic approach.

→ docs at cursor.com/docs

Windsurf

What it is: An AI-native IDE from Codeium, built as a VS Code fork with a proprietary "Cascade" agentic engine that emphasises deep codebase understanding and multi-step autonomous edits.

Who it's for: Teams and individuals who want a polished, out-of-box AI IDE experience with Codeium's model hosting rather than BYO API keys.

Key strength: Cascade's context engine indexes the full codebase and maintains a persistent understanding of code relationships, reducing the need to manually inject context for large refactors.

Why not deep here: Like Cursor, it is a distinct fork rather than an extension, and its Cascade model is proprietary. This guide favours open, model-agnostic tooling for maximum flexibility.

→ docs at codeium.com/windsurf

Antigravity

What it is: A newer entrant in the AI IDE space positioning itself around autonomous, long-horizon coding tasks with a focus on multi-agent orchestration within the editor environment.

Who it's for: Engineers interested in multi-agent workflows directly inside the IDE, particularly for complex features that benefit from decomposition into parallel sub-tasks.

Key strength: Early emphasis on multi-agent coordination distinguishes it from single-agent IDE tools; designed from the ground up for agentic rather than assistive use.

Why not deep here: Still establishing its production track record at the time of writing. Covered here for awareness; verify current state at their official documentation.

→ docs at antigravity.dev

Cline / Roo Code

What it is: Cline (formerly Claude Dev) is the upstream open-source VS Code extension from which Kilo Code was forked. Roo Code is a community fork of Cline with additional features. Both remain active and maintain similar capabilities to Kilo Code.

Who it's for: Developers who prefer the upstream Cline codebase, or the Roo Code variant's specific additions, over Kilo Code's fork. Feature sets are closely related.

Key strength: Broad model support, active community, and (for Cline) the reference implementation that downstream forks track.

Why not deep here: Kilo Code was chosen as this guide's primary because of its active maintenance and feature differentiation; the core concepts transfer directly to Cline and Roo Code.

→ docs at github.com/cline/cline and roocode.com

Continue.dev

What it is: An open-source VS Code and JetBrains extension that acts as an AI coding assistant with a strong emphasis on configurability — you define your own context providers, slash commands, and model routing in a local config file.

Who it's for: Developers and teams who want fine-grained control over what context is sent to the model, and who want a tool that integrates with internal documentation and custom data sources.

Key strength: Highly customisable context pipeline. Supports local models via Ollama, making it the strongest choice for air-gapped or data-sensitive environments.

Why not deep here: Its strengths are in context configuration rather than autonomous agentic tasks. This guide focuses on the agentic end of the spectrum.

→ docs at docs.continue.dev

JetBrains AI Assistant / Junie

What it is: JetBrains AI Assistant is the built-in AI layer across the JetBrains IDE family (IntelliJ, PyCharm, GoLand, etc.). Junie is JetBrains' agentic coding agent, available as part of the AI Assistant subscription.

Who it's for: Developers already working in JetBrains IDEs who want AI assistance without switching editors. Particularly relevant for Java/Kotlin, Python, and Go shops standardised on JetBrains tooling.

Key strength: Deep JetBrains IDE integration — understands project structure, run configurations, debugger state, and the JetBrains refactoring engine in ways that VS Code extensions cannot match for JB-centric workflows.

Why not deep here: This guide is VS Code-centric. JetBrains tooling is the correct choice for JetBrains users; the concepts transfer.

→ docs at jetbrains.com/ai

5.10 The wider field — terminal-resident alternatives

Claude Code and Gemini CLI are the two primary terminal-resident agents in this guide, but the terminal ecosystem has other well-established tools worth knowing. The three below have meaningful user bases and distinct philosophies.

Aider

What it is: One of the earliest and most mature open-source terminal-resident AI coding tools. Aider integrates tightly with git — it reads your repo, proposes changes as diffs, and commits them automatically. Supports a wide range of models via LiteLLM.

Who it's for: Developers who want a stable, battle-tested, open-source terminal agent with strong git integration and broad model support. Popular in the self-hosted and open-source model community.

Key strength: Git-native workflow: changes are proposed as clean diffs and committed with sensible messages, making the agent's history first-class in your repo history.

Why not deep here: Aider's interaction model is more diff-centric than conversational. Claude Code and Gemini CLI offer broader agentic capabilities (shell execution, web search, MCP integration) that this guide emphasises.

→ docs at aider.chat

GitHub Copilot CLI / Spark / Workspace

What it is: GitHub's family of AI developer tools. Copilot CLI brings AI to the terminal for shell command generation and explanation. Copilot Workspace is a cloud-based agentic environment for issue-to-PR workflows. Spark is GitHub's natural-language app-building experiment.

Who it's for: Teams already on GitHub with Copilot Business or Enterprise subscriptions; Copilot CLI specifically suits developers who want quick shell command assistance without leaving the terminal.

Key strength: Deep GitHub integration — Copilot Workspace can read an issue, plan a fix, and open a draft PR with changes, all within the GitHub UI. The tightest AI integration for GitHub-native workflows.

Why not deep here: The GitHub Copilot family is a commercial product tightly coupled to GitHub. This guide focuses on tools that work locally and independently of a specific platform host.

→ docs at docs.github.com/copilot

Codex CLI

What it is: OpenAI's open-source terminal agent, backed by the GPT-5 model family. Codex CLI runs in a sandboxed environment with configurable approval modes — from full auto to manual approval for every action. Written in TypeScript and available via npm.

Who it's for: Developers in the OpenAI ecosystem, or anyone who wants an open-source terminal agent with sandbox-first safety controls and GPT-5 model access.

Key strength: Strong safety story: the sandboxed execution model and granular approval modes make it well-suited for environments where you want tight control over what the agent can do.

Why not deep here: This guide is structured around the Anthropic and Google ecosystems. Codex CLI's concepts map directly — the mental models transfer once you have hands-on experience with Claude Code or Gemini CLI.

→ docs at github.com/openai/codex

5.11 Choosing the surface for the task — a decision matrix

No single tool wins every category. The table below maps common task types to the three primary tools, using a three-value scale: Best (optimal choice for this task type), Good (works well, minor tradeoffs), and Avoid (possible but not the right fit). Use it as a starting point — your own workflow, model preferences, and existing subscriptions will shift the cells.

Task type Kilo Code (IDE) Claude Code (terminal) Gemini CLI (terminal)
Quick single-file edit Best — inline diff, instant context Good — works, more ceremony Good — works, more ceremony
Multi-file refactor Good — can do it, diff review in IDE Best — autonomous multi-step, shell access Best — large context window helps here
Security review Good — Ask mode without writes Best — precise instruction-following, read-only mode Good — capable; smaller model nuance than Claude
Exploratory build (greenfield) Good — Architect mode useful Best — conversational planning + execution Good — strong reasoning, free tier is low-friction
CI / headless automation Avoid — requires GUI Best — designed for headless use Best — CLI-first, scriptable
Doc writing / README generation Good — Ask or Code mode Good — strong prose, prompt once and iterate Best — 1M context; ingest full repo for accurate docs
Zero-budget / free-tier use Good — BYO free-tier API key Avoid — no free tier without API key Best — genuine free tier via personal Google account

5.12 Hands-on: same task in two of three, compare

The goal of this exercise is to develop felt intuition about tool differences — not just read about them. Pick one of the following tasks and run it in two of the three primary tools. Record what you observe: how long it took, what context was required, how the output differed, and what the interaction felt like.

Suggested task A — IDE vs terminal

  • Open a small project (your own, or clone a simple open-source repo). In Kilo Code, use Code mode: "Add input validation to every public function that currently has none." Accept or reject the diffs as you review them.
  • Then, without resetting the code, run Claude Code or Gemini CLI in the same directory: "Review the recent changes for missing edge cases or error conditions."
  • Note how the terminal agent sees the same code, what it surfaces, and how the feedback loop feels different.

Suggested task B — terminal vs terminal

  • In any project directory, run Claude Code: "List the top five architectural concerns in this codebase with a short rationale for each." Then run Gemini CLI with the identical prompt.
  • Compare side by side: note differences in style, depth, what each missed, and how the 1M context window affects Gemini's ability to reference specific file locations.
  • There is no single correct answer — the comparison itself is the learning.

After both runs

  • Write a two-sentence note to yourself about which tool you would reach for first for this class of task and why.
  • That note, accumulated over several exercises, becomes your personal decision matrix — more accurate than any generic table.

6. Spec-Driven Development: OpenSpec & SpecKit

What you'll learn: why specs matter when agents code at speed, two production approaches (OpenSpec and SpecKit) to driving features through specs, how they differ, and how to apply either to a real CLI tool.

6.1 Why specs matter when agents code fast

Drift is cheap; drift recovery is expensive

  • Agentic coding tools can generate hundreds of lines of working code in seconds — their greatest asset and the source of a subtle, compounding risk.
  • When a human writes code slowly, each line is a moment of conscious alignment with intent. When an agent writes code fast, that alignment check collapses.
  • The agent will produce code that compiles, passes tests, and looks plausible — but may be solving a slightly different problem than the one you actually have.
  • The first sign of drift is often not a failing test but a code review comment like "this isn't quite what I asked for," or worse, a production bug that traces back to a misunderstood requirement.

Why code review is too late

  • Code review catches drift after implementation. At agentic speed, a single session can produce changes across dozens of files before anyone looks at a diff.
  • By the time drift is detected, the agent has built more on top of it — integration points, tests, documentation. The cost of unwinding grows non-linearly.

The right countermeasure: spec first

  • A spec written before the agent writes the first line of code externalises intent into a form the agent can read, reason over, and be held to.
  • It becomes the ground truth that resolves ambiguity — not memory, not conversation history, not hope.
  • When the spec is versioned alongside the code, drift becomes visible as a diff between the spec and the implementation, not as a mysterious behavior in production.

Specs also cure context degradation

  • Long agent sessions lose fidelity. The spec file, re-injected at the start of each session, restores full context instantly.
  • It is the cheapest possible form of persistent memory.
  • The time investment in writing a spec is almost always recovered in the first session it prevents from going sideways.
  • At scale — multiple engineers, multiple agents, multiple concurrent features — specs are not overhead; they are the coordination layer that makes agentic development coherent.

6.2 The contract — spec → behavior → tests

What a spec actually is

  • A spec is not documentation written after the fact — it is a contract: a formal statement of what a feature must do, written before implementation begins.
  • The contract has three parties: the spec that describes behavior, the implementation that realises it, and the tests that enforce it.
  • When all three are in sync, you have working software. When any two diverge, you have a bug, a doc mistake, or a test gap — all recoverable.
  • When all three diverge independently, you have an undocumented feature that no-one can safely change.
📄
SPEC
describes behavior
→
🤖
AGENT
reads spec, writes impl
→
✅
TESTS
enforce the contract
Spec diverges from impl? → tests catch it. Impl diverges from tests? → CI catches it. Tests diverge from spec? → review catches it.

Roles in the triangle

  • The agent's role is mechanical: read the spec as primary context, generate implementation to satisfy it, then run or write tests that prove the contract holds.
  • The human's role is to write and review the spec — the one artifact that requires genuine domain understanding and product judgment. Everything else can be delegated.
  • The spec is the only artifact an agent cannot write for you. An agent can refine a draft spec, suggest edge cases, and flag ambiguities — but the initial "this is what it must do" judgment is irreducibly human.
  • Investing time here is not overhead; it is the work.

6.3 OpenSpec model — workspace and proposals

OpenSpec is an open-source specification management system designed for codebases where specs live alongside code in git.

Core idea

  • Specs are first-class files, not wiki pages or tickets — they live in a versioned workspace, are updated through a formal change process, and are archived (not deleted) when superseded.
  • Note: The directory layout below reflects the OpenSpec model at the time of writing. Always verify the current canonical structure against the official repository at github.com/Fission-AI/OpenSpec before adopting it — directory names and file conventions have evolved across versions.
openspec/
├── specs/                        # canonical (accepted) specs live here
│   └── gitlog-report.md          # example: a feature spec
│
└── changes/                      # in-flight proposals
    ├── add-gitlog-report/        # one subdirectory per change id
    │   ├── proposal.md           # change rationale + summary
    │   └── specs/                # delta specs for this change
    │       └── gitlog-report.md  # the proposed new or modified spec
    │
    └── archive/                  # accepted/rejected changes moved here
        └── add-gitlog-report/    # original structure preserved

Change lifecycle: three states

  • Proposed — in openspec/changes/<id>/.
  • Accepted — spec promoted to openspec/specs/; change directory moved to openspec/changes/archive/.
  • Rejected — moved to archive without promotion.
  • The archive discipline is intentional: rejected or superseded proposals are preserved for audit and context, not deleted.

Canonical specs vs. changes directory

  • The canonical specs in openspec/specs/ are the authoritative source of truth. Agents are expected to read from this directory before implementing any feature.
  • The changes directory is ephemeral working space — it holds proposals under review and disappears from the active tree on acceptance.

CLI operations (lifecycle only)

  • openspec list — show active changes.
  • openspec show <id> — display a change's proposal and delta specs.
  • openspec validate — lint spec structure.
  • openspec archive <id> — move an accepted change to the archive.
  • Content — writing the spec, reasoning about it — is handled by the AI slash commands, not the CLI.

6.4 OpenSpec workflow walk-through

This walk-through uses gitlog-report — a CLI tool that reads git log and produces a per-author contribution summary — as the running example. Every step is reproducible in any project that has OpenSpec installed.

Note on slash commands

  • OpenSpec's AI integration uses slash commands (e.g. in Claude Code or compatible shells) to drive change creation.
  • The exact command name may vary with OpenSpec version — always check the current README at github.com/Fission-AI/OpenSpec.
  • At the time of writing, the command pattern is /opsx:propose <change-id>.
# Step 1 — create a new change proposal via AI slash command
/opsx:propose add-gitlog-report

# OpenSpec creates the scaffold:
# openspec/changes/add-gitlog-report/
# ├── proposal.md          ← AI fills in rationale/summary
# └── specs/
#     └── gitlog-report.md ← you write (or AI drafts) the spec

The generated proposal.md looks like:

# Change: add-gitlog-report

**Type:** new-feature
**Status:** proposed

## Rationale
Introduce a `gitlog-report` CLI command that aggregates git log output
into a per-author contribution summary, supporting JSON and Markdown output.

## Summary of changes
- Adds `openspec/specs/gitlog-report.md` describing the new command's
  interface, flags, output schema, and edge-case behavior.

## Linked spec delta
`openspec/changes/add-gitlog-report/specs/gitlog-report.md`

You then write or review the delta spec in openspec/changes/add-gitlog-report/specs/gitlog-report.md. Once the spec is approved (by review, PR merge, or team sign-off), accept the change.

# Step 2 — validate the spec structure
openspec validate

# Step 3 — list active changes
openspec list
# → add-gitlog-report  proposed  (1 spec delta)

# Step 4 — after approval, archive the change and promote the spec
openspec archive add-gitlog-report
# Promotes:  openspec/changes/add-gitlog-report/specs/gitlog-report.md
#         → openspec/specs/gitlog-report.md
# Archives:  openspec/changes/add-gitlog-report/
#         → openspec/changes/archive/add-gitlog-report/

After acceptance

  • The agent can now be invoked with: "Implement gitlog-report according to openspec/specs/gitlog-report.md."
  • The spec is the contract; the canonical location is stable; the agent has no ambiguity about what to build.

6.5 SpecKit model — slash commands and artifacts

SpecKit takes a different philosophy: rather than a file-system workspace with a CLI lifecycle, it provides a set of AI slash commands that each write a specific artifact into a feature-scoped directory. The workflow is slash-command-driven rather than file-system-driven, and it is designed to work natively inside Claude Code or compatible AI environments.

Note on naming

  • SpecKit's directory structure and exact slash command names may differ from what is shown below.
  • Always verify the current artifact layout and command names against the official SpecKit documentation before adopting the pattern in a new project.

The four primary slash commands and their outputs:

CommandWritesPurpose
/specify <feature>spec.mdGenerate the feature specification — acceptance criteria, interface contract, edge cases
/plan <feature>plan.mdGenerate a phased implementation plan from the spec
/tasks <feature>tasks.mdBreak the plan into atomic, checkable task list
/implement <feature>source filesExecute the tasks, writing code against spec + plan

Artifact paths and self-documenting chain

  • Artifacts are written into a feature-scoped directory — the exact path pattern depends on the SpecKit version; common conventions include .specify/specs/<feature>/ or specs/<NNN-feature>/.
  • The key invariant: each feature gets its own isolated directory containing all four artifacts in sequence.
  • Each command is idempotent — you can re-run /specify to refine the spec without losing the plan, because each artifact is a separate file.
  • Each command reads its predecessors — /plan reads spec.md, /tasks reads both, /implement reads all three. The chain is self-documenting.

6.6 SpecKit workflow walk-through

The same gitlog-report example, now in SpecKit terms. Starting in a project directory with SpecKit configured.

# Step 1 — specify the feature
/specify gitlog-report

# Writes: specs/001-gitlog-report/spec.md
# (or .specify/specs/gitlog-report/spec.md — verify with your SpecKit version)

The generated spec.md contains:

# Spec: gitlog-report

## Feature
A CLI command that reads `git log` for a repository and produces a
per-author contribution summary.

## Acceptance criteria
- Reads git log from the current directory (or --path flag)
- Produces per-author: commit count, days active, lines added/removed
- Outputs JSON (default) and Markdown (--format md)
- Supports --since / --until date range filters
- Handles empty repository gracefully (zero-row output, exit 0)
- Top-5 most-touched files listed per author
- Runs in under 5 seconds on a 10,000-commit repo

## Interface
`gitlog-report [--path <dir>] [--format json|md] [--since <ISO-date>] [--until <ISO-date>] [--include-merges]`

## Edge cases
- Merge commits: counted as commits, lines excluded by default (opt-in via `--include-merges`)
- Unborn HEAD (no commits): returns empty result set
- Author email deduplication: case-insensitive
      
# Step 2 — generate implementation plan from spec
/plan gitlog-report
# Writes: specs/001-gitlog-report/plan.md
# (phases: git log parsing, data aggregation, output formatting, CLI wiring)

# Step 3 — break plan into tasks
/tasks gitlog-report
# Writes: specs/001-gitlog-report/tasks.md
# (atomic checklist: [ ] parse git log output, [ ] aggregate by author, ...)

# Step 4 — implement
/implement gitlog-report
# Reads all three artifacts, writes source files
# Agent works task by task, checks each off as it completes

Artifacts as ground truth

  • At every stage the artifact file is the ground truth.
  • If the implementation diverges from spec.md, re-running /implement corrects it.
  • If the spec changes, re-run /plan and /tasks to regenerate downstream artifacts before re-implementing.

6.7 Side-by-side comparison

DimensionOpenSpecSpecKit
Core philosophy Specs as first-class files with a formal change lifecycle Slash-command-driven artifact chain, each command produces one file
File structure openspec/specs/ (canonical) + openspec/changes/<id>/ (proposals) specs/<NNN-feature>/ containing spec.md, plan.md, tasks.md
Change tracking Explicit proposal + archive lifecycle; rejected proposals preserved Git history on artifact files; no formal proposal/archive concept
Primary interaction CLI (openspec list/show/validate/archive) + AI slash commands for authoring AI slash commands exclusively (/specify /plan /tasks /implement)
Spec granularity One spec file per feature (canonical); delta specs per change One spec.md per feature; plan and tasks are derived artifacts
Archive model Formal archive directory; changes archived on acceptance/rejection No formal archive; old specs remain in place, superseded by new ones
Multi-tool support Tool-agnostic — any AI tool can read the spec files Designed for Claude Code and Gemini CLI; slash commands may not work elsewhere
Onboarding friction Higher — requires workspace setup, CLI install, change conventions Lower — slash commands work immediately in a compatible AI environment
Auditability High — every change has a proposal with rationale; archive is permanent Moderate — artifact files are versioned by git, but no formal rationale file
Best fit Teams with multiple contributors, long-lived projects, compliance needs Solo developers or small teams moving fast with AI-first tooling

OpenSpec

Git-native. Specs live as versioned files under openspec/specs/; the git history is the audit trail.

Manual authoring. You write and edit spec files directly; CLI commands (openspec validate, openspec archive) enforce structure.

Tool-agnostic. Any AI tool — Claude Code, Cursor, Gemini CLI — can read the spec files. No slash-command dependency.

Change proposals. Each spec change goes through a formal proposal + archive lifecycle, preserving the rationale for every decision.

SpecKit

Slash-command-driven. /specify → /plan → /tasks → /implement walks the full chain; each command produces one artifact file.

Guided pipeline. The skill scaffolds spec, plan, and task files automatically — lower setup friction than OpenSpec.

Claude / Gemini-first. Designed for environments with slash-command support; commands may not work in other AI tools.

Approval-gated. Each stage waits for human sign-off before proceeding, keeping you in control at every transition.

6.8 Decision matrix — when OpenSpec fits, when SpecKit fits

Neither system is universally superior. The right choice depends on team size, workflow maturity, tooling preferences, and how much process overhead is acceptable for the project's scale.

SignalReach for OpenSpecReach for SpecKit
Team size 3+ engineers; multiple concurrent features 1–2 engineers; fast-moving solo or pair project
Change discipline You want a formal record of why each spec changed, not just what changed Git history on spec.md is sufficient audit trail
Tooling Mixed AI tools in use — some team members on Cursor, some on Claude Code Team is uniformly on Claude Code or Gemini CLI with slash-command support
Integration with CI You want openspec validate to run in CI as a lint step CI integration is not a current requirement
Lifecycle clarity You need a clear "this spec is the accepted version" moment in the process Spec files are edited in-place; latest version is always current
Onboarding speed You can invest in workspace setup upfront for long-term benefit You need to start speccing immediately with no setup cost

Decision heuristics

  • If your project already has a PR review process and you want specs to go through the same gates as code, OpenSpec's change-and-archive model maps naturally onto that workflow.
  • If your project is a personal tool or a prototype where the bottleneck is speed of iteration rather than coordination, SpecKit's slash-command chain gives you the discipline of specs without the ceremony.
  • The two systems are not mutually exclusive — some teams adopt SpecKit for rapid early-stage work and migrate to OpenSpec as the project matures and more contributors join.

6.9 Living with specs — drift, refactoring, archiving

Specs are not write-once artifacts. Code evolves, requirements change, and the spec accurate in month one will drift from the implementation by month three — unless you actively maintain it. The question is not whether drift will happen, but how you detect and correct it.

Detecting drift

  • Include a "spec review" step in your regular code review process: when a PR modifies a feature, check whether the corresponding spec file was also updated. If the implementation changed but the spec did not, flag it.
  • Some teams run a periodic "spec audit" session — every quarter, read through the canonical specs and run a quick prompt: "Does the current implementation of <feature> match this spec? Flag discrepancies." The agent will surface them in minutes.

Refactoring specs

  • When a feature genuinely changes scope or behavior, update the spec first, then update the implementation — the spec-driven equivalent of test-first development.
  • The spec change is the declaration of intent; the implementation change is the execution.
  • In OpenSpec: create a new change proposal for the spec update before touching the code.
  • In SpecKit: re-run /specify with updated requirements before re-running /plan and /implement.

Archiving specs

  • When a feature is deprecated or removed, do not delete its spec — archive it.
  • In OpenSpec: handled by the archive lifecycle. In SpecKit: move the spec directory to an archived/ subdirectory.
  • Deleted specs disappear from the record; archived specs preserve the history of decisions. Future engineers — or future agents — will find context in archived specs that they cannot find anywhere else.

Spec granularity creep

  • Over time, specs accumulate detail — a 2-page spec becomes a 10-page spec as edge cases are documented, exceptions catalogued, and historical context appended. This is mostly healthy.
  • Watch for the point where the spec becomes harder to parse than the code. If an agent takes 60 seconds of context-loading to read the spec before it can start work, the spec has grown too large.
  • Consider splitting into a core spec (the contract) and a separate decisions log (the history).

6.10 Anti-patterns — speccing what doesn't need a spec

Spec-driven development has real overhead. A spec file needs to be written, reviewed, maintained, and eventually archived. For the right features, this overhead is paid back many times over. For the wrong features, it is pure waste.

Anti-pattern 1: Speccing trivial CRUD

  • If the feature is "add a /users/:id GET endpoint that returns the user by ID," the spec is already fully described by the sentence you just wrote plus the existing data model.
  • Writing a formal spec file adds no information. The agent can implement this from a one-line prompt.
  • Reserve specs for features with non-trivial business logic, multiple output formats, or behavior that cannot be inferred from naming conventions.

Anti-pattern 2: Spec effort exceeds implementation effort

  • If writing and reviewing the spec takes longer than the implementation would have taken without one, the spec is not providing value at this scale.
  • This most often happens with one-off scripts, migration scripts, or throwaway tooling. If the tool will be used once and discarded, a spec is overhead with no return. A rough prompt is sufficient.

Anti-pattern 3: Specs that drift untouched

  • A spec that no-one updates is worse than no spec — it actively misleads.
  • Agents that read a stale spec will implement against outdated requirements with full confidence, because they have no way to know the spec is stale.
  • If your team cannot commit to maintaining specs, do not introduce them. An honest "no spec, use judgment" is safer than a lying spec file.

Anti-pattern 4: Over-speccing exploratory work

  • In the early stages of a feature — when you are not yet sure what the right interface is — writing a detailed spec freezes premature decisions.
  • In this phase, use a lightweight "spike spec" (a few bullet points of hypotheses) rather than a full behavioral contract.
  • Graduate to a full spec once the shape of the feature is clear.

Anti-pattern 5: Spec by committee

  • A spec that needs sign-off from six people before any code is written reproduces the worst of traditional requirements processes without the benefits of agility.
  • Keep spec review tight: one author, one reviewer, merge.
  • Larger specs can go through wider review — but the default should be lightweight, not heavyweight.

6.11 Mini-walkthrough: gitlog-report spec

Mini-walkthrough · gitlog-report — write the spec

The tool: gitlog-report is a CLI command that reads git log for a repository and produces a per-author contribution summary. It is a real, buildable tool — simple enough to implement in an afternoon, complex enough to benefit from a spec. We will use it as the running example across Ch 6–11, then build it end-to-end in Ch 17.

Acceptance criteria:

  • AC1 — Reads git log from the current directory, or from a path specified via --path <dir>
  • AC2 — Produces per-author: commit count, days active, commits per day, lines added, lines removed
  • AC3 — Lists the top 5 most-touched files per author (by total line change delta)
  • AC4 — Outputs JSON by default; Markdown report via --format md
  • AC5 — Supports --since <date> and --until <date> date range filters (ISO 8601 format)
  • AC6 — Handles an empty repository (no commits) gracefully: zero-row output, exit code 0
  • AC7 — Handles merge commits consistently: counted in commit total, lines excluded by default (opt-in via --include-merges)

OpenSpec proposal snippet — what the file at openspec/changes/add-gitlog-report/proposal.md looks like after /opsx:propose add-gitlog-report:

# Change: add-gitlog-report

**Type:** new-feature
**Status:** proposed

## Rationale
Teams want a quick, scriptable way to generate contribution summaries
from git history — for retrospectives, billing reports, and onboarding
context. `gitlog-report` fills this gap with a single command.

## Summary of changes
Adds `openspec/specs/gitlog-report.md` defining the CLI interface,
output schema (JSON + Markdown), date filtering, merge-commit handling,
and empty-repo edge case.

## Linked spec delta
`openspec/changes/add-gitlog-report/specs/gitlog-report.md`

SpecKit /specify output snippet — what specs/001-gitlog-report/spec.md looks like after /specify gitlog-report:

# Spec: gitlog-report

## Feature
CLI command producing per-author git contribution summaries.

## Interface
gitlog-report [--path <dir>] [--format json|md]
              [--since <ISO-date>] [--until <ISO-date>]
              [--include-merges]

## Acceptance criteria
- [ ] Reads git log from CWD or --path
- [ ] Per-author: commits, days-active, commits/day, lines+/-
- [ ] Top-5 most-touched files per author
- [ ] JSON output (default), Markdown (--format md)
- [ ] --since / --until date filtering
- [ ] Empty repo → zero-row output, exit 0
- [ ] Merge commits: excluded from line counts by default

## Edge cases
- Author email deduplication: case-insensitive
- Unborn HEAD (no commits): empty result, no error
- Binary files: excluded from line-change counts

Both formats capture the same contract — the difference is workflow. OpenSpec routes the spec through a formal change proposal before it becomes canonical. SpecKit writes spec, plan, tasks, and implementation in one slash-command chain.

Next chapter: we take this spec and plan the implementation — breaking it into phases and tasks an agent can execute step by step. → Ch 7: Plan-Driven Execution.

→ Ch 7. Plan-Driven Execution → Ch 8. Test-Driven Development with Agents → Ch 17. Worked Example: gitlog-report end-to-end

7. Plan-Driven Execution

What you'll learn: what a plan is (and how it differs from a spec), how to write one your agent can execute, when to review vs replan, and how to apply the rhythm to gitlog-report.

7.1 What "plan" means here — different from spec

In everyday speech, "spec" and "plan" are near-synonyms. In agentic development they are completely distinct artifacts with different audiences, different lifecycles, and different purposes — confusing them is one of the most reliable ways to produce a chaotic codebase.

Spec vs. plan — the key distinction

  • A spec answers the question what — a durable contract of acceptance criteria, interface definitions, and edge cases. Lives in version control alongside the code, changes rarely (only when requirements change), written for humans and agents to reference indefinitely. The source of truth about intent.
  • A plan answers the question how — a transient execution guide of ordered tasks, files to touch, code steps, and commit points. Lives for the duration of one session (or one sprint). Once the feature is merged, the plan is archived or deleted. The source of truth about sequence.
  • Practical consequence: never modify the spec to satisfy a plan. If your plan conflicts with the spec, the plan is wrong — replan.
  • Never edit the spec mid-session unless the requirements themselves have changed — a rare, deliberate decision requiring stakeholder sign-off.
Dimension Spec Plan
Question answered What to build How to build it
Lifecycle Durable — survives the session Transient — one session or sprint
Audience Humans + agents (forever) Agent executing this run
Changes when Requirements change Approach changes (freely)
If it conflicts with the other Spec wins — plan must adapt —

7.2 Brainstorm → spec → plan → execute — the four-step rhythm

Structured agentic development follows a four-step rhythm. Each step produces an artifact that the next step consumes. Skipping a step is possible — sometimes correct — but skip intentionally, not accidentally.

💡
BRAINSTORM
Explore the problem space.
What are we actually solving?
Skill: superpowers:brainstorming
→
📄
SPEC
What to build.
Acceptance criteria. Interface.
Ch 6 · OpenSpec · SpecKit
→
🗺️
PLAN
How to build it.
Ordered tasks. Files. Commits.
superpowers:writing-plans
→
⚡
EXECUTE
Agent follows plan.
Commits at each task boundary.
superpowers:executing-plans

The four steps

  • Brainstorm — open-ended exploration with the agent. Not writing code — pressure-testing assumptions, surfacing unknowns, reaching clarity about what you actually want. Invest 5–15 minutes here; it repays itself in avoided spec rewrites. Use the superpowers:brainstorming skill.
  • Spec — formalise the output of brainstorm into a durable contract (Ch 6 territory). The spec is written or reviewed before a single line of implementation is touched. May be a Markdown file checked into the repo, an OpenSpec proposal, or a SpecKit /specify output.
  • Plan — take the spec and decompose it into a numbered sequence of tasks the agent can execute one by one (the subject of this chapter). Use the superpowers:writing-plans skill or Claude plan mode.
  • Execute — the agent follows the plan task by task, committing after each one. Review diffs at commit boundaries. If the plan becomes wrong mid-execution, replan (see 7.7). Use the superpowers:executing-plans skill.

7.3 Anatomy of a good plan file

A plan file is a Markdown document (typically PLAN.md or specs/<feature>/plan.md) with a predictable structure. The superpowers:writing-plans skill produces this format automatically; the checklist below tells you what to look for when reviewing a plan the agent drafted.

Five structural properties of good plans

  1. Numbered tasks — each task is a discrete unit of work with a single, verifiable outcome. If a task can't be described in one sentence, split it.
  2. Files-touched per task — every task names the files it will create, modify, or delete. This prevents surprise scope creep and makes review tractable.
  3. Code or command blocks within each task — the agent should not need to invent the approach mid-execution. Key commands, function signatures, or scaffolding snippets live inside the task so the agent stays on the planned path.
  4. Commit cadence — each task (or logical group of tasks) ends with a git commit. Atomic commits mean atomic rollback. Never let the agent execute more than one logical change before committing.
  5. Verification step — every task ends with a check: run the tests, grep for the expected output, or confirm the file exists. The agent should not mark a task complete without running its own verification.
# PLAN: gitlog-report

## Task 1 — Scaffold project structure
**Files:** pyproject.toml, src/gitlog_report/__init__.py, src/gitlog_report/cli.py
**Steps:**
1. Create `pyproject.toml` with entry-point `gitlog-report = "gitlog_report.cli:main"`
2. Create empty `__init__.py`
3. Create `cli.py` stub: `def main(): pass`
**Verify:** `pip install -e . && gitlog-report --help` exits 0
**Commit:** `feat(scaffold): initialise gitlog-report package`

## Task 2 — Argument parser
**Files:** src/gitlog_report/cli.py
**Steps:**
1. Add `argparse.ArgumentParser` with --path, --format, --since, --until, --include-merges
**Verify:** `gitlog-report --help` lists all five flags
**Commit:** `feat(cli): add argument parser`
...

Commit boundary discipline

  • Each task is bounded, verifiable, and ends with a commit — an agent following this plan cannot accidentally do too much in one step.
  • The commit boundary enforces a natural pause where you can review the diff.
  • The superpowers:writing-plans skill generates plan files in this format from a spec. Invoke it with: /writing-plans or trigger via the Skill tool.

7.4 Plan mode in Claude Code (vs Kilo Architect — important asymmetry)

Claude Code has a dedicated plan mode that lets the agent think through an implementation before touching any files. Understanding its constraints — and how it differs from Kilo Architect's planning model — is essential for choosing the right tool.

Entering plan mode in Claude Code

  • Cycle through modes with Shift+Tab until the mode indicator shows Plan
  • Or prefix your request with "Plan:" to signal plan-mode intent (the agent will enter plan mode before responding)
  • The mode persists for the session; the agent will not exit it until you approve

The approval gate

  • The agent must invoke the ExitPlanMode tool to leave plan mode. That tool requires your explicit confirmation.
  • The agent cannot silently slip from planning to execution — you always see the full plan and approve before a single file is touched.

Tool permissions in plan mode:

Tool Allowed in plan mode? Why
Read, Grep, Glob Allowed Read-only — needed to understand the codebase
WebFetch Allowed Read-only — needed to research APIs and docs
Edit, Write Blocked No file writes until plan is approved
Bash Blocked No shell execution — prevents side effects
MCP tools (file-modifying) Blocked All side-effecting tools suspended
ExitPlanMode Gated Requires your explicit approval to execute
Claude Code plan mode vs Kilo Architect — a critical asymmetry

These two tools both "plan" but they work very differently:

Aspect Claude Code plan mode Kilo Architect
File writes during planning Forbidden Allowed — Architect writes files
Plan output format Conversational response (in chat) Written directly to PLAN.md on disk
Role separation Same agent — mode switch Separate orchestrator (Architect) + executor (Code)
When to use Quick interactive planning in Claude Code Kilo-native projects; plan-as-artifact workflows

Practical implication: in Claude Code plan mode, the plan lives in the conversation. If you want the plan as a committed file — so it's reviewable and resumable — you must ask the agent to write it out after plan mode ends. In Kilo Architect, the plan is a file from the start. Choose accordingly, or use the superpowers:writing-plans skill (which always writes a file, regardless of which agent you're in).

7.5 Plan files as artifacts — committable, reviewable

A plan that exists only in conversation history has two problems: it disappears when the session ends, and it can't be reviewed by a teammate. Treating your plan as a committed artifact — a Markdown file in the repo — solves both.

Three benefits of committing your plan file

  1. Reviewability — a PR containing PLAN.md alongside the spec lets a reviewer verify that the planned approach actually satisfies the spec criteria before a single line of implementation is written. This is the cheapest code review possible.
  2. Resumability — if you close the session mid-execution, the next session starts with: "Read PLAN.md and continue from Task N." No context reconstruction. No guessing what was done.
  3. Auditability — the git log records when the plan changed and why. If a post-mortem asks "did we have a plan for this?", the answer is in the history.

File paths and commit convention

  • PLAN.md at repo root for small features; specs/<feature>/plan.md alongside the spec for larger work; .claude/PLAN.md for plans that are agent-internal and shouldn't appear in the main repo tree.
  • Commit message convention: plan(<feature>): draft execution plan — makes the plan easy to find in git log --oneline without grep.

7.6 Reviewing your agent's plan before it acts

Whether the plan comes from Claude plan mode, Kilo Architect, or the superpowers:writing-plans skill, review it before execution begins. A five-point check catches the most common failure modes.

  1. Right files? Every task names the files it will touch. Verify each file actually exists (for modifications) or makes sense to create at that path (for new files). Watch for files that are never mentioned — a plan that modifies cli.py but never touches the corresponding test file is almost certainly incomplete.
  2. Right order? Task N should not depend on something that Task N+2 creates. Read the dependency chain forward: if step 3 imports a module that step 5 creates, the plan will fail at step 3. The agent can get this wrong.
  3. Complete code in steps? Key function signatures, command invocations, and config snippets should appear in the task body — not be delegated to the agent's judgment at execution time. Vague instructions like "implement the parser" are plans waiting to go wrong. Specific instructions like "implement parse_git_log(path: str) -> list[Commit] in parser.py" are plans that execute predictably.
  4. No placeholders? Scan for TODO, ..., <fill in>, as appropriate. Placeholders in a plan become agent hallucinations during execution. Fill them in now or mark the task as requiring human input before the agent reaches it.
  5. Verification steps? Every task should have a concrete check: a test command to run, an output to grep for, a file to confirm exists. "Verify the feature works" is not a verification step. "Run pytest tests/test_parser.py -v and confirm all 4 tests pass" is.

Time investment

  • The five-point check takes two to five minutes. It reliably prevents sessions that go sideways at step 3 and require a full replan. Pay the two minutes.
1
Right files?

Every task names the exact files it will touch. Verify each exists (for modifications) or makes sense at that path (for new files). A plan that edits cli.py but never mentions its test file is incomplete.

2
Right order?

Read the dependency chain forward: Task N must not import or call something that only Task N+2 creates. Agents get ordering wrong more often than you'd expect.

3
Complete code in steps?

Key function signatures, command invocations, and config snippets must appear in the task body — not delegated to the agent's judgment at execution time. "Implement the parser" is vague; parse_git_log(path: str) → list[Commit] is actionable.

4
No placeholders?

Scan for TODO, ..., <fill in>, "as appropriate." Placeholders in a plan become agent hallucinations during execution. Fill them now or mark the task as requiring human input before the agent reaches it.

5
Verification steps?

Every task needs a concrete check: a test command to run, an output to grep for, a file to confirm exists. "Verify the feature works" is not a verification step. "Run pytest tests/test_parser.py -v and confirm all 4 tests pass" is.

7.7 Mid-plan replanning when reality changes

Even a well-reviewed plan will sometimes collide with reality — the codebase was in a different state than expected, a dependency has a breaking API change, or the chosen approach reveals a design flaw not visible from the spec. You have two options: amend or abandon.

Amend vs. abandon

  • Amend when the reality change is local: the current task and at most one successor need to change, but the overall structure and commit plan remain valid. Update the affected tasks, commit the updated plan (e.g., plan(gitlog-report): amend task 3 — use argparse subcommands), then continue. The agent re-reads the updated plan.
  • Abandon and replan when the collision is structural: the approach cannot satisfy the spec without touching more than two or three tasks, or the planned file structure is wrong. Stop, reset to the last clean commit, write a new plan, review it, then re-execute.
  • Sunk cost reasoning ("we're already 60% done") is the enemy. A bad 60% is more expensive to fix than a clean restart.

Signs you need a full replan

  • The agent has started improvising — adding files not in the plan, skipping tasks, or reordering steps without telling you.
  • The current task has grown past its planned scope and is pulling in two or three others.
  • The git diff is much larger than expected for the current task.
  • A test introduced in an earlier task is now failing because of the current task's changes (integration conflict).

Quick sanity check

  • When in doubt: git diff HEAD~1 and count the files changed. If it's more than the plan said, you're already off-plan.

7.8 Plan templates and patterns

Most plans fall into one of four structural patterns. Recognising the pattern lets you get to a draft plan faster — either by prompting the agent with the pattern name, or by instantiating the template yourself.

Pattern Typical task sequence Watch for
Bug fix Reproduce → write failing test → fix → verify → commit Fix scope creep — fix only the bug, not the surrounding code
Feature add Scaffold → types/interfaces → core logic → CLI/API surface → tests → docs → commit Tests written after logic (TDD inverts this — see Ch 8)
Refactor Characterise tests → extract → move → update callers → verify → commit No behaviour change — any test failure is a bug in the refactor
Migration Shim old API → introduce new → migrate callers one-by-one → remove shim → verify → commit Never remove the shim until all callers are migrated

Using the writing-plans skill with a pattern

  • The superpowers:writing-plans skill accepts a pattern argument (--pattern bug-fix | feature | refactor | migration) and generates a template-populated plan.
  • You still fill in the specifics — file names, function signatures, test commands — but the skeleton is correct.

7.9 When to skip the plan (yes, sometimes)

Planning has a real cost: it takes time, requires a spec first, and can feel like bureaucratic overhead on obviously small changes. The rule is: plan when the agent could go wrong; skip when it can't.

Safe to skip

  • A single-file typo or doc fix; renaming a variable with a find-and-replace; adding one line to a config file — trivially mechanical changes where the diff will obviously be correct. Just give the agent a direct instruction and review the diff.
  • Exploratory or throwaway work where you're not committing the output — prototyping an approach, generating a draft to read and discard, investigating a bug by trial and error. Plans impose commit structure; explorations don't benefit from that structure.

Not safe to skip

  • Any change that touches more than two files.
  • Any change that involves a new abstraction or interface.
  • Any change where a wrong approach would require undoing more than 20 lines.
  • Any change that a teammate will review. For these, the two-minute plan review (7.6) is not overhead — it is the review.
  • Decision test: if the agent goes wrong, how long does recovery take? If the answer is more than five minutes, write a plan.

7.10 Plans across sessions — handoff and resume

Agentic sessions are bounded by context windows, working hours, and attention. A feature that takes four hours of elapsed time will probably span multiple sessions. The plan file is the mechanism that makes multi-session work coherent.

Handoff protocol

  • Before closing a session mid-plan, the agent should update the plan file to mark completed tasks (e.g., prefix with [x]) and annotate the current task with a "stopped here" note including the last commit SHA.
  • Commit this updated plan file. Commit message: plan(gitlog-report): checkpoint after task 3 — resume at task 4.

Resume protocol

  • Open a new session, inject the spec and the updated plan file at the start of the context.
  • Prompt: "Read PLAN.md. Tasks 1–3 are complete (see commit abc1234). Continue from Task 4."
  • The agent reconstructs working state from the plan + git log, not from conversation history. This is reliable across model updates, team members, and days-long gaps.

Team handoff

  • If a different engineer (or agent) picks up mid-plan, the same protocol applies.
  • The plan file encodes intent; the git log encodes what was done; together they are a complete handoff document — no "what was I doing?" conversation needed.
  • The superpowers:executing-plans skill handles checkpoint and resume automatically when invoked with --resume. For manual workflows, the pattern above requires no tooling beyond git.

7.11 Mini-walkthrough: write the gitlog-report plan

Mini-walkthrough · gitlog-report — write the plan

Where we are: Ch 6.11 produced the gitlog-report spec with seven acceptance criteria. We now decompose those criteria into a numbered plan an agent can execute task by task. Each task follows TDD order: write the test → run it (confirm it fails) → implement → verify → commit.

Acceptance criteria from Ch 6.11 spec (our input):

  • Reads git log from CWD or --path <dir>
  • Per-author: commit count, days active, commits/day, lines added, lines removed
  • Top 5 most-touched files per author (by line-change delta)
  • JSON output (default); Markdown report via --format md
  • --since / --until date range filters (ISO 8601)
  • Empty repository: zero-row output, exit code 0
  • Merge commits: counted in total, lines excluded by default; opt-in via --include-merges

The plan — 6 tasks:

# PLAN: gitlog-report
# Spec: specs/gitlog-report/spec.md
# Pattern: feature-add (TDD order)

## [1] Scaffold — package + CLI entry point
Files: pyproject.toml, src/gitlog_report/__init__.py,
       src/gitlog_report/cli.py, tests/__init__.py
Steps:
  1. Create pyproject.toml with [project.scripts] entry point
  2. Create package stub files
  3. Write test: test_cli_help_exits_0 — assert subprocess returns 0
  4. Run test (confirm it fails — ImportError or ModuleNotFoundError)
  5. Implement cli.py stub: argparse with --path, --format,
     --since, --until, --include-merges
  6. Run test (confirm it passes)
Verify: pytest tests/test_cli.py::test_cli_help_exits_0 passes
Commit: feat(scaffold): initialise gitlog-report package

## [2] Git log parser — ingest & model
Files: src/gitlog_report/parser.py, tests/test_parser.py,
       tests/fixtures/sample.log
Steps:
  1. Define Commit datatype: sha, author, email, date, files, lines+/-
  2. Write tests: parse 3-commit fixture log; assert field values;
     merge commit detected; binary file excluded
  3. Run tests (all fail — parser not implemented)
  4. Implement parse_git_log(path: str) -> list[Commit]
     using git log --numstat --no-merges (default)
  5. Run tests (all pass)
Verify: pytest tests/test_parser.py -v — 4 tests pass
Commit: feat(parser): parse git log into Commit records

## [3] Aggregation — per-author statistics
Files: src/gitlog_report/aggregate.py, tests/test_aggregate.py
Steps:
  1. Write tests: aggregate 3-author fixture; assert commit counts,
     days-active, commits/day (float), lines+/-, top-5 files
  2. Run tests (all fail)
  3. Implement aggregate(commits: list[Commit]) -> list[AuthorStats]
     AuthorStats: author, email, commit_count, days_active, commits_per_day,
                  lines_added, lines_removed, top_files[5]
  4. Run tests (all pass)
Verify: pytest tests/test_aggregate.py -v — all tests pass
Commit: feat(aggregate): compute per-author contribution stats

## [4] Output formatters — JSON and Markdown
Files: src/gitlog_report/formatters.py, tests/test_formatters.py
Steps:
  1. Write tests: format_json(stats) returns valid JSON matching schema;
     format_md(stats) returns Markdown table with correct headers
  2. Run tests (all fail)
  3. Implement format_json + format_md
  4. Run tests (all pass)
Verify: pytest tests/test_formatters.py -v — all tests pass
Commit: feat(formatters): JSON and Markdown output formatters

## [5] Date filtering + edge cases
Files: src/gitlog_report/parser.py (update), tests/test_parser.py (extend)
Steps:
  1. Write tests: --since/--until filter by ISO date; empty repo returns [];
     --include-merges includes merge commit line counts
  2. Run tests (fail — filter logic not implemented)
  3. Add date range args to parse_git_log; pass --after/--before to git log
  4. Handle unborn HEAD: catch CalledProcessError, return []
  5. Run tests (all pass)
Verify: pytest tests/test_parser.py -v — all tests pass (including new 3)
Commit: feat(parser): date filtering and empty-repo edge case

## [6] CLI integration + end-to-end test
Files: src/gitlog_report/cli.py (complete), tests/test_e2e.py
Steps:
  1. Wire main(): parse args → parse_git_log → aggregate → format → print
  2. Write e2e test: run gitlog-report on this repo's own .git;
     assert output is valid JSON with at least one author entry
  3. Run e2e test (may fail — integration issues)
  4. Fix any integration issues
  5. Run full suite: pytest tests/ -v
Verify: all tests pass; gitlog-report --format md produces a Markdown table
Commit: feat(cli): complete CLI integration — gitlog-report ready

How to invoke the plan agent: with the plan file committed, start a new session and prompt: "Read specs/gitlog-report/spec.md and PLAN.md. Execute the plan task by task, committing after each task. Ask before proceeding past any task that fails its verify step."

Next chapter: we execute Tasks 1–2 using Test-Driven Development — writing the failing test first, then watching the agent make it pass. → Ch 8: Test-Driven Development with Agents.

← Ch 6. Spec-Driven Development → Ch 8. Test-Driven Development with Agents → Ch 11. Multi-Agent & Parallel

8. Test-Driven Development with Agents

What you'll learn: why TDD becomes more important (not less) with agents, how the red-green-refactor cycle works as a control rod, the test types that survive agent edits, and how to write the gitlog-report tests before the implementation.

8.1 TDD with agents — does it still make sense? (yes, more than ever)

A common reaction to AI-assisted coding is: "The agent writes code so fast, do I really need to slow down with tests?" The answer is the opposite of what that reaction implies. TDD matters more with agents, not less.

Why tests matter more, not less

  • When you write code by hand, you have a mental model of what it does — you can feel when something is off. Agents don't have that sensory feedback loop.
  • Agents generate code that looks convincing, compiles, and passes a cursory read. Without a failing test to refute them, they can claim "done" while the behaviour is still wrong — and they will sound confident doing it.
  • Tests are the only objective signal an agent can't fake: they either pass or they don't.
  • When you give an agent a failing test and the instruction "make this pass without modifying the test," you have created an unambiguous success criterion. The agent iterates against that criterion. Without it, the agent optimises for your approval — a much noisier signal.

Three concrete problems TDD prevents in agentic work

  • Silent regressions: Agent edits file A to fix a bug; test suite immediately tells you file B broke. Without tests you discover this at demo time.
  • Scope creep by accident: Agents occasionally rewrite more than you asked for. Tests catch when "fixing" one function altered the contract of another.
  • Confident wrong answers: Agents produce fluent, plausible outputs. Tests are the blunt instrument that says "no, the output was wrong" regardless of how fluent it sounded.

The core discipline

  • Before you ask the agent to implement anything, you — or the agent under your direction — must write a failing test that encodes the acceptance criterion.
  • The test is a compressed, executable version of the spec (see Ch 6). Implementation is the activity of making it pass.

8.2 Red → green → refactor as a control rod

The classic TDD cycle is red → green → refactor. With agents this cycle becomes a control rod — the mechanism that prevents the system from going critical.

🔴
RED
Write a failing test.
Run it. Confirm red.
You own this step.
→
🟢
GREEN
Tell agent: make this
test pass; don't touch
the test file.
→
🔵
REFACTOR
Agent cleans up.
Suite stays green.
You approve the diff.

How each step works with an agent

  • Red: You write (or review) the failing test — this is a human responsibility. The test encodes your interpretation of the spec. If the agent writes the test without you verifying it against the spec, you risk the agent testing the wrong thing — producing false confidence. At minimum, read and approve every test that enters the suite.
  • Green: Hand the failing test to the agent with a constrained prompt: "Make test_date_filter pass. Do not modify tests/test_gitlog.py. Show me the diff before writing it." The agent iterates. You watch the test output. Green means done — nothing more.
  • Refactor: Once green, the agent may refactor for clarity, performance, or structure. Confirm the suite stays green after each refactor step. Never let the agent refactor on a red suite — that way lies a codebase where nothing works and no one knows why.

Why the control-rod metaphor fits

  • The test suite absorbs energy from the agent's generative capacity and releases it in controlled, verifiable increments.
  • Remove the tests (or let the agent update them freely) and the reaction goes uncontrolled.

8.3 Writing the failing test first — yourself, or with the agent

Gold vs. silver standard for test authorship

  • Gold standard — you write the test: you have read the spec (Ch 6), understand the acceptance criteria, and translate one criterion into one test function. The test is small, has a single assertion, and has a name that reads like a sentence: test_date_filter_excludes_commits_before_start_date.
  • Silver standard — agent drafts, you verify: ask: "Read specs/gitlog-report/spec.md. Write failing pytest tests for the acceptance criteria in §3. Do not write any implementation. Return only the test file." Then read every test against the spec line by line. Delete any test not traceable to a specific acceptance criterion.
  • The "test what the spec says" rule (see Ch 6): a test not corresponding to a named acceptance criterion is speculative. Speculative tests encode the agent's assumptions, not yours — they pass when the agent satisfies its own assumptions, creating a closed loop that excludes your intent entirely.

Checklist before adding any test

  • Can I point to the exact line in the spec this test covers?
  • Does the test name match the acceptance criterion in plain language?
  • Does the test fail right now (against no implementation or a stub)?
  • Does the test have exactly one reason to fail?

When to revise

  • If any answer is "no" or "I'm not sure," revise the test before adding it to the suite.

8.4 The "stop until green" discipline

This is a simple rule with profound consequences: do not move to the next task while any test in the suite is red.

Why agents violate this — and why they're wrong

  • Agents will routinely try to move on. The pattern: "Test X is failing but that's because we haven't implemented feature Y yet — let me implement Y first and come back to X."
  • This is almost always wrong. A failing test means the current task is incomplete. Moving on builds a stack of incomplete tasks that interact in ways that become progressively harder to untangle.

Correct response to a red test

  1. Stop. Do not write new features.
  2. Understand why the test is red. Read the error output literally.
  3. If the test is wrong (tests the wrong thing), fix the test and document why.
  4. If the implementation is wrong, fix the implementation.
  5. Run the full suite. Confirm all-green before proceeding.

Applied to agents in practice

  • When you see an agent producing output and your test runner shows red, interrupt. Paste the test failure into the agent's context.
  • Ask: "This test is still failing. Do not add new code. Fix only what is needed to make this test pass." The constraint "do not add new code" prevents the agent from burying the failure under additional complexity.
  • The discipline also applies to refactoring: never ask an agent to refactor code while tests are red. A red-plus-refactor session produces a diff where bug and cleanup are entangled, making review and bisection nearly impossible.

8.5 Golden tests — fixed, hand-curated truth

What a golden test is

  • A golden test has a hand-curated, fixed input and a hand-verified, fixed expected output. No generated fixtures. No dynamic comparisons.
  • The expected value is typed in by a human who checked it against the spec and confirmed it is correct.
  • Golden tests are the most durable test type in an agentic codebase. Because both input and output are fixed strings or structures, no agent refactor can accidentally make them pass on wrong output — if the output changes for any reason, the test fails.

Example for gitlog-report (two authors, three commits):

GOLDEN_GIT_LOG = """
abc1234 Alice 2024-01-15 Add parser module
