def5678 Bob   2024-01-16 Fix edge case in parser
ghi9012 Alice 2024-01-17 Add CLI entry point
"""

GOLDEN_EXPECTED = {
    "Alice": {"commit_count": 2, "files_changed": 0, "insertions": 0, "deletions": 0},
    "Bob":   {"commit_count": 1, "files_changed": 0, "insertions": 0, "deletions": 0},
}

def test_golden_multi_author():
    # parse_log_text() parses captured `git log` output into the per-author
    # summary — the pure-parsing layer beneath parse_gitlog(repo_path, ...),
    # which shells out to git first and then calls this same parser.
    result = parse_log_text(GOLDEN_GIT_LOG)
    assert result == GOLDEN_EXPECTED

Rules for golden tests

  • The expected value is always a literal — never computed at test time.
  • When the expected output legitimately changes (because a requirement changed), update the golden value manually and commit with a message explaining why.
  • Never let an agent update a golden value without your explicit review. Treat any agent-proposed change to a golden value as a requirement change, not a test fix.

Best uses

  • Golden tests are especially powerful for parsing, formatting, and report-generation tasks — exactly the kind of work gitlog-report does.

8.6 Snapshot tests and their dangers with agents

Snapshot tests (as offered by Jest, pytest-snapshot, and similar tools) automatically capture the output of a function and save it as a file. On subsequent runs, the test compares current output to the saved snapshot and fails only if they differ.

Why snapshot updates are a trap with agents

  • Agents will happily update snapshots to match wrong output. The pattern: agent introduces a regression → test fails → agent runs snapshot update → test passes → agent reports success → you merge broken code.
  • The snapshot update command inverts the relationship between test and truth: instead of the expected output being fixed and the implementation being wrong, the expected output becomes wrong and the test silently accepts it.

How to treat snapshot tests in an agentic codebase

  • Flag snapshot update as a reviewed action. Any commit that touches a __snapshots__ directory or .snap file must be reviewed by a human who verifies the new snapshot is correct.
  • Prefer golden tests (§8.5) for any output that can be hand-curated.
  • If you use snapshots, add a CI lint rule that fails if snapshot files are modified without a corresponding label/tag indicating human review.
  • Never run --updateSnapshot (or equivalent) in an automated agent loop. That flag must require explicit human invocation.

Bottom line on snapshots

  • Snapshots are not inherently bad — they catch unexpected changes.
  • They are bad when the mechanism for accepting changes is automated or undiscriminated.
  • In agentic contexts, assume the agent will find and use the update path unless you explicitly prevent it.

8.7 Anti-mocking discipline — integration over isolation

The classic argument for mocks is speed and isolation: unit tests should not touch the network, the filesystem, or the database. This is sound advice for human-written tests in CI with shared resources — but it is partially wrong for agentic codebases.

The mock problem with agents

  • Agents mock the thing they just wrote. If the agent writes GitLogParser and then writes a test for it using a mock of GitLogParser, the test is testing the mock, not the parser — it passes trivially while the real parser can be completely broken.

The anti-mocking discipline

  • Prefer integration tests against real dependencies wherever those dependencies are cheap to run locally.
  • Use a real temporary directory instead of mocking the filesystem.
  • Use a real in-memory SQLite database instead of mocking your ORM.
  • Use a real subprocess call to git log on a temp repo instead of mocking the git output.
  • Use real HTTP fixtures (recorded once, replayed) instead of mocking requests.

Why integration tests beat mocks here

  • Integration tests are slower but they test the actual contract. An agent that breaks the real git log interaction will be caught by an integration test and will not be caught by a mock-based unit test.
  • Exception: external services with rate limits, expensive third-party APIs, non-deterministic services. For these, record real responses once (using a tool like vcrpy or msw) and replay them.
  • Recorded responses are fundamentally different from hand-writing mocks — the recorded response is real truth, not agent-constructed fiction.
Golden tests
Hand-curated input + fixed expected output. Best for parsers, formatters, and report generators where truth can be written down; fragile if output is legitimately non-deterministic.
Snapshot tests
Auto-capture output on first run, fail on change. Good for catching unexpected regressions; dangerous with agents — they'll run --updateSnapshot and silently accept wrong output as truth.
Integration tests
Exercise real dependencies (filesystem, subprocess, in-memory DB). Best for catching contract mismatches agents introduce when they mock what they wrote; slower than unit tests.
Unit tests
Isolate a single function against mocked dependencies. Fast and precise, but agents mock the thing they just wrote — passing trivially while the real implementation breaks.
Eval loops
Run the actual prompt against fixed inputs and check against expected outputs. Only type that catches prompt regressions and model-version drift; expensive (real LLM calls) so run on slow path only.

8.8 Eval loops — tests for the prompt, not just the code

Why eval loops are necessary

  • When your system includes an LLM call — a prompt asking the model to extract, classify, summarise, or decide — the code around it may be correct while the prompt itself is producing wrong outputs.
  • Unit tests on the code will not catch this. You need an eval loop: a test that runs the actual prompt against a fixed set of inputs and checks the outputs against fixed expected values.
  • An eval loop has the same structure as a golden test (§8.5), except the "function under test" is a prompt-plus-model call rather than a pure function.
EVAL_CASES = [
    {
        "input": "Extract the author name from: 'abc123 Alice 2024-01-15 Fix bug'",
        "expected": "Alice",
    },
    {
        "input": "Extract the author name from: 'def456 Bob Smith 2024-02-01 Add feature'",
        "expected": "Bob Smith",
    },
]

def test_author_extraction_eval():
    for case in EVAL_CASES:
        result = call_llm(AUTHOR_EXTRACTION_PROMPT, case["input"])
        assert result.strip() == case["expected"], (
            f"Prompt produced wrong output for input: {case['input']!r}"
        )

What eval loops catch

  • Prompt regressions when the prompt template is edited.
  • Model drift when you upgrade to a new model version.
  • Edge cases the prompt handles inconsistently (multi-word names, special characters, empty input).

When to run eval loops

  • Eval loops are expensive (real LLM calls) — run them on a slow path: pre-commit on prompt file changes, or nightly CI, not on every unit test run.
  • The key is that they exist and are non-negotiable gates before shipping a prompt change.
  • For agents writing prompt-heavy features, the eval loop is the spec equivalent for the prompt layer. Require it before any prompt change is merged.

8.9 Coverage as a verification signal

Coverage is necessary but not sufficient

  • Line coverage tells you which lines were executed by at least one test — a useful signal but an unreliable quality metric.
  • The failure mode is coverage theatre: 100% line coverage with tests that make no meaningful assertions. Every line runs; nothing is verified.
  • A line with 0% coverage is definitely untested. A line with 100% coverage might still be untested in any meaningful sense.

How to use coverage correctly in an agentic codebase

  • Use coverage to find gaps, not to prove quality. A module at 40% coverage has obvious gaps to address. A module at 100% coverage needs its tests read — not celebrated.
  • Use branch coverage, not just line coverage. Branch coverage catches untested conditional paths. Agents often write code with multiple branches and test only the happy path.
  • Set a floor, not a ceiling. Require, say, 80% branch coverage as a CI gate. Don't optimise for 100% — that incentivises coverage theatre.
  • Mutation testing is the honest metric. Tools like mutmut (Python) or Stryker (JS) introduce small bugs and check whether any test fails. If 100% of mutations survive (no test catches any injected bug), your coverage is theatre regardless of the number.

The right question for agents

  • Never let an agent report "coverage is 95%" as evidence of completeness.
  • Ask instead: "How many of the acceptance criteria from the spec are covered by assertions in the test suite?" That is the number that matters.

8.10 When the agent writes bad tests (and how to spot it)

Agents write bad tests — not from malice, but from the same pattern-matching that makes them write good-looking code: they optimise for tests that pass, not for tests that would catch real bugs.

Sign What it looks like Why it's bad
Tautological test assert result == result or assert len(result) > 0 Always passes; tests nothing
Tests the mock Mock returns X; test asserts X; real function never called Implementation can be broken; test will pass
Asserts implementation detail assert parser._internal_cache == {} Breaks on every refactor; tests structure not behaviour
No assertion Test calls function, captures result, ends without assert Passes even if function throws wrong output (some frameworks)
Happy-path only 10 tests, all with valid well-formed inputs Edge cases (empty input, malformed data) completely untested
Spec-untraceable test Test covers behaviour not in the spec Agent inventing requirements; may conflict with real spec
Soft assertion assert "Alice" in str(result) instead of exact match Passes even when result is malformed; hides bugs in structure

How to catch bad tests systematically

  1. Run the test suite against a known-broken implementation. Introduce a deliberate bug (delete a function body, return a wrong value) and run the tests. Any test that still passes is either testing the wrong thing or is tautological.
  2. Read assertions literally. For every assert statement, ask: "What would make this assertion false?" If the answer is "almost nothing," the assertion is weak.
  3. Count the assertions per test. A test with zero assertions is always a bug. A test with one assertion that asserts a trivially true condition is effectively zero.
  4. Run mutation testing periodically (§8.9). Any surviving mutant is a test gap.

8.11 Mini-walkthrough: write the gitlog-report tests first

Mini-walkthrough · gitlog-report — write the failing tests

This walkthrough builds on the acceptance criteria from Ch 6.11 (spec for gitlog-report) and the task breakdown from Ch 7.11 (PLAN.md Tasks 1–2: parse git log, aggregate per-author stats). We write the failing tests before any implementation. The agent's job in Ch 9 will be to make them pass.

All four tests below are in tests/test_parser.py. The module under test is src/gitlog_report/parser.py, which does not exist yet — that is the point. These tests are the specification in executable form. (The spec's --since/--until CLI flags map to the start_date/end_date parameters of parse_gitlog.)

Setup — temporary git repo fixture

import subprocess, textwrap, os, pytest
from pathlib import Path
from gitlog_report.parser import parse_gitlog  # ImportError until Ch 9

@pytest.fixture()
def tmp_git_repo(tmp_path):
    """Create a minimal git repo with a known commit history."""
    repo = tmp_path / "repo"
    repo.mkdir()
    subprocess.run(["git", "init"], cwd=repo, check=True, capture_output=True)
    subprocess.run(
        ["git", "config", "user.email", "test@example.com"], cwd=repo, check=True
    )
    subprocess.run(
        ["git", "config", "user.name", "Test"], cwd=repo, check=True
    )
    return repo

def _make_commit(repo: Path, author: str, date: str, message: str):
    """Add a dummy file and commit as author on date."""
    (repo / "file.txt").write_text(message)
    subprocess.run(["git", "add", "."], cwd=repo, check=True, capture_output=True)
    env = {**os.environ, "GIT_AUTHOR_NAME": author,
           "GIT_AUTHOR_EMAIL": f"{author.lower().replace(' ', '.')}@example.com",
           "GIT_AUTHOR_DATE": date,
           "GIT_COMMITTER_NAME": author,
           "GIT_COMMITTER_EMAIL": f"{author.lower().replace(' ', '.')}@example.com",
           "GIT_COMMITTER_DATE": date}
    subprocess.run(
        ["git", "commit", "-m", message], cwd=repo, check=True, capture_output=True, env=env
    )

Test 1 — empty repository

# Acceptance criterion (Ch 6.11, AC6 — empty repository):
#   Given a repo with no commits, the report returns an empty dict.

def test_empty_repo_returns_empty_dict(tmp_git_repo):
    result = parse_gitlog(tmp_git_repo, start_date=None, end_date=None)
    assert result == {}, f"Expected empty dict, got {result!r}"

Test 2 — single author

# Acceptance criterion (Ch 6.11, AC2 — per-author commit count):
#   Given a repo with N commits from one author,
#   the report lists that author with commit_count == N.

def test_single_author_commit_count(tmp_git_repo):
    _make_commit(tmp_git_repo, "Alice", "2024-01-10T10:00:00", "first commit")
    _make_commit(tmp_git_repo, "Alice", "2024-01-11T10:00:00", "second commit")
    _make_commit(tmp_git_repo, "Alice", "2024-01-12T10:00:00", "third commit")

    result = parse_gitlog(tmp_git_repo, start_date=None, end_date=None)

    assert set(result.keys()) == {"Alice"}
    assert result["Alice"]["commit_count"] == 3

Test 3 — multiple authors

# Acceptance criterion (Ch 6.11, AC2 — per-author commit count):
#   Given commits from multiple authors, each author appears
#   in the report with their own correct commit count.

def test_multi_author_counts(tmp_git_repo):
    _make_commit(tmp_git_repo, "Alice", "2024-01-10T10:00:00", "alice 1")
    _make_commit(tmp_git_repo, "Bob",   "2024-01-11T10:00:00", "bob 1")
    _make_commit(tmp_git_repo, "Alice", "2024-01-12T10:00:00", "alice 2")
    _make_commit(tmp_git_repo, "Carol", "2024-01-13T10:00:00", "carol 1")

    result = parse_gitlog(tmp_git_repo, start_date=None, end_date=None)

    assert set(result.keys()) == {"Alice", "Bob", "Carol"}
    assert result["Alice"]["commit_count"] == 2
    assert result["Bob"]["commit_count"]   == 1
    assert result["Carol"]["commit_count"] == 1

Test 4 — date filter (THE test Ch 9.11 will use to catch the injected bug)

# Acceptance criterion (Ch 6.11, AC5 — date range filter):
#   Given a start_date, commits before that date are excluded.
#
# *** NOTE: This is the test that Ch 9.11 deliberately breaks by injecting
#     an off-by-one bug into the date comparison (>= vs >).
#     Watch this test catch the regression. ***

def test_date_filter_excludes_commits_before_start_date(tmp_git_repo):
    _make_commit(tmp_git_repo, "Alice", "2024-01-05T10:00:00", "before window")  # excluded
    _make_commit(tmp_git_repo, "Alice", "2024-01-10T10:00:00", "boundary commit")  # included (on boundary)
    _make_commit(tmp_git_repo, "Alice", "2024-01-15T10:00:00", "after start")    # included

    result = parse_gitlog(
        tmp_git_repo,
        start_date="2024-01-10",
        end_date=None,
    )

    assert set(result.keys()) == {"Alice"}, (
        "Alice should appear — she has commits on and after 2024-01-10"
    )
    assert result["Alice"]["commit_count"] == 2, (
        "Expected 2 commits (boundary + after), got "
        f"{result['Alice']['commit_count']}. "
        "If this is 3, the start_date filter is not excluding early commits. "
        "If this is 1, the filter is using strict > instead of >= on the boundary date."
    )

Run them now — all four should fail with ImportError (the module doesn't exist yet). That red state is the correct starting point.

$ pytest tests/test_gitlog.py -v
FAILED tests/test_gitlog.py::test_empty_repo_returns_empty_dict - ImportError
FAILED tests/test_gitlog.py::test_single_author_commit_count - ImportError
FAILED tests/test_gitlog.py::test_multi_author_counts - ImportError
FAILED tests/test_gitlog.py::test_date_filter_excludes_commits_before_start_date - ImportError

Four red tests. The suite is ready. In Ch 9, we inject a bug into the date-filter implementation and watch test_date_filter_excludes_commits_before_start_date catch it exactly — demonstrating why writing that test first (and writing it precisely on the boundary condition) matters.

Next chapter: we'll inject a bug into the date-filter implementation and watch the date-filter test catch it. → Ch 9: Debugging with Agents.

← Ch 6. Spec-Driven Development ← Ch 7. Plan-Driven Execution → Ch 9. Debugging with Agents → Ch 10. Safety, Review & Verification

9. Debugging with Agents

What you'll learn: the systematic debugging loop with an agent, why agents fix bugs wrong without it, evidence patterns, and how to debug the gitlog-report off-by-one bug step by step.

9.1 The systematic debugging loop

Debugging with an agent without a framework is a gamble. The agent will pattern-match to the most plausible-looking cause, propose a fix, and report success — even when the root cause is untouched. To prevent this, Claude Code provides the superpowers:systematic-debugging skill, which enforces a 5-stage loop before any fix is written.

The 5-stage cycle

  1. Reproduce — confirm the bug is deterministically triggerable. No reproduction, no debugging.
  2. Isolate — narrow from "the system is broken" to "this specific input to this specific function produces this specific wrong output."
  3. Root cause — identify the exact line, condition, or invariant violation that causes the wrong output. Not the symptom — the cause.
  4. Fix — apply the minimal change that corrects the root cause. Not the symptom.
  5. Verify — rerun the failing test (green), then rerun the entire suite (no regressions).

Why the order is enforced

  • You cannot skip to stage 4 (Fix) without the agent having produced evidence at stages 2 and 3. This constraint is deliberate: it prevents the most common agent failure mode — applying a patch that silences the symptom while leaving the root cause in place.
  • To invoke: in Claude Code, type /systematic-debugging (via the skill). Describe the bug. The agent asks you to complete each stage before advancing. Resist the urge to skip ahead.
1
Reproduce

Confirm the bug is deterministically triggerable. Write the reproduction recipe — exact steps, preconditions, expected vs. actual output — before touching any code. No reproduction, no debugging.

2
Isolate

Narrow from "the system is broken" to "this specific input to this specific function produces this specific wrong output." Produce the minimal failing case. Agents will skip this and jump to the full system — don't let them.

3
Root cause

Read the actual code. State a falsifiable hypothesis: "The bug is line 47 of parser.py — the condition uses > instead of >=." Not the symptom. The cause.

4
Fix

Apply the minimal change that corrects the root cause. Not the symptom. If the fix touches more than 3 lines, question whether it's truly minimal or whether it's bundling unrelated cleanup.

5
Verify

Rerun the failing test (green), then rerun the full suite (no regressions). Fixes that introduce regressions traded one bug for another — both are failures.

9.2 Reproduce → isolate → root cause → fix → verify

Reproduce reliably first

  • If you cannot reproduce the bug on demand, your fix is a hope. A fix applied to an unreproducible bug may appear to work — the bug simply didn't trigger — and will resurface under conditions you didn't anticipate.

The reproduction recipe pattern: before touching any code, write down (or have the agent write down) the exact steps that reliably produce the failure:

# Reproduction recipe — write this before any fix attempt
# Bug: date_filter includes boundary commit when it should not
# Preconditions: gitlog_report/parser.py at commit abc1234
# Steps:
#   1. Create tmp git repo with commits on 2024-01-05, 2024-01-10, 2024-01-15
#   2. Run parse_gitlog(repo, start_date="2024-01-10", end_date=None)
#   3. Assert result["Alice"]["commit_count"] == 2
# Expected: 2 (commits on 2024-01-10 and 2024-01-15)
# Actual:   1 (commit on 2024-01-10 excluded)
# Confirmed on: Python 3.11, pytest 7.4

What each stage actually requires

  • With the recipe written, you have pinned the bug in space and time. Any fix that doesn't make the recipe produce the expected result hasn't fixed the bug.
  • Isolate means producing a minimal failing case — the smallest input that still triggers the bug. Agents will skip this and jump to the full system. Don't let them. A minimal case eliminates false suspects and makes the root cause obvious.
  • Root cause means reading the actual code and stating a falsifiable hypothesis: "The bug is on line 47 of parser.py — the condition uses > instead of >=."
  • Verify means running the full suite, not just the test that was red. Fixes that introduce regressions traded one bug for another.

9.3 Bugs the agent will fix wrong (and why)

Agents don't maliciously produce wrong fixes — they pattern-match to common resolutions and optimise for making the immediate test pass. The table below shows the recurring failure modes.

Bad fix type What it looks like Why it's wrong
Cargo-cult fix Copies a pattern from a similar file without understanding why Fix is coincidental; root cause is unchanged; bug re-emerges on next edge case
Surface-symptom patch Wraps the buggy function in a try/except or adds a special-case check for the known-bad input Root cause survives; next input that triggers it won't be caught by the special case
Test mutation Changes the assertion in the failing test to match the wrong output The test now passes, but the spec is violated; the bug is hidden behind a lying test
Over-generalisation Rewrites a 5-line function into a 40-line one to "handle all edge cases" Introduces new surface area; makes future bugs harder to isolate
Wrong layer fix Fixes the calling code instead of the function that produced the wrong value Every caller now needs the same fix; root cause is still in the shared function
Snapshot update Runs --updateSnapshot to "fix" the failing test Makes wrong output into the new expected value; the bug is now the spec (see §8.6)

The universal tell — and the pushback

  • For all of these: the agent says "fixed" and the immediately-failing test goes green — but you haven't seen the agent read and reason about the function body.
  • Push back: "Show me which line was wrong and why. Do not show me the diff yet."

9.4 Root cause vs patch — keeping the agent honest

Patch vs. root cause fix

  • A patch silences the symptom. A root cause fix corrects the invariant that was violated. The test may go green in both cases — only one has actually fixed the bug.

The technique for forcing root cause work is the "why?" cascade. After the agent names a cause, ask "why?" until you reach an invariant — a property of the system that should always hold but was violated:

Agent: "The count is 1 instead of 2 because the boundary commit is excluded."
You:   "Why is it excluded?"
Agent: "Because the date comparison uses > instead of >=."
You:   "Why does it use > ?"
Agent: "The initial implementation assumed exclusive lower bound."
You:   "What is the correct invariant?"
Agent: "start_date is inclusive. Commits on start_date must be included."
You:   "That is the root cause. Now show me the minimal fix."

When to stop the cascade — and how to spot a patch

  • The cascade stops when the agent can state an invariant — a design decision that was either wrong or correctly specified but incorrectly implemented. Everything above that level is symptom, not cause.
  • Signs you have a patch, not a fix: the fix adds a special case, wraps in a try/except, modifies the test, or is larger than 3 lines for a bug the cascade traced to a single operator.
  • A root cause fix for an off-by-one is one character. If the agent's proposed diff is 30 lines, ask: "Is this the root cause fix or a workaround?"

9.5 Reading the agent's hypotheses critically

When you paste a bug into an agent, it will generate hypotheses. These are often plausible and frequently wrong. Reading them critically is a skill.

What to look for

  • Evidence vs. assertion. "The bug is in parse_date" — did the agent read parse_date? Ask: "Show me the line in parse_date that supports this hypothesis." If the agent can't cite a specific line, it's guessing.
  • Confident tone on uncertain reasoning. Agents express hypotheses with the same fluency regardless of confidence. A confident-sounding hypothesis with no cited evidence is a signal to verify, not accept.
  • First hypothesis bias. Agents tend to anchor on their first hypothesis. If you push back once, they adjust. If you push back twice, they often return to the original hypothesis. This is a sign to run the experiment yourself: test the hypothesis by deliberate insertion of a print statement or assertion.
  • Hypothesis count. An agent that lists 5 possible causes for a single failing test is diffusing responsibility. Ask it to rank them by likelihood and provide evidence for the top one before moving on.

The rule: hypotheses are test candidates, not conclusions

  • Treat agent hypotheses as predictions: "if this is the cause, then changing X should make the test pass." Test the prediction.
  • If it fails, the hypothesis was wrong — regardless of how confident the agent sounded.

9.6 Bisecting with the agent's help

When you don't know when a bug was introduced, git bisect is the most reliable tool. Agents can help run and interpret it, but you must stay in control of the process.

git bisect workflow with an agent

git bisect start
git bisect bad                        # current commit is broken
git bisect good <known-good-sha>       # last commit known to pass the test
# git checks out the midpoint commit
# Ask agent: "Run pytest tests/test_gitlog.py::test_date_filter_excludes_commits_before_start_date"
# If PASS: git bisect good
# If FAIL: git bisect bad
# Repeat until git bisect prints the first-bad commit

Roles in bisect

  • The agent's role: run the test at each bisect step, report pass/fail, tell you which git bisect good/bad to issue.
  • Your role: issue the command and read the final output. Do not let the agent issue git bisect reset or skip steps — it may miss the true first-bad commit.
  • Binary search of inputs is bisect for data, not commits. When a function fails on large input but passes on small input, cut the input in half and recurse into the failing half. Frame the search: "Does the function fail on the first half or the second half of this list?"

Narrowing checklist

  • Start with the full failing case confirmed.
  • Remove half the variables. Does it still fail?
  • Keep halving until you cannot remove anything without the bug disappearing. What remains is the minimal reproducer.
  • Paste the minimal reproducer back to the agent: "This is the smallest input that produces the bug. Now find the root cause."

9.7 Logs, traces, stack frames — feeding evidence

An agent debugging without evidence is reasoning in a vacuum. The quality of the debug session is directly proportional to the quality of evidence you feed it.

What to paste verbatim

  • The full pytest failure output, including the assertion error and the traceback. Do not truncate.
  • The relevant stack frame — the line in your code (not a library) where execution was when the assertion failed.
  • The actual vs. expected values from the assertion message (if present).

What to summarise

  • Long log files — paste only the lines around the error (±10 lines of context).
  • Repeated identical stack frames — paste one instance and note "repeated N times."
  • Long configuration files — paste only the section relevant to the failing behaviour.

What to leave out

  • Library internals that are not part of your codebase (e.g., deep pytest or stdlib frames).
  • Passing test output — only the failures are evidence of the bug.
  • Unrelated error messages from previous sessions.

Concrete example — good evidence paste

FAILED tests/test_gitlog.py::test_date_filter_excludes_commits_before_start_date

AssertionError: Expected 2 commits (boundary + after), got 1.
If this is 1, the filter is using strict > instead of >= on the boundary date.
assert result["Alice"]["commit_count"] == 2
       → result["Alice"]["commit_count"] is 1

Traceback (most recent call last):
  File "tests/test_gitlog.py", line 58, in test_date_filter_excludes_commits_before_start_date
    assert result["Alice"]["commit_count"] == 2, (...)
AssertionError

Why this evidence is sufficient

  • This evidence tells the agent: what test failed, what the assertion was, what the actual vs. expected values were, and which line in your code triggered it. That is sufficient. More is noise.

9.8 When to step in yourself

Agents get stuck. The diagnostic signal is: 3 iterations with no forward progress. If the agent has proposed 3 different fixes, all of which still fail the same test with the same error, the agent is in a pattern-matching loop with insufficient context. Stepping in is not a failure — it is the correct response.

Signs to stop the agent and intervene

  • The same test fails with the same error after 3 agent iterations.
  • The agent's proposed diffs are getting larger with each iteration (scope creep under pressure).
  • The agent starts suggesting changes to the test file to "resolve the conflict."
  • The agent reports a fix as successful but doesn't show the passing test output.
  • The agent introduces a new failure while fixing the original one, and then focuses on the new failure instead.

What to do when you step in

  1. Stop the agent: "Stop. Do not produce any more code."
  2. Read the function body yourself. Not the diff — the function as it currently exists.
  3. Trace the execution manually: follow the inputs through the code path until you find where the output diverges from what you expect.
  4. Identify the exact line. State it to the agent: "The bug is on line 47. The condition is commit_date > start_date. It should be commit_date >= start_date. Make only that change."
  5. Verify the result yourself.

Division of labor

  • The agent is most useful for the exploration and verification phases.
  • The isolation step — reading code and following the execution path — is sometimes faster to do yourself than to explain to an agent in enough detail that it can do it correctly.

9.9 Debugging the agent (not the code) — when prompts go bad

Sometimes the bug is not in the code — it is in the prompt: the instructions you gave the agent that caused it to generate the wrong code in the first place.

Signs that the prompt is the bug

  • The agent generates subtly wrong code every time you ask for the same feature, even after multiple corrections.
  • The agent misunderstands a key term you use (e.g., you say "date filter" and the agent consistently interprets it as exclusive lower bound).
  • The agent adds code you didn't ask for, repeatedly.
  • Fixes to the code keep reverting to the buggy pattern in subsequent sessions.

Debugging a bad prompt — same 5-stage loop

  1. Reproduce: Identify the prompt fragment that consistently produces the wrong output.
  2. Isolate: Remove context from the prompt until you find the minimal fragment that triggers the wrong behaviour.
  3. Root cause: Is the term ambiguous? Is the constraint missing? Is the context contradicting itself?
  4. Fix: Rewrite the ambiguous fragment. Add the missing constraint. Remove the contradiction.
  5. Verify: Run the corrected prompt against the same inputs that produced the bug. Confirm the output is now correct.

Common prompt bugs

  • Under-specified acceptance criteria — the agent fills gaps with assumptions.
  • Absent negative constraints — "do not modify the test file" missing.
  • Ambiguous terminology — the agent resolves the same term differently in different sessions.
  • The fix is always the same: make the constraint explicit, not implicit.

9.10 The systematic-debugging skill and Kilo Debug mode

Claude Code: no /debug command — use the skill

  • Claude Code has no built-in /debug slash command — do not look for one.
  • The correct path is the superpowers:systematic-debugging skill. Invoke via the Skill tool or by typing the skill name in a Claude Code session.
  • The skill enforces the 5-stage cycle (§9.1), requires evidence at each stage, and prevents the agent from advancing to Fix without a stated root cause. It is the structured alternative to ad-hoc debugging.

Kilo Code: dedicated Debug mode

  • Kilo Code provides a dedicated Debug mode designed for targeted diagnostics. It surfaces tools and prompts oriented around reading, tracing, and root-cause analysis rather than writing fixes.
  • Verify exact enforcement guarantees against current Kilo Code docs — mode constraints evolve across versions.
  • The pedagogical value is the same regardless: switching to Debug mode signals to yourself (and the agent) to slow down and diagnose before patching.

Patterns and limits (both tools)

  • Both tools work best when fed the evidence described in §9.7 — full test output, relevant stack frame, minimal reproducer.
  • Neither tool can read your mind about what "correct" means. You must state the expected behaviour explicitly.
  • The systematic loop is not a guarantee. An agent that cannot find the root cause in 5 iterations should be stopped — not given more iterations. At that point, §9.8 applies: you read the code yourself.
  • For bugs in agentic infrastructure (hooks, skills, the harness itself), the debugging process is the same but the evidence is different: paste hook logs, harness configuration, and the exact command that triggered the wrong behaviour.

9.11 Mini-walkthrough: break it, then debug it

Mini-walkthrough · gitlog-report — break it, then debug it

This walkthrough builds directly on Ch 8.11 — specifically test_date_filter_excludes_commits_before_start_date, which was written to cover the boundary condition on start_date. We inject an off-by-one bug into the date_filter function and walk the agent through all 5 stages of the systematic debugging loop. The test from 8.11 catches the bug exactly because it was written on the boundary case.

Step 1 — Inject the bug

In gitlog_report/parser.py, find the date filter condition and change >= to >:

# BEFORE (correct)
if commit_date >= start_date:
    include_commit(commit)

# AFTER (injected bug — off-by-one, boundary commit excluded)
if commit_date > start_date:
    include_commit(commit)

This is a one-character change. The bug is subtle: it only manifests when a commit's date exactly equals start_date — the boundary case. Without the boundary-focused test from Ch 8.11, this bug would pass all happy-path tests.

Step 2 — The failing test

$ pytest tests/test_gitlog.py -v

PASSED  tests/test_gitlog.py::test_empty_repo_returns_empty_dict
PASSED  tests/test_gitlog.py::test_single_author_commit_count
PASSED  tests/test_gitlog.py::test_multi_author_counts
FAILED  tests/test_gitlog.py::test_date_filter_excludes_commits_before_start_date

======================== FAILURES ========================
FAILED tests/test_gitlog.py::test_date_filter_excludes_commits_before_start_date

AssertionError: Expected 2 commits (boundary + after), got 1.
If this is 1, the filter is using strict > instead of >= on the boundary date.
assert result["Alice"]["commit_count"] == 2
       → result["Alice"]["commit_count"] is 1

Exactly one test fails. The error message is the one we wrote in Ch 8.11 — self-diagnosing. The agent now has everything it needs to begin the systematic loop.

Step 3 — Systematic debugging walkthrough

Stage 1 — Reproduce: Paste the test output above to the agent. Ask: "Run test_date_filter_excludes_commits_before_start_date and confirm it fails." The agent confirms: failure is deterministic. Reproduction recipe matches. We proceed.

Stage 2 — Isolate: Ask: "Which input triggers the failure?" The agent traces: the boundary commit (date == start_date) is the suspect. A commit dated 2024-01-15 (after) is included; a commit dated 2024-01-05 (before) is excluded; the commit dated 2024-01-10 (on the boundary, the same as start_date) is excluded when it should be included. The minimal failing case is one commit at exactly start_date.

Stage 3 — Root cause: Ask: "Read gitlog_report/parser.py. Find the date comparison. State the exact line and what it does." The agent reads and reports:

Line 47: if commit_date > start_date:
The condition is strict greater-than. Commits whose date equals start_date
fail this condition and are excluded. The invariant (start_date is inclusive)
is violated. Root cause: operator should be >= not >.

Stage 4 — Fix: Ask: "Apply the minimal fix. Do not change anything else." The agent changes one character:

# Line 47 — one-character fix
- if commit_date > start_date:
+ if commit_date >= start_date:

Stage 5 — Verify: Rerun the failing test first, then the full suite:

$ pytest tests/test_gitlog.py::test_date_filter_excludes_commits_before_start_date -v
PASSED  tests/test_gitlog.py::test_date_filter_excludes_commits_before_start_date

$ pytest tests/test_gitlog.py -v
PASSED  tests/test_gitlog.py::test_empty_repo_returns_empty_dict
PASSED  tests/test_gitlog.py::test_single_author_commit_count
PASSED  tests/test_gitlog.py::test_multi_author_counts
PASSED  tests/test_gitlog.py::test_date_filter_excludes_commits_before_start_date

4 passed in 0.31s — no regressions

Lesson

The test from Ch 8.11 caught the bug exactly because it was written on the boundary condition. The three other tests — empty repo, single author, multi-author — all passed with the injected bug. Only the test that explicitly checked "boundary commit is included" caught the off-by-one. This is why the discipline from §8.3 matters: a test must cover the specific condition it is supposed to protect. A test that only covers the happy path would have let this bug through.

The fix was one character. The diagnosis took 5 structured steps. The ratio is correct: most of debugging is finding the root cause, not writing the fix. The systematic loop ensures you don't write a 30-line workaround for a 1-character bug.

Next chapter: we run a full review pass on the debugged code. → Ch 10: Safety, Review & Verification.

← Ch 8. TDD with Agents → Ch 10. Safety, Review & Verification → Ch 15. Superpowers (systematic-debugging skill)

10. Safety, Review & Verification

What you'll learn: the three review layers, when to use which tool (code review, /security-review, /ultrareview), the verification-before-completion discipline, and how to spot agent hallucinations in code.

10.1 The three review layers — pre-merge, code, security

Every non-trivial change should pass through three distinct review layers before merge. Each layer has a different scope, different tooling, and catches a different class of defect. Treating them as one combined "review" means each one is done shallowly — you need the distinctions.

Layer 1 · Pre-merge (you)

Who: the author, before anyone else sees it.

Catches: obvious blunders, missing tests, incomplete diffs, left-in debug logs, unintended scope creep.

How: read your own diff. git diff --stat and git diff HEAD. Does the changed surface match the ticket?

Layer 2 · Code review (agent or peer)

Who: an agent (via /code-review) or a human reviewer.

Catches: logic errors, missing edge cases, poor naming, architectural drift, test quality.

How: structured prompt or skill. The reviewer reads intent + implementation and flags mismatches.

Layer 3 · Security review (specialist)

Who: /security-review or a security engineer for high-stakes code.

Catches: injection vectors, secret leaks, insecure defaults, dangerous API usage.

How: purpose-built scan focused on attack surface, not correctness. Runs independently of code review.

Pre-merge (you)
Author-run before anyone else sees it. Catches obvious blunders, missing tests, incomplete diffs, debug logs left in, and unintended scope creep. Tool: git diff HEAD.
Code review (agent)
Agent or peer reviews logic, edge cases, naming, and architectural drift. Use a skeptic-stance prompt — not "review this" but "find every place this could fail silently." Tool: /code-review.
Security review (specialized)
Purpose-built scan for injection vectors, secret leaks, insecure defaults, and dangerous API usage. Runs independently — security reviewers are not thinking about naming conventions. Tool: /security-review.

Keep the scopes clean

  • The most common mistake is collapsing all three into a single "does this look good?" pass.
  • Layer 2 reviewers are not thinking about injection vectors; Layer 3 reviewers are not thinking about naming conventions.

10.2 Code review with agents — beyond rubber-stamping

The default is a rubber-stamp

  • Without guidance, an agent will trend toward politeness: it will notice obvious issues but hedge, praise structure before raising concerns, and stop after two or three findings even when more exist.
  • To get real code review, you need a skeptic stance prompt.

The difference:

# Rubber-stamp prompt (don't use)
"Review this code for me."

# Skeptic stance prompt (use this)
"You are a senior engineer who is skeptical of AI-generated code.
Your job is to find bugs, not confirm correctness.
List every place this code could fail silently, produce wrong output,
or behave differently under concurrent load.
Be terse. Be ruthless. Do not praise anything.
Rate each finding: BLOCKER | MAJOR | MINOR."

Reviewing the review (recursion)

  • The agent's review is itself an artifact that can contain errors. Agents hallucinate findings — flagging non-existent bugs, citing APIs incorrectly, or misreading control flow.
  • Before acting on a finding, verify it manually: read the actual line the agent cited. Does the code actually do what the agent says it does? Does the cited function exist with that signature?
  • Skeptic stance toward the reviewer: treat every finding as a hypothesis, not a fact. Confirm before you fix.

10.3 /security-review deep dive

The /security-review skill in Claude Code runs a security-focused analysis pass on your codebase or diff. It is distinct from code review: it does not comment on architecture or naming — it looks for attack surface.

What it scans

  • Injection patterns — SQL injection, shell injection (subprocess with untrusted input), template injection, path traversal.
  • Secret leakage — hardcoded credentials, tokens in source, secrets in log statements.
  • Insecure defaults — TLS disabled, signature verification skipped, debug flags left on.
  • Dangerous API usage — eval(), pickle.loads() on untrusted data, os.system() with interpolated input.
  • OWASP-adjacent patterns — missing authentication checks, direct object references, missing rate limiting.

What it misses (calibration required)

  • Business logic flaws — "user A can see user B's data" depends on your domain model, not the code shape. The scanner cannot reason about intent.
  • Auth boundary correctness — it can spot a missing @require_auth decorator but cannot verify your auth model is sound end-to-end.
  • Second-order effects — data flows across service boundaries, timing attacks, cache poisoning.
  • Configuration-layer vulnerabilities — IAM policies, firewall rules, Kubernetes RBAC. These live outside the code the scanner reads.

When and how to run

  • Run /security-review before every merge to a main branch.
  • It runs locally, in-terminal, synchronously. Results appear inline.

10.4 /ultrareview — multi-agent deep review

The /ultrareview skill is fundamentally different from /code-review or /security-review. Understanding these differences is required before using it.

Critical: /ultrareview is cloud-based, paid, and asynchronous

  • Cloud-based: your code is sent to remote agents running in Anthropic's infrastructure — not locally on your machine.
  • Metered and paid: each run consumes tokens across multiple agents in parallel. It is not free. Reserve it for high-value changes (pre-production releases, security-sensitive refactors, complex feature additions).
  • Asynchronous: results do not appear in your terminal. The review is dispatched; findings return to your inbox, not the CLI session you launched it from. You will not see output immediately.

What /ultrareview does

  • Spawns multiple parallel subagents with different review lenses — correctness, performance, security, architecture, test coverage — and synthesises their findings into a structured report.
  • Because multiple independent agents review the same code, it catches things a single-pass review misses: agents disagree, and disagreements surface ambiguities no single reviewer would flag.

When to use it

  • Before a production release or a release candidate branch merge.
  • When a significant refactor touches security boundaries (auth, payments, data access).
  • When you want a second opinion on a complex design decision before it gets load-bearing.

When not to use it

  • Routine feature work, documentation changes, small bug fixes — the cost and latency are not justified. Use /code-review instead.

10.5 Verification-before-completion — running the thing you built

The most common error: claiming done without running the thing

  • An agent can generate code that looks complete, passes a mental model check, and even passes static analysis — and still fail at runtime in the first smoke test.
  • "I've implemented the feature" is a claim about behaviour. Behaviour is only observable by running the code.

The superpowers:verification-before-completion skill encodes this as a non-negotiable gate. The pattern it enforces:

# Verification-before-completion pattern
# Do NOT claim done until ALL four steps pass:

1. BUILD   — the project compiles / the imports resolve / no syntax errors
             (run: python -c "import mymodule" or npm run build)

2. RUN     — the program starts and reaches its main loop without crashing
             (run: python main.py --help, or start the server, or execute the CLI)

3. SMOKE   — a minimal end-to-end path through the new feature produces
             the expected output with a real (or realistic) input
             (not mocked — actual I/O through the new code path)

4. CLAIM   — only after 1, 2, 3 pass can you write "done" or "implemented"

How to use the skill

  • Invoke with /verification-before-completion in Claude Code. It asks you to produce evidence for each stage. The agent cannot skip stages.
  • Especially important after refactoring — it's easy to rename a function, forget one call site, and produce code that passes code review but crashes on first use.
  • The evidence rule: every "done" claim must be backed by at least one piece of concrete evidence — a test run output, a screenshot of the feature working, a copy of the smoke test output. "It should work" is not evidence.

10.6 Hallucinations in code — APIs that don't exist, imports that fail

Code hallucination is qualitatively different from text hallucination. In a sentence, a hallucinated fact is detectable by cross-referencing. In code, a hallucinated API looks syntactically identical to a real one — the code is well-formed, passes linting, and reads naturally, until it is run.

Common hallucination patterns

  • Made-up library functions: pandas.read_sql_smart(), requests.get_cached(), os.path.resolve_relative() — functions that sound like they should exist but don't.
  • Wrong method signatures: calling a real function with arguments it doesn't accept, or in an order it doesn't expect.
  • Invented module paths: from django.contrib.auth.utils import check_token where check_token doesn't exist at that path.
  • Version mismatch: a real API that existed in an older version and was removed or renamed — the agent's training data included it.

The Read-then-Edit verification pattern

Before accepting any agent-generated code that uses an API you didn't write yourself, verify the API exists. The pattern:

# Read-then-Edit verification pattern
# Before: agent generates code using an API

# Step 1: READ — use the Read tool to check the actual source or docs
#   "Read the actual file at site-packages/requests/api.py and confirm
#    that get() accepts a `timeout` parameter and its default value."

# Step 2: CONFIRM — does the function/class/method exist at that path?
#   Does it accept the arguments the agent used?
#   Is it exported from the module the agent imported it from?

# Step 3: EDIT — only proceed with the generated code once confirmed.
#   If the API doesn't exist: ask the agent to find the correct one,
#   or use the real docs to substitute the correct call.

# Do NOT: assume "it looks plausible" means it works.
# Do NOT: run the code as the first verification step — read first.

For external libraries

  • Use the WebFetch tool to pull the actual documentation page, not the agent's memory of it.
  • The agent's training data has a cutoff; the library may have changed since then.

10.7 The "looks right vs is right" gap

Code can pass every check and still be wrong

  • Compiles without errors.
  • Passes a linter at zero warnings.
  • Passes a type checker (mypy, TypeScript, etc.).
  • Is syntactically and stylistically indistinguishable from correct code.
  • Passes a casual code review.

Where AI-assisted bugs live

  • The gap between "looks right" and "is right" is where most AI-assisted bugs live. They don't look like bugs — they look like working code with a subtle behavioural error: an off-by-one on an edge case, a missing await, a silent integer overflow, a dictionary lookup that returns None silently instead of raising.
  • The type-checks-pass, tests-fail case is the most dangerous variant: the type system confirms type consistency but cannot verify logic correctness. A function typed as (int, int) -> int that returns a * b instead of a + b passes every type check — only a test catches it.
  • This is why the verification discipline (§10.5) requires a smoke test, not just a build pass. And it's why TDD from Ch 8 is protective: writing the test before implementation codifies what "right" means before "looks right" code can be accepted.

Practical heuristic

  • If a code change is more than 20 lines and has no corresponding test change, the "is right" question is unanswered. Don't merge it without asking why.

10.8 Receiving code review gracefully

Receiving code review — whether from an agent or a human — is a skill. The default response to negative feedback is defensive: discount the finding, reframe the code so the finding no longer applies, or implement a surface-level change that satisfies the letter of the review without addressing the concern. All of these responses make code worse.

Core discipline (superpowers:receiving-code-review)

  1. Assume the reviewer is right until proven otherwise. Disagreement is a signal that something is unclear — either the code is unclear, or the reviewer misread it. Both outcomes need resolution.
  2. Verify findings before implementing. Performative agreement — implementing a change because the reviewer flagged it, without understanding whether the finding is correct — is dangerous. You can introduce a new bug while "fixing" a non-existent one.
  3. Ask for clarification before dismissing. A finding you don't understand is not a finding you can safely ignore. "I don't see the problem here" is the start of a conversation, not the end of one.
  4. Distinguish BLOCKER from MINOR. Not all findings are equal. A BLOCKER (correctness bug, security hole) must be resolved before merge. A MINOR (naming preference, style suggestion) can be deferred or declined with explanation.

Applies equally to agent reviewers

  • Don't implement every suggestion the agent makes without reading the finding. Agents make incorrect review comments.
  • Read the finding, check the code it references, and make your own judgment.

10.9 Self-review prompts — getting the agent to critique itself

Before requesting external review, you can get the agent to critique its own output. This catches a class of errors the agent is aware of but didn't surface during generation — it knows about edge cases it didn't handle, APIs it wasn't sure about, and assumptions it made.

High-value self-review prompts

# Prompt 1: find a bug
"Find a bug in the code you just wrote.
Be specific: give the line number and the exact failure condition."

# Prompt 2: list failure modes
"List the ways this code could produce wrong output.
Enumerate silently wrong cases — cases where no exception is raised
but the result is incorrect."

# Prompt 3: find the unhandled inputs
"What inputs does this code not handle?
List at least three cases where the input is valid but the code
will produce an error or incorrect result."

# Prompt 4: find the assumption
"What assumption does this code make that the caller might violate?
State the assumption explicitly and suggest how to guard against it."

# Prompt 5: find the missing test
"What test case is missing from the test suite that, if it existed,
would fail right now?"

Why specificity matters — and which to always run

  • Vague prompts get vague responses: "Is there anything wrong with this?" elicits reassurance. "Find a bug" forces the agent to search.
  • "What inputs does this not handle?" forces enumeration of failure modes rather than confirmation of success modes.
  • Run at least Prompt 2 and Prompt 3 on every non-trivial code generation before accepting the output.

10.10 Sign-off gates — what blocks a "done" claim

A "done" claim is a commitment. The gates below must pass — this is not a suggestion. Each gate catches a real class of defect that the previous gate does not catch.

Sign-off checklist

  • Build passes. npm run build / python -m compileall / go build exits clean. No import errors, no syntax errors.
  • Tests pass. Full test suite green. Not just the new tests — the full suite. No regressions.
  • Manual smoke. You or the agent ran a real end-to-end path through the new behaviour with actual input. Not mocked.
  • Security scan clean. /security-review run, findings reviewed. BLOCKERs resolved or explicitly accepted with documented rationale.
  • Code review complete. Findings reviewed. BLOCKERs and MAJORs resolved. MINORs triaged.
  • No TODOs in diff. git diff main | grep -i 'TODO\|FIXME\|HACK' is empty (or every hit is intentional and tracked).
  • Reviewer sign-off. A human (or an /ultrareview pass for high-stakes changes) has approved the change.

Gates are sequential, not parallel

  • If tests fail, the smoke test result is not meaningful — the code you smoked isn't the code that's shipping.
  • If the build doesn't pass, the tests don't reflect reality.

10.11 Mini-walkthrough: full review pass

Mini-walkthrough · gitlog-report — full review pass

Building on the Ch 9.11 walkthrough: the off-by-one bug in parser.py has been fixed (> → >=). The code is correct. Now we run the full three-layer review before declaring it ready to merge.

Review 1 · Inline code review (code-review skill)

Invoke: /code-review with the skeptic stance prompt. The agent reviews parser.py and the test suite.

Finding (MAJOR): missing input validation on the start_date parameter.

# Finding from code review
# File: parser.py, function: parse_gitlog()
# Issue: start_date parameter is used directly in datetime.strptime()
#        with no validation. If the caller passes "not-a-date" or None,
#        parse_gitlog() raises an unhandled ValueError with a cryptic
#        traceback, not a meaningful error.
#
# Recommendation: validate the date format at function entry.
# Add: if start_date and not re.match(r'\d{4}-\d{2}-\d{2}', start_date):
#          raise ValueError(f"start_date must be YYYY-MM-DD, got: {start_date!r}")
# Severity: MAJOR — affects all callers passing arbitrary input.

Action: fix applied. A ValueError with a clear message is now raised on malformed date input.

Review 2 · /security-review (local, synchronous, in-terminal)

Invoke: /security-review. Runs locally in the Claude Code session. Results appear in-terminal immediately.

Finding (BLOCKER): shell injection risk in the subprocess call.

# Finding from /security-review
# File: parser.py, line 23
# Issue: subprocess.run(f"git log {repo_path} --format=...", shell=True)
#        repo_path is passed in by the caller. If repo_path contains
#        shell metacharacters (e.g., "; rm -rf ~"), they execute with
#        the privileges of the calling process.
#
# Recommendation: pass arguments as a list, shell=False (the default).
# Before: subprocess.run(f"git log {repo_path} ...", shell=True)
# After:  subprocess.run(["git", "log", repo_path, "--format=..."])
# Severity: BLOCKER — shell injection with caller-controlled input.

Action: fix applied. shell=True removed; argument list used instead. This is a real vulnerability class — the code review pass did not flag it because code reviewers are not specifically scanning for shell metacharacter injection.

Review 3 · /ultrareview (cloud, async — results go to inbox)

Invoke: /ultrareview on the full diff.

Note on /ultrareview UX: this command dispatches the review to remote agents running in the cloud. It is metered and paid. After you press enter, the terminal will acknowledge dispatch — but you will not see results in this session. The findings return asynchronously to your inbox. Check your inbox after a few minutes for the structured report.

Finding (returned to inbox, MAJOR): architectural concern — tight coupling between parsing and date filtering.

# Finding from /ultrareview (received in inbox)
# Architectural concern — tight coupling
# The parse_gitlog() function both parses the raw git log format
# AND applies the date filter in a single pass. This makes it
# impossible to test parsing correctness independently from
# filtering correctness, and impossible to reuse the parser
# without also binding the caller to the filtering logic.
#
# Recommendation: separate concerns.
#   parse_gitlog(repo_path) → raw commit records (no date filter)
#   filter_by_date(commits, start_date, end_date) → filtered records
# This matches the single-responsibility principle and makes
# both functions independently testable and reusable.
# Severity: MAJOR — not a blocker for this diff, but a design debt
#           item to track before the codebase grows further.

Action: tracked as a design debt issue. The refactor is non-trivial and out of scope for this fix; it's logged for the next sprint, not merged in the current change.

Sign-off

All three reviews complete. BLOCKERs (shell injection) and MAJORs (missing validation) resolved. The architectural concern (tight coupling) is tracked but deferred. Build passes, full test suite green, smoke test confirms the fixed behaviour. Change is ready to merge.

Next chapter: we'll polish the reviewed code in parallel with multi-agent workflows. → Ch 11: Multi-Agent & Parallel.

← Ch 9. Debugging with Agents → Ch 11. Multi-Agent & Parallel → Ch 15. Superpowers (verification-before-completion) → Ch 17. Worked Example end-to-end

11. Multi-Agent & Parallel Workflows

What you'll learn: when one agent isn't enough, how to dispatch parallel subagents without conflicts, worktree isolation, the agent-of-agents orchestrator pattern, and how to polish gitlog-report in parallel.

11.1 When one agent isn't enough

A single agent working sequentially has two fundamental limits: context window saturation and wall-clock time. When a task has genuinely independent sub-problems, forcing one agent through them sequentially wastes both.

Three signals that you need more than one agent

  • Independent investigation. You need to research three unrelated APIs simultaneously. Each investigation has no dependency on the others. A single agent would context-switch, losing depth; parallel agents each work the full window on one problem.
  • Large context dilution. A 200-file codebase plus test suite plus documentation saturates the main session. Farming sub-problems to subagents preserves the orchestrator's context for integration work, not retrieval.
  • Specialized roles. One agent reads the spec, a second writes the implementation, a third reviews it. Each agent's prompt is tuned for one job. The reviewer agent receives no implementation bias — it read the spec first and forms its own expectations.

The coordination cost

  • Going parallel requires decomposing cleanly, managing worktrees or shared state explicitly, and integrating results.
  • If sub-problems are not truly independent, you pay coordination cost without the throughput benefit — worse than sequential.
  • Rule: only parallelize at clean dependency boundaries.

11.2 Subagents in Claude Code — the Agent tool

How the Agent tool works

  • Claude Code exposes an internal Agent tool the model can call to spawn subagents within the same session.
  • When the orchestrating agent issues multiple Agent tool calls in a single response, Claude Code dispatches them in parallel — each subagent gets its own context, its own tool access, and runs concurrently.
  • The Agent tool accepts a prompt parameter (what the subagent should do) and an optional description. The orchestrator receives each subagent's final output as a tool result and integrates them.

Subagent type identifiers

  • Claude Code also exposes named agent types that map to specialized behaviors. The exact identifiers and their casing are subject to Claude Code version changes — always verify against the current Claude Code documentation before scripting against them.
  • At time of writing, commonly referenced identifiers include:
  • general-purpose — default; reads, writes, runs commands
  • code-reviewer — review-focused, no writes
  • Explore — read-only codebase exploration
  • Plan — spec analysis and decomposition, no execution

Named types vs. unnamed dispatch

  • Verify exact identifiers and casing against current Claude Code docs before use — these may differ between Claude Code versions.
  • In practice, most parallel work dispatches unnamed subagents via the Agent tool with a well-scoped prompt.
  • Named types are useful when you want Claude Code to enforce role constraints (e.g., a review agent that cannot write files).

11.3 Dispatching parallel subagents — rules of thumb

Critical prerequisite

  • Parallel dispatch is only safe when the subagents' work is genuinely independent. Two agents editing the same file simultaneously produce a merge conflict at best and silent data corruption at worst.
  • When you issue a single message with multiple Agent tool calls, Claude Code runs them concurrently. Each subagent gets its own context window seeded with the prompt you provide — they do not share context and cannot "see" each other's in-progress work.

Rules of thumb for parallel dispatch

  • One file per agent. Two agents must not write to the same file in the same dispatch batch. If they need to touch overlapping files, sequence them.
  • Independent output paths. Each agent writes to a different directory, branch, or worktree. The orchestrator integrates after all agents complete.
  • Front-load context in prompts. Each subagent starts with zero memory of prior conversation. Include all necessary context in the dispatch prompt — do not assume shared state.
  • Limit fanout. More than 4–5 parallel agents is rarely beneficial and raises coordination cost sharply. Prefer 2–3 well-scoped agents over 8 narrowly-scoped ones.
  • Explicit integration step. After subagents complete, the orchestrator reads all outputs and synthesizes. This step is not free — budget tokens and turns for it.

Live example — this guide's own build

  • This is what happens in real-time when this guide is built: an orchestrator agent dispatches multiple subagents — one per chapter — in a single turn. Each agent writes its section independently. The orchestrator then integrates the outputs.

11.4 Worktrees for isolation — git worktree patterns

When parallel agents need to write code (not just read), shared file state is the primary failure mode. The solution is git worktrees: one checkout per agent, all pointing at the same underlying repo.

# Create three worktrees for three parallel agents
git worktree add ../gitlog-feat -b polish/json
git worktree add ../gitlog-tests -b polish/tests
git worktree add ../gitlog-docs -b polish/docs

How it works

  • Each agent is dispatched with an explicit working directory: Agent A works in ../gitlog-feat, Agent B in ../gitlog-tests, Agent C in ../gitlog-docs.
  • No agent ever touches another's tree. When all three complete, you review each branch independently, then merge.

Lifecycle

  1. Create worktrees before dispatching agents (git worktree add).
  2. Dispatch agents with their worktree path in the prompt.
  3. Agents commit to their branch independently.
  4. Orchestrator collects, reviews, and merges (or raises PRs).
  5. Clean up: git worktree remove ../gitlog-feat after merge.

Using the skill

  • The superpowers:using-git-worktrees skill automates this setup — handles worktree creation, branch naming, and teardown.
  • Invoke it before dispatching parallel agents on any non-trivial task to ensure clean isolation from the current workspace.

11.5 /loop — recurring tasks and polling

The /loop skill runs a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval, or lets the model self-pace between iterations. It is designed for polling external state — CI status, deploy health, rate-limited APIs — without you manually re-issuing the same command.

# Check CI every 5 minutes until green
/loop 5m "check if CI is passing on the current branch and report status"

# Self-paced loop — model decides when to run again based on output
/loop /babysit-prs

When to use /loop

  • Polling a deploy pipeline until it completes or fails.
  • Watching a test suite for new failures during active development.
  • Monitoring an external API endpoint for a state change you're waiting on.
  • Running a recurring quality check (lint, typecheck) at a fixed interval during a long coding session.

When not to use /loop

  • One-shot tasks — /loop is overhead for anything you only need once.
  • Avoid loops that generate large output per iteration — token consumption compounds quickly with recurring agents.

11.6 /schedule — cron-style remote agents

The /schedule skill creates, updates, and lists remote agent routines that execute on a cron schedule — they run independently of your local Claude Code session. You close your terminal; they keep running.

# Run a daily standup summary at 9am
/schedule "every day at 9am, summarize the last 24h of commits across all branches"

# One-time scheduled run
/schedule "run the security-review skill at 3pm today"

How it works under the hood

  • Schedules are registered routines that trigger a remote Claude Code agent at the specified time.
  • The ScheduleWakeup tool is the underlying mechanism — it registers a wakeup event that fires the agent on schedule. (Verify the exact tool name against current Claude Code docs — internal tool identifiers change between releases.)
  • Inspect and delete schedules via /schedule list and /schedule delete.

Use cases and constraints

  • Use cases: nightly security scans, automated PR summaries, daily dependency checks, recurring test runs on a staging branch, automated standup generation.
  • Constraints: scheduled agents run with the permissions and context available at schedule-creation time. They cannot interactively ask for clarification.
  • Design scheduled agent prompts to be self-contained and handle ambiguity gracefully (e.g., "if no new commits, output 'nothing to report' and stop").

11.7 Agent-of-agents — the orchestrator pattern

The orchestrator pattern is the highest-leverage multi-agent structure: one parent agent decomposes a problem, dispatches subagents in parallel to work independent sub-problems, then integrates their outputs into a unified result.

Signature pattern: four steps

  1. Decompose. Parent agent reads the task and identifies N independent sub-problems. Independence check: "Can sub-problem A be solved without knowing the output of sub-problem B?" If yes: parallel. If no: sequential.
  2. Dispatch. Parent agent issues N Agent tool calls in a single turn. Each subagent prompt includes: the sub-problem scope, all necessary context (file paths, relevant code, constraints), the output format expected, and what NOT to do (write to shared files, make network calls outside scope, etc.).
  3. Wait. Claude Code runs all N subagents concurrently. Parent agent is blocked until all complete.
  4. Integrate. Parent agent receives N results. It reconciles conflicts, fills gaps, resolves dependencies between sub-results, and produces the final artifact.
1
Decompose into independent tasks

Parent agent reads the task and identifies N sub-problems. Independence check: "Can sub-problem A be solved without knowing the output of sub-problem B?" If yes: parallel. If no: sequential. Only parallelize at clean dependency boundaries.

2
Dispatch subagents in parallel

Issue N Agent tool calls in a single turn. Each prompt includes: sub-problem scope, all necessary context (file paths, relevant code, constraints), expected output format, and explicit boundaries — what NOT to touch.

3
Each works in isolation (worktree)

Claude Code runs all N subagents concurrently. Each gets its own context window and (ideally) its own git worktree. Agents do not share context — they cannot see each other's in-progress work. Enforce one-file-per-agent.

4
Integrate results

Parent agent receives N outputs. It reconciles conflicts, fills gaps, resolves cross-sub-result dependencies, and produces the final artifact. Budget a full turn for this — integration is real work, not a no-op.

Orchestrator design principles

Orchestrator is the context holder. The orchestrator's session accumulates all sub-results. It must have enough context budget to receive and process N subagent outputs. If the sub-results are large, summarize in subagent prompts ("return a 200-word summary of your findings, not the full output").

Sub-problems must be expressible as self-contained prompts. If the decomposition requires inline real-time coordination between subagents (Agent A's output feeds Agent B's input), that is a sequential dependency, not parallel work.

Integration is work. Budget a full turn (sometimes several) for integration. The orchestrator reading 3–5 substantial subagent outputs and synthesizing them is not a no-op turn.

11.8 Kilo Code Orchestrator mode

Kilo Code (VS Code extension, Chapter 12) implements the orchestrator pattern in IDE-resident form via its Orchestrator mode. Rather than manually issuing Agent tool calls, you switch Kilo Code into Orchestrator mode and describe the high-level task — Kilo Code decomposes it, dispatches subagents (called "boomerang tasks" in Kilo Code's terminology), and integrates their results.

Multi-mode coordination in Kilo Code

  • Orchestrator mode — decomposes and delegates. Does not write code directly.
  • Code mode — the default subagent mode. Implements a specific task.
  • Architect mode — designs structure and produces specs, does not implement.
  • Ask mode — read-only question-answering. Never writes files.
  • Debug mode — specialized for root-cause analysis.

A typical orchestrated flow

  • Switch to Orchestrator mode → describe the feature → Orchestrator mode spawns an Architect subagent to write the spec, then two Code subagents (implementation + tests) in parallel, then a Debug subagent if tests fail.
  • The user interacts only with the Orchestrator; subagents are dispatched automatically.
  • Key advantage over manual Agent tool calls: Kilo Code tracks subagent state in a visible boomerang task panel, making it easier to see which subagents are in-flight, which completed, and which need attention.

11.9 When parallel hurts — shared state, races, context fragmentation

Multi-agent is not a universal speedup. Three failure modes are common enough that you need to recognize them before reaching for parallelism.

Shared state races

  • Two agents dispatched to "add error handling to the codebase" will both modify the same utility files — one agent's writes overwrite the other's.
  • Result: silent data loss or a broken repo. Prevention: enforce one-file-per-agent and use worktrees.

Context fragmentation

  • Each subagent has a partial view of the system. Agent A knows the API layer; Agent B knows the database layer. Neither knows both.
  • When Agent A makes an assumption about database behavior that contradicts Agent B's implementation, you get a coherent-looking but internally inconsistent result.
  • Prevention: the orchestrator must hold the full-system context and inject relevant cross-cutting constraints into every subagent prompt. Do not assume subagents will "figure out" what they don't know.

Coordination tax exceeding parallelism gain

  • For tasks that take 5 minutes sequentially: creating worktrees, crafting 3 well-scoped prompts, dispatching, integrating, and resolving conflicts may take longer.
  • Parallelism wins at scale — large tasks with clear decomposition boundaries. It loses on small tasks and tasks with implicit coupling.

Anti-patterns

  • Shotgun dispatch: spawning many agents without decomposing — "do the whole refactor in parallel." No clean boundaries → guaranteed conflicts.
  • Context-free subagents: prompts that say "fix the auth module" without providing the module path, relevant interfaces, or constraints. Subagent hallucinates context.
  • No integration plan: dispatch agents, collect outputs, declare done — without actually reading and reconciling the results. Integration is where most multi-agent value gets destroyed.
  • Parallel review of dependent code: dispatching a review agent on code that a concurrent implementation agent is still writing.

11.10 The token math of multi-agent

Multi-agent is not free — each subagent consumes tokens independently. The cost model:

Token cost formula

Total tokens = N × (C_prompt + C_work + C_output)
  + C_orchestrator

Where:
  N           = number of parallel subagents
  C_prompt    = tokens in each subagent's seeded context (your dispatch prompt
                + any injected file content)
  C_work      = tokens consumed by the subagent during its work turns
  C_output    = tokens in the subagent's final output returned to orchestrator
  C_orchestrator = tokens consumed by the orchestrator reading + integrating
                   all N outputs

Worked example: 3 subagents

You dispatch 3 subagents. Each receives a 4 000-token prompt, does 8 000 tokens of work, and returns a 2 000-token result. The orchestrator reads all three results (6 000 tokens) and produces a 3 000-token integration.

Subagent cost: 3 × (4 000 + 8 000 + 2 000) = 42 000 tokens
Orchestrator:  6 000 (input) + 3 000 (output) = 9 000 tokens
Total:                                          51 000 tokens

Sequential equivalent (same work, one agent):  ~28 000 tokens
Overhead:                                       +23 000 tokens (≈ 82 % more)

But wall-clock time: 3 × sequential → 1 × parallel (if tasks are truly independent)
Break-even: the 82% token overhead is justified if the work takes > ~15 min sequentially
            and you need the result in < 5 min.

When the overhead is worth paying

  • Multi-agent amortizes only when the tasks are genuinely parallel and the wall-clock savings matter.
  • For background tasks where you don't care about latency, sequential is almost always cheaper.
  • For interactive workflows where you need results fast, the token overhead is often worth paying.

11.11 Mini-walkthrough: gitlog-report polish pass in parallel

Mini-walkthrough · gitlog-report — polish pass in parallel

Starting point: the gitlog-report script from Ch 7–8, reviewed and bug-fixed in Ch 10.11. The core implementation is complete and passing review. This is a polish pass — we are not re-implementing anything. We are extending and documenting an already-reviewed codebase.

Goal: run three parallel agents to add three independent improvements simultaneously:

  • (a) Feature extension: add a --json output flag to gitlog-report
  • (b) Additional test coverage: edge cases — very large repo (10 000+ commits), unicode author names, repo with no history
  • (c) README + usage docs: write README.md and usage examples for the script

These three tasks touch different files (source, tests, docs) — a clean three-way independence boundary.

Step 1 — Invoke the git worktrees skill, then create three worktrees:

# Use the superpowers:using-git-worktrees skill to ensure clean isolation
# Then create the three branches:

git worktree add ../gitlog-feat   -b polish/json
git worktree add ../gitlog-tests  -b polish/tests
git worktree add ../gitlog-docs   -b polish/docs

Skill reference: superpowers:using-git-worktrees — automates this setup, handles branch naming, and ensures no shared state between worktrees.

Step 2 — Single dispatch message with three parallel Agent tool calls:

# Orchestrator prompt (single message, dispatches 3 agents in parallel):

"Dispatch 3 subagents in parallel:

Agent A — working in ../gitlog-feat (branch polish/json):
  Add a --json flag to gitlog-report. When passed, output the report as a
  JSON array of objects {author, commit_count, insertions, deletions} instead of
  the plain-text table. No other changes. Commit to polish/json.

Agent B — working in ../gitlog-tests (branch polish/tests):
  Add edge-case tests to the existing test suite for:
    (1) very large repo (mock 10 000+ commits),
    (2) author names containing unicode (e.g., '山田 太郎', 'Ångström, Erik'),
    (3) repo with zero commits (empty history).
  Do not modify the main script. Commit to polish/tests.

Agent C — working in ../gitlog-docs (branch polish/docs):
  Write README.md for gitlog-report covering: purpose, installation,
  usage examples (plain text, --json, --since, --author filters),
  output format description, and contributing notes.
  Do not modify the main script or tests. Commit to polish/docs."

Skill reference: superpowers:dispatching-parallel-agents — guides the orchestrator prompt structure, ensures each subagent gets full context, and handles the integration step.

Step 3 — Integration: 3 PRs → merge order → conflict resolution:

# After all three agents complete and commit:

# Raise three PRs
gh pr create --head polish/json   --base main --title "feat: add --json output flag"
gh pr create --head polish/tests  --base main --title "test: edge-case coverage"
gh pr create --head polish/docs   --base main --title "docs: README and usage"

# Merge order (safest to reduce conflicts):
# 1. docs (README only — no code changes, zero conflict risk)
# 2. tests (test files only — no conflict with docs)
# 3. feature (source change — review independently, then merge last)

# If polish/json conflicts with main after merges:
git checkout polish/json
git rebase main
# Resolve any conflicts, then:
git push --force-with-lease origin polish/json

What this walkthrough demonstrates:

  • Three parallel worktrees eliminate shared-state races by giving each agent an isolated filesystem.
  • The dispatch message is a single orchestrator turn — Claude Code runs all three agents concurrently.
  • Integration is explicit: three PRs, a reasoned merge order, and a conflict resolution path.
