  • The orchestrator (you) holds the full picture; each subagent had a scoped view.

Next: we ship the whole pipeline end-to-end in Ch 17. The gitlog-report built across Ch 7 (plan), Ch 8 (TDD), Ch 10 (review), and Ch 11 (parallel polish) becomes the worked example for the complete agentic development lifecycle.

← Ch 10. Safety, Review & Verification → Ch 13. Claude Code Deep Dive (sub-agents, /loop, /schedule) → Ch 15. Superpowers (dispatching-parallel-agents, using-git-worktrees) → Ch 17. Worked Example end-to-end

12. Kilo Code Deep Dive

What you'll learn: Kilo Code's architecture, the five modes and why they exist, BYO-model routing, custom rules, MCP integration, and how to configure Kilo for your daily workflow.

12.1 Architecture — VS Code extension, Cline/Roo lineage

Kilo Code is an open-source VS Code extension that embeds an AI coding agent directly into the IDE.

Origins and emphasis

  • Descends from the Cline (formerly Claude Dev) project; shares significant lineage with Roo Code — both are open-source forks of the same codebase that diverged in different directions.
  • Particular emphasis on multi-model routing, structured modes, and team configuration: explicitly designed for professional workflows where different tasks warrant different models and different capability surfaces.

How the extension is wired

  • Runs as a VS Code webview extension; on activation registers a sidebar panel, a set of VS Code commands, and a background Node.js process that manages model API calls.
  • Communicates with VS Code editor APIs to read files, run terminal commands, apply diffs, and interact with the broader workspace.
  • Unlike tools that operate purely through a chat interface, Kilo Code has bidirectional access: it can read the active editor selection, open new tabs, trigger tasks, and observe terminal output — all within the same session context.

Why open-source matters operationally

  • Configuration schemas, mode definitions, and routing rules are human-readable JSON and YAML files committed to your repository.
  • AI configuration is version-controlled alongside your code — team members get consistent behavior by checking out the same config.
  • Verify the extension's current GitHub repository for the latest architecture details, as the extension structure evolves across releases.

12.2 The five modes — Architect / Code / Ask / Debug / Orchestrator

Architect mode

  • For planning and high-level design — can read files and write specification documents, but constrained from making arbitrary code edits or running shell commands.
  • Use it to produce a technical spec, review architecture, or produce a structured plan for subsequent execution.
  • Typically routed to the most capable and expensive model — doing reasoning-heavy work where quality matters more than speed.

Code mode

  • The general coding workhorse — full file read/write access and terminal execution.
  • Default mode for implementation tasks: writing functions, fixing bugs, running tests.
  • Where most token spend occurs; routing a fast, capable model (e.g., Claude Sonnet) here balances quality and cost.

Ask mode

  • Strictly read-only — can read files and answer questions, but cannot write, edit, or execute.
  • Ideal for code Q&A, understanding an unfamiliar codebase, or checking your mental model of a system — tasks where you want an answer, not a change.
  • Because it cannot write, it cannot make mistakes that need reverting; a smaller, faster model works well here, and cost savings on high-volume Q&A sessions are significant.

Debug mode

  • Specialized for root-cause analysis — can read files, read terminal output, and run diagnostic commands (e.g., running tests to collect failure messages).
  • Prompting is tuned toward systematic hypothesis testing rather than broad implementation.
  • Reach for it when you have a specific failing test or error and want targeted diagnosis without rewriting half the module.

Orchestrator mode

  • The multi-agent coordinator — decomposes a high-level task, dispatches subagents in other modes (called "boomerang tasks" in Kilo Code's terminology), and integrates their results.
  • Does not write code directly — delegates to Code, Debug, or Architect subagents.
  • Covered in depth in Ch 11.8 and is the subject of the parallel workflows walkthrough.
Architect
Read-only + spec writes — plan and design without touching code.
Code
Full file read/write + terminal — the implementation workhorse.
Ask
Strictly read-only — Q&A and codebase exploration with zero write risk.
Debug
Read + diagnostics — targeted root-cause analysis without broad rewrites.
Orchestrator
Decomposes tasks and dispatches subagents — never writes code directly.

12.3 Why modes exist — constraint as feature

The instinct when encountering modes is to see them as restrictions. The correct mental model is the opposite: modes are footgun prevention.

Ask mode — the zero-write guarantee

  • When you ask a question in Ask mode, you have a guarantee — not a hope — that the AI cannot accidentally modify a file.
  • That guarantee changes how you interact: you can freely explore a live production codebase without a mental safety tax.
  • You do not need to mentally track whether this particular question might trigger a tool call that rewrites something.

Architect mode — keeping design in the design space

  • When doing architectural planning, you want the model to think broadly and boldly — proposing structure, questioning assumptions, suggesting refactors.
  • But you don't want it to start implementing that refactor mid-thought; Architect mode's write constraint keeps it in the design space.
  • The constraint is not a limitation — it enforces the conceptual boundary between designing and building, which is a real boundary that matters for quality.

Mode-specific prompting — more than tool gating

  • Modes shape the system prompt the model receives, not just what tools are available.
  • Architect mode primes the model for structure, tradeoffs, and specifications; Code mode primes it for implementation correctness and test coverage; Debug mode primes it for evidence-gathering and hypothesis testing.
  • The combination of constrained tools and mode-specific prompting means mode switching is a meaningful state transition, not just a label change.

12.4 Picking a model per mode — BYO providers

Bring Your Own model architecture

  • Configure any combination of supported providers — Anthropic, OpenAI, Google Gemini, AWS Bedrock, Azure OpenAI, OpenRouter, and local models via Ollama.
  • Route different modes to different models; this is where significant cost optimization lives: frontier model for Architect, fast capable model for Code, cheap/fast model for Ask.

Mode-to-model routing is configured in Kilo Code's custom modes file. Verify the exact file path and schema against the current Kilo Code documentation — historically this has been .kilocode/modes.json or a .roomodes file at the workspace root, and the schema evolves across versions. A representative configuration at the time of writing:

Example: per-mode model routing (.kilocode/modes.json — verify path & schema against current docs)

{
  "modes": {
    "architect": {
      "model": "claude-opus-4-7",
      "provider": "anthropic",
      "maxTokens": 8192
    },
    "code": {
      "model": "claude-sonnet-4-7",
      "provider": "anthropic",
      "maxTokens": 8192
    },
    "ask": {
      "model": "gemini-3-flash",
      "provider": "google",
      "maxTokens": 4096
    },
    "debug": {
      "model": "claude-sonnet-4-7",
      "provider": "anthropic",
      "maxTokens": 8192
    },
    "orchestrator": {
      "model": "claude-opus-4-7",
      "provider": "anthropic",
      "maxTokens": 16384
    }
  }
}

Schema details (field names, nesting, provider identifiers) change across Kilo Code versions. Always check the official docs before committing this config.

Credential split — routing in VCS, keys in secure store

  • Provider-level API keys are stored in VS Code's secure credential store (not in the config file), so the committed modes.json is safe to check in.
  • Each team member provides their own keys via the Kilo Code settings panel; the routing logic itself is shared configuration.
  • This split — shared routing in version control, per-person credentials in secure storage — is the right pattern for team environments.

12.5 Custom modes — defining your own

What custom modes let you do

  • Beyond the five built-in modes, define custom modes with their own name, system prompt, tool permissions, and model assignment.
  • A custom "Reviewer" mode, for example, might have read-only file access (like Ask), a system prompt tuned for finding bugs and suggesting improvements, and route to a different model than the default Ask config.
  • Custom modes are defined in the same modes config file as built-in mode overrides.

Example: custom "Reviewer" mode (verify schema against current Kilo Code docs)

{
  "customModes": [
    {
      "slug": "reviewer",
      "name": "Reviewer",
      "model": "claude-opus-4-7",
      "provider": "anthropic",
      "roleDefinition": "You are a senior code reviewer. Your job is to identify bugs, security issues, performance problems, and design violations in the provided code. You do NOT write code or suggest rewrites — you identify and explain problems only.",
      "groups": ["read"],
      "toolPermissions": {
        "readFile": true,
        "writeFile": false,
        "executeCommand": false,
        "browser": false
      }
    }
  ]
}

Key config fields

  • The groups field controls which tool categories are available — common groups include read (file reads), edit (file writes), command (terminal execution), and browser (web access).
  • The roleDefinition string becomes the mode's system prompt.
  • You can define as many custom modes as your workflow needs: a "SecurityAuditor" mode with no writes, a "Documenter" mode that writes only Markdown, a "Migrator" mode with access to your migration toolchain.
  • Verify the groups values and toolPermissions schema against the current Kilo Code documentation — these identifiers may differ between versions.

Onboarding use case

  • Custom modes are particularly powerful for onboarding: a "NewbieHelper" mode with an explanatory system prompt and no write access gives junior developers a safe exploration surface without the risk of accidental modifications.
  • The mode definition lives in version control, so the whole team benefits automatically.

12.6 Custom rules — per-project and global

Kilo Code supports two levels of custom rules: per-project rules and global rules.

Two scopes

  • Per-project rules live inside the repository — historically in a .kilocode/rules/ directory (verify the exact path against current docs, as it may also be .clinerules or similar).
  • Global rules live in the user's home configuration directory and apply to every Kilo Code session regardless of which project is open.

Per-project rules — what belongs here

  • Coding conventions (naming, formatting, file organization).
  • Framework-specific patterns (e.g., "always use the Result type for error handling, never throw").
  • Testing requirements ("every public function must have a corresponding unit test").
  • Security invariants ("never log PII, never hardcode credentials").
  • Because they live in the repository, they're automatically applied for every team member — when a new developer joins, they get the same AI behavior everyone else has, with no manual configuration step needed.

Example per-project rule files (.kilocode/rules/ — verify path against current docs)

.kilocode/rules/
  coding-style.md      # Naming conventions, file structure, import order
  testing.md           # Unit test requirements, coverage expectations
  security.md          # No PII logging, no hardcoded secrets, SQL parameterization
  git-conventions.md   # Commit message format, branch naming, PR size limits

Global rules — what belongs here

  • Your personal preferences across all projects: preferred response style, verbosity level, how you like diffs presented.
  • Authentication or workspace patterns that are consistent across your work.
  • Developer identity information (e.g., "My name is Alice; sign all generated commit messages accordingly").
  • Global rules are not committed to any repository — they live in your user profile and apply universally; team-shared conventions stay in the repo, personal preferences stay personal.

Evaluation and precedence

  • Rules are evaluated at the start of each task in that mode; Kilo Code injects the applicable rules into the system prompt as additional context.
  • Precedence when both global and per-project rules are present: per-project rules take priority for any conflict, with global rules filling in the rest.
  • Verify the exact rule file format, directory structure, and precedence behavior against current Kilo Code documentation.

12.7 Settings deep dive — auto-approve, terminal, MCP, telemetry

Auto-approve toggles

  • Settings panel accessible via the Kilo Code sidebar gear icon or VS Code settings under kilocode.*; provides fine-grained auto-approval controls per tool category.
  • Configurable options: auto-approve file reads always; auto-approve file writes only within specific directories (e.g., src/ but not .env or secrets/); require approval for all terminal commands; auto-approve terminal commands that match a specific allowlist of safe patterns.
  • Key insight: auto-approve is not binary — directory-scoped write approval and command-specific approval gives you automation speed for low-risk operations while retaining human gates for high-risk ones.
  • Exact settings keys and UI location should be verified against the current Kilo Code release notes, as these have moved between versions.

Terminal integration

  • Kilo Code runs commands in a VS Code integrated terminal, observes their output, and uses that output to inform subsequent decisions.
  • Settings control: which shell is used (bash, zsh, fish, or the VS Code default), whether terminal sessions are reused across tasks or fresh per-task, and the timeout for long-running commands.
  • For workflows with verbose build logs: configure a maximum output length to prevent runaway context consumption.

MCP server registration

  • MCP servers are registered in Kilo Code's settings as a JSON array under the MCP configuration section (see 12.9 for a full example).
  • Settings also control whether MCP tools are auto-approved per server or require manual confirmation per-call — useful when a server exposes a mix of safe read operations and destructive write operations.

Telemetry

  • By default Kilo Code collects usage statistics (mode switches, model selections, task durations) to improve the product.
  • Can be disabled entirely via the kilocode.telemetry.enabled setting (verify the exact key name).
  • For enterprise environments where data governance matters, telemetry should be reviewed and disabled if required by policy.
  • Kilo Code's open-source codebase means you can audit exactly what telemetry is collected before enabling it — a significant advantage over closed-source tools.

12.8 Memory and Context Bank

By default, Kilo Code does not persist memory across sessions — each new task starts fresh, with no knowledge of prior conversations or decisions.

What the Context Bank is

  • The Context Bank (also referenced as Memory Bank in some Kilo Code versions — verify current terminology) is the mechanism for explicitly persisting information across sessions.
  • Works by writing structured files to a designated location in your project (historically .kilocode/memory/ or memory-bank/ at the project root) that Kilo Code reads at the start of each new session.

Standard file set

  • projectbrief.md — high-level project description and goals.
  • activecontext.md — current task state, recent decisions, open questions.
  • progress.md — what's done, what's in progress, what's blocked.
  • systempatterns.md — architectural decisions and conventions.
  • Kilo Code agents in supported modes read these files before beginning work, so they arrive with project context rather than starting blind; when significant decisions are made during a session, the agent can update these files so the next session inherits that knowledge.

When to use it

  • For long-lived projects, the Context Bank is the difference between an AI that needs re-orientation every session and one that operates with continuity.
  • Maintaining it is low-overhead — the agent can update it as part of its end-of-task cleanup.
  • For one-off tasks or short projects, the overhead is not worth the setup cost; it pays off on projects spanning multiple sessions over days or weeks.
  • Verify the exact file names, directory location, and update mechanisms against current Kilo Code documentation, as this feature has evolved significantly across versions.

12.9 MCP integration in Kilo

Registration

  • MCP integration follows the same standard described in Ch 16, but the registration mechanism is specific to Kilo Code's settings.
  • MCP servers are registered via the Kilo Code settings panel or directly in the VS Code settings.json.
  • Once registered, an MCP server's tools become available to modes that have the mcp tool group enabled.
  • Verify the exact settings structure against current Kilo Code docs.

Example: registering an MCP server in Kilo Code settings (verify schema against current docs)

// In VS Code settings.json (or Kilo Code's MCP config section):
{
  "kilocode.mcpServers": {
    "filesystem": {
      "command": "npx",
      "args": ["-y", "@modelcontextprotocol/server-filesystem", "/workspace"],
      "env": {}
    },
    "github": {
      "command": "npx",
      "args": ["-y", "@modelcontextprotocol/server-github"],
      "env": {
        "GITHUB_TOKEN": "${env:GITHUB_TOKEN}"
      }
    },
    "postgres": {
      "command": "npx",
      "args": ["-y", "@modelcontextprotocol/server-postgres"],
      "env": {
        "DATABASE_URL": "${env:DATABASE_URL}"
      }
    }
  }
}

Runtime behavior and cost

  • Each registered MCP server starts as a subprocess when Kilo Code loads and remains running for the duration of the VS Code session.
  • The server's exposed tools appear in the Kilo Code tool list and can be called from any mode with MCP access enabled.
  • Configurable per-server auto-approval: for a read-only filesystem server you might auto-approve all calls; for a server that writes to a database, require per-call confirmation.
  • Tool calls to MCP servers count against your context window just like any other tool call — large responses from database or filesystem servers can consume substantial tokens, so scoping queries carefully matters for cost management.

Mode-scoped MCP access

  • MCP servers can be scoped to specific modes via mode tool permissions — e.g., a "DatabaseDebug" custom mode might have access to the Postgres MCP server while Code mode does not, preventing accidental database modifications during routine coding.
  • This is the composability advantage of the modes-plus-MCP architecture: capability access is defined declaratively per mode, not negotiated per-task.

12.10 Commit message and PR features

Automated commit message generation

  • After a Code mode task that modifies files, Kilo Code can inspect the diff and generate a structured commit message following conventional commit format (feat, fix, docs, refactor, etc.).
  • Quality depends on the model used in Code mode — more capable models produce more accurate and contextually appropriate messages.
  • Configure commit message templates in per-project rules to enforce a specific format (e.g., requiring issue numbers, ticket IDs, or specific scoping).

PR description generation

  • Kilo Code can analyze a branch diff versus the base branch, read the associated commits, and produce a structured PR description covering change summary, motivation, testing notes, and reviewer checklist.
  • Integrates with VS Code's GitHub extension to pre-populate the PR creation dialog.
  • For teams with PR description standards, encoding them as per-project rules ensures Kilo Code generates descriptions that match the template.
  • Exact UI locations and commands for commit message and PR generation vary between Kilo Code versions — verify the current interface in the extension's changelog or documentation before building team workflows around specific UI interactions.

12.11 Cost dashboard and tracking

Per-task cost visibility

  • Kilo Code maintains a per-session cost tracker — the sidebar panel shows token consumption and estimated dollar cost after each task completes, broken down by input tokens, output tokens, cache hits (if the provider supports prompt caching), and total cost at the model's current pricing.
  • Makes cost visible at the task level rather than as a surprise on the monthly bill.

Identifying cost hotspots

  • Orchestrator mode tasks tend to have high orchestrator-turn costs because they read all subagent outputs into a single context.
  • Ask mode Q&A sessions are frequently the highest-volume but should be low-cost if routed to a fast/cheap model — this is the main reason to use a cheaper model in Ask mode.
  • Large file reads in Code mode (e.g., reading a 3000-line file to make a 5-line change) are a common optimization target.
  • If a mode consistently consumes more than expected: check whether rules files inject large content, whether MCP server responses are verbose, or whether the mode's system prompt is disproportionately long.

Budget caps and export

  • Supports per-provider budget limits — set a daily or per-task token cap that triggers a warning or hard stop before exceeding the threshold.
  • Verify the exact budget/cap configuration path and behavior against current Kilo Code documentation, as this feature has evolved across versions.
  • For team environments, cost tracking data is exportable for allocation and chargeback purposes.

12.12 Working with multi-repo and monorepo setups

Multi-root workspace rules loading

  • VS Code supports multi-root workspaces (a .code-workspace file that references multiple folders), and Kilo Code respects this structure.
  • Kilo Code loads per-project rules from each workspace folder independently — the rules in packages/api/.kilocode/rules/ apply when the agent is working in the API package; rules in packages/frontend/.kilocode/rules/ apply in the frontend package.
  • Enables per-package conventions in a monorepo without a single unwieldy global rules file.

Context Bank placement in monorepos

  • A project-level Context Bank at the workspace root captures cross-package architectural decisions; package-level context captures package-specific state.
  • Recommended structure: .kilocode/memory/projectbrief.md at the root describes the overall system; individual packages maintain their own activecontext.md if in active development.
  • Orchestrator mode can read both levels to maintain a coherent cross-package view when dispatching subagents to different packages.

Cross-repo sessions

  • Use a VS Code workspace file that references all related repositories; Kilo Code operates across all of them in a single session.
  • Code mode can read both repositories' codebases and write to both — a capability advantage over CLI-based tools that are inherently single-repo-scoped.
  • Verify multi-root workspace behavior against current Kilo Code documentation — file access and rules resolution across workspace roots may have version-specific edge cases.

12.13 Power-user shortcuts

Mode quick-switch

  • Kilo Code registers a VS Code command for switching modes directly from the keyboard — default keybinding may be Ctrl+Shift+M / Cmd+Shift+M or accessible via the command palette as "Kilo Code: Switch Mode" (verify in current docs).
  • Assigning a dedicated keybinding via VS Code's keyboard shortcuts editor (keybindings.json) makes the Architect→Code→Ask cycle a muscle memory operation.

At-mentions and context injection

  • @ prefixes in the chat input inject context directly: @file:path/to/file.ts injects a file's contents, @folder:src/ injects a directory listing, @git:diff injects the current git diff, @problems injects the VS Code Problems panel contents (compile errors, linter warnings).
  • Combining these in a single message — "@file:src/auth.ts @problems fix the TypeScript error on line 42" — gives the model precise context without filesystem exploration, dramatically reducing tool call turns and associated latency.

Task continuity and resume

  • Kilo Code tracks task history in the sidebar panel; click a previous task to resume it — re-loading the task's context and continuing from where it left off.
  • Useful for long-running tasks interrupted mid-session; respects the Context Bank if enabled, merging session state with persisted memory for a coherent continuation.
  • Specific shortcut keys, @-mention syntax details, and resume behavior should be verified against the current Kilo Code documentation as these have changed between major versions.

12.14 Hands-on: configure Kilo Code for your daily workflow

This walkthrough takes you from a fresh Kilo Code install to a fully configured daily driver. Follow each step sequentially; steps 4–6 can be done in any order once step 3 is complete.

Walkthrough · Configure Kilo Code for your daily workflow

  1. Install the extension. Open VS Code, go to Extensions (Ctrl+Shift+X / Cmd+Shift+X), search "Kilo Code," install the official extension by Kilo Code (verify the publisher name in the VS Code Marketplace — there are similarly-named extensions). After install, the Kilo Code sidebar icon appears in the activity bar. Click it to open the main panel.
  2. Add your API keys. Click the Settings icon (gear) in the Kilo Code panel. Under "API Providers," add keys for the providers you plan to use. At minimum, add your Anthropic API key for Claude models. If you plan to use Google Gemini for Ask mode (as in the 12.4 example), add your Google AI Studio API key. Keys are stored in VS Code's credential store — they are not written to any file in your workspace.
  3. Create the Kilo Code config directory in your project. 
    mkdir -p .kilocode/rules
touch .kilocode/rules/coding-style.md
touch .kilocode/rules/testing.md
touch .kilocode/rules/security.md
    Populate each rules file with your project's conventions. Start minimal — 5–10 bullet points per file. Rules files are injected into every relevant task's context, so bloated rules files waste tokens on every request.
  4. Set up mode-to-model routing. Create .kilocode/modes.json (verify the exact filename against current docs). Use the template from 12.4 as a starting point, substituting your preferred models per mode. Commit this file to your repository — teammates will inherit your routing config automatically.
  5. Enable the Context Bank. Create the memory directory: 
    mkdir -p .kilocode/memory
touch .kilocode/memory/projectbrief.md
touch .kilocode/memory/activecontext.md
touch .kilocode/memory/progress.md
touch .kilocode/memory/systempatterns.md
    Write a 2–3 paragraph projectbrief.md describing what your project is, its tech stack, and key constraints. The other files start empty and grow as sessions proceed. Verify the exact directory name and file set against current Kilo Code documentation.
  6. Register an MCP server (optional but recommended). If you use GitHub, add the GitHub MCP server per the 12.9 example. If you have a local database, add the appropriate database MCP server. Restart VS Code after editing the MCP config to pick up the new server registration.
  7. Configure auto-approve for your risk tolerance. In Kilo Code settings, enable auto-approve for file reads and writes within src/ and tests/. Require approval for terminal commands. This gives you speed on the low-risk operations you'll accept hundreds of per day, and a gate on the higher-risk ones.
  8. Commit the config. 
    git add .kilocode/
git commit -m "chore: add Kilo Code project config (modes, rules, memory scaffold)"
    From this point, the project has a version-controlled AI configuration. Any teammate cloning the repo gets the same modes, rules, and memory structure. They provide only their own API keys.

Daily usage pattern:

  • Morning: Switch to Ask mode. Review overnight CI failures, read relevant code sections, understand the day's starting state. No accidental writes.
  • Planning: Switch to Architect mode. Describe the feature or refactor for the day. Get a structured plan and spec document.
  • Implementation: Switch to Code mode. Work through the spec produced by Architect. Auto-approve writes within src/.
  • Debugging: If tests fail, switch to Debug mode. Provide the error output and let Debug mode run targeted diagnostics without rewriting unrelated code.
  • End of day: Ask Code mode to update .kilocode/memory/activecontext.md and progress.md with today's decisions and current state. Tomorrow's session starts with context.

This pattern eliminates context re-establishment overhead. The most expensive part of an AI coding workflow is repeatedly explaining the same background context. The Context Bank, per-project rules, and consistent mode routing solve this at the infrastructure level — you invest in the config once and amortize it across every subsequent session.

← Ch 5. IDE vs Terminal ← Ch 4. Model Selection & Cost → Ch 13. Claude Code Deep Dive → Ch 15. Superpowers (Kilo Code modes/rules adaptation) → Ch 16. MCP Servers

13. Claude Code Deep Dive

What you'll learn: Claude Code's architecture, CLAUDE.md and memory, slash commands, skills, plugins, hooks, sub-agents, plan mode, output styles, fast mode, scheduling primitives, settings, and how to configure Claude Code for your daily workflow.

13.1 Architecture — terminal, headless, IDE extensions

Claude Code is Anthropic's official CLI for Claude — a Node.js process that runs in your terminal and gives Claude bidirectional access to your filesystem, shell, and development environment.

Surface-agnostic by default

  • Runs anywhere a terminal runs: local laptops, remote SSH sessions, CI containers, Docker environments — no VS Code dependency.
  • Interaction is through a REPL-style prompt that accepts natural language requests, slash commands, and multi-turn conversations.

Headless mode

  • Allows Claude Code to be driven programmatically — via shell scripts, CI pipelines, or other orchestration systems — without a human at the keyboard.
  • Reads instructions from stdin or command-line flags, executes the task, and exits; a first-class participant in automated workflows (GitHub Actions step, pre-commit hook, cron job).
  • The --output-format json flag (or equivalent; verify the current flag name in claude --help) produces machine-parseable results.

IDE extensions

  • Available for VS Code and JetBrains IDEs; embed the Claude Code REPL inside the IDE's sidebar or panel with direct access to the open editor context: the active file, selected text, diagnostic errors, and diff views.
  • The underlying agent is identical — the extensions are thin shells that pass editor context into the same CLI runtime.
  • Verify which IDE extension versions are currently shipping and stable, as the JetBrains extension in particular has lagged behind the VS Code release cadence historically.
CLAUDE.md
Instruction injection at global, project, and subdirectory scope.
Slash commands
In-session control surface — built-in and custom per-project commands.
Skills
Named, reusable workflow bundles invoked via the Skill tool or /command.
Plugins
Distribution bundles that package skills, commands, and hooks together.
Hooks
Shell scripts wired to lifecycle events — SessionStart, PreToolUse, Stop.
Sub-agents
Isolated parallel agents spawned via the Agent tool for concurrent tasks.
Plan mode
Approval-gate that separates reasoning from execution before files change.
Memory
CLAUDE.md files + hooks persist context and preferences across sessions.

13.2 CLAUDE.md — global, project, subdirectory scope

CLAUDE.md is Claude Code's instruction-injection mechanism. Unlike a system prompt passed at invocation time, CLAUDE.md files are discovered automatically based on where you are in the filesystem. Claude Code reads them at session start; there are three distinct scopes, all active simultaneously when they exist.

Global scope (~/.claude/CLAUDE.md)

  • Applies to every Claude Code session on this machine, regardless of project.
  • Right place for personal preferences spanning all projects: preferred language style, response format preferences, always-on constraints ("never commit with --no-verify"), personal skill registrations, and identity context the agent should always have.
  • Think of it as your personal system prompt layer — changes here affect every project immediately; treat it like a dotfile.

Project root scope (<project>/CLAUDE.md)

  • Applies when Claude Code is run inside that project directory or any subdirectory.
  • Right place for project-specific guidance: tech stack, architectural constraints, testing conventions, CI pipeline details, code style rules specific to this repo, and codebase context the agent should always have loaded.
  • Committing this file to the repository ensures every teammate gets the same agent behavior — the most important CLAUDE.md for team workflows.

Subdirectory scope (<project>/src/payments/CLAUDE.md)

  • Applies only when Claude Code's working context is within that subdirectory.
  • Use for modules with unusual conventions, security-sensitive areas needing extra constraints ("never log PII in this module"), or subsystems where a domain-specific glossary helps.
  • Subdirectory CLAUDE.md files stack on top of the project root and global files — all three merge into the active instruction set; particularly useful in monorepos where different packages have different conventions.
Claude Code vs Kilo Code — memory and instruction injection: Claude Code uses CLAUDE.md files at three scope levels (global ~/.claude/, project root, subdirectory). Kilo Code uses .kilocode/rules/ — a directory of Markdown rule files that are loaded per-mode or globally. Both approaches store instructions as plain text files that are version-controlled alongside the project. The key difference: Kilo Code's rules system supports per-mode targeting (a rule file can apply only in Code mode, not in Ask mode), while Claude Code's CLAUDE.md is always-active within its scope. If you need per-capability gating, Kilo Code's rules model is more expressive; if you want a single coherent instruction document per scope level, CLAUDE.md's simplicity wins.

13.3 Slash commands — built-in and custom

Slash commands are Claude Code's in-session control surface. Type / at the prompt to see the full list.

Built-in commands

  • /clear — reset the conversation context to an empty state.
  • /compact — summarize the current conversation and replace the history with a compact representation, reducing token usage while preserving essential state.
  • /init — scaffold a new CLAUDE.md for the current project by analyzing the codebase.
  • /review — trigger a code review skill on the current diff or specified files.
  • /security-review — run a security-focused review.
  • /config — open settings; /help — list all available commands.
  • Other notable built-ins: /ultrareview (deep review with multiple passes, if installed as a skill), /schedule (create or manage scheduled remote agents), /loop (run a command on a dynamic interval), /plugin (plugin management).
  • Several slash commands are thin wrappers around skills — invoking /review triggers the code-review skill via the Skill tool internally.
  • The full list evolves across releases; /help is the canonical source.

Custom commands

  • Live in ~/.claude/commands/ (global, available in all sessions) or <project>/.claude/commands/ (project-scoped).
  • Each custom command is a Markdown file whose filename becomes the command name: ~/.claude/commands/standup.md becomes /standup.
  • The file body is a prompt template that Claude executes when the command is invoked; reference $ARGUMENTS in the template to pass positional arguments.
  • The fastest way to codify recurring workflows — daily standup generation, PR description drafting, changelog updates — into a single keyable action.

13.4 Skills — what they are, how they activate

What a skill is

  • Reusable capability units — structured prompting packages that encode a complex, multi-step workflow into a named, invocable artifact.
  • Lives in ~/.claude/skills/<name>/SKILL.md; the file has two parts: a YAML frontmatter block declaring metadata, and a Markdown body containing the actual instructions Claude follows when the skill executes.

A representative SKILL.md structure:

SKILL.md structure (~/.claude/skills/my-skill/SKILL.md)

---
name: my-skill
description: "One-line description shown in skill list"
version: 1.0
author: your-name
tags: [review, quality]
---

# My Skill

When this skill is invoked, do the following:

1. Read the current git diff with `git diff HEAD`.
2. Analyze each changed file for ...
3. Produce a structured report in the following format: ...

Activation mechanisms

  • Discoverable as slash commands: if ~/.claude/skills/code-review/SKILL.md exists, type /code-review and Claude will invoke it.
  • Invokable programmatically via the Skill tool — other skills or orchestrating agents can call Skill({ skill: "code-review", args: "..." }) to chain skill executions.
  • Auto-discovery: skills registered in ~/.claude/skills/ appear automatically in /help output and in the skill list shown in the system reminder — dropping a valid SKILL.md file into the directory is sufficient for activation, no separate registration needed.

13.5 Plugins and the plugin marketplace

What plugins are

  • Distribution bundles that package multiple skills, custom commands, and hooks into a single installable unit.
  • Where skills are individual capabilities, a plugin is a coherent capability set — e.g., a "Jira workflow" plugin might include an Atlassian MCP integration, a /triage-issue skill, a /generate-status-report skill, and a hook that auto-attaches Jira context at session start.

Installing from the marketplace

  • Two-step process: first use /plugin marketplace add <marketplace> (a marketplace source — typically a GitHub repo such as obra/superpowers-marketplace) to register the marketplace; then run /plugin (the interactive plugin management flow) to browse, install, configure, and activate plugins from it — or install one directly with /plugin install <name>@<marketplace>.
  • Installed plugins are tracked in your Claude Code settings; their components (skills, commands, hooks) are registered automatically.
  • Verify this flow against current Claude Code documentation — the plugin system is evolving and the exact command sequence may differ in newer releases.

Local and team distribution

  • Install plugins from local paths or git URLs for custom or private plugins.
  • Plugin manifest defines what gets installed and where: skills land in ~/.claude/skills/, commands in ~/.claude/commands/, hooks are merged into ~/.claude/settings.json.
  • For teams, packaging your shared Claude Code configuration as a plugin and hosting it on an internal registry gives everyone consistent capabilities with a single install step.

13.6 Hooks — lifecycle and use cases

Hooks are shell commands or scripts that Claude Code executes at defined points in the session lifecycle. Configured in .claude/settings.json (project-level) or ~/.claude/settings.json (global).

Lifecycle events

  • SessionStart — fires when a new Claude Code session begins.
  • PreToolUse — fires before any tool call, with the tool name and parameters available as environment variables.
  • PostToolUse — fires after a tool call completes.
  • Stop — fires when the session ends or /clear is run.

Use cases

  • Inject dynamic context at session start (e.g., running git status and appending its output to the session).
  • Enforce constraints before tool use (e.g., blocking Bash calls that contain rm -rf /).
  • Log all tool calls to an audit file.
  • Send a Slack notification when a long-running task completes.
  • Update a MEMORY.md file after each session ends.
  • Hooks execute with the same permissions as the Claude Code process — they can read/write files, call APIs, and run arbitrary shell commands.

Example hooks configuration in .claude/settings.json

{
  "hooks": {
    "SessionStart": [
      {
        "command": "git log --oneline -5 2>/dev/null || true",
        "description": "Show recent commits at session start"
      }
    ],
    "PostToolUse": [
      {
        "matcher": { "tool": "Bash" },
        "command": "echo \"[$(date -u +%H:%M:%S)] Bash call completed\" >> ~/.claude/audit.log",
        "description": "Audit log for Bash tool use"
      }
    ],
    "Stop": [
      {
        "command": "~/.claude/scripts/update-session-notes.sh",
        "description": "Update session notes on exit"
      }
    ]
  }
}

Verify the exact hook event names and matcher syntax against current Claude Code docs — the hooks schema has evolved across versions and matcher fields differ per event.

13.7 Sub-agents — the Agent tool

How sub-agents work

  • The Agent tool allows the orchestrating Claude instance to spawn specialized sub-agents as separate, isolated sessions.
  • Each sub-agent has its own context window, its own tool access, and executes a specific task in parallel or sequentially; the orchestrator receives the sub-agent's output when it completes.
  • The mechanism behind parallel code review, concurrent test generation, and any pattern where you want multiple specialized perspectives simultaneously.

Sub-agent types and lifecycle tools

  • Sub-agent types at time of writing include: general-purpose agents, exploration-focused agents optimized for read-heavy codebase analysis, planning agents focused on structured decomposition, and code-reviewer agents tuned for quality analysis.
  • The TaskCreate, TaskGet, TaskList, and TaskStop tools provide programmatic sub-agent lifecycle management within skills and hooks.
  • Exact identifiers, names, and capability sets of sub-agent types are version-specific — always verify against current Claude Code documentation before relying on a specific type name in production workflows.

Task isolation principle

  • Each sub-agent should receive a self-contained task brief with all the context it needs embedded in its initial prompt.
  • Sub-agents do not share memory with the orchestrator or with each other — any shared state must be passed explicitly through the task description and result.
  • For large parallel workloads (e.g., reviewing 20 files concurrently), this isolation is a feature: sub-agents cannot interfere with each other's reasoning.
  • The orchestrator's job is decomposition, dispatch, and synthesis of results.

13.8 Plan mode workflow

What plan mode does

  • Claude reads your request, analyzes the codebase, and produces a structured execution plan — a list of proposed actions (file writes, shell commands, API calls) with rationale — but does not execute any of them.
  • You review the plan, request modifications if needed, then explicitly approve execution; this separates the reasoning step from the action step and gives you a review checkpoint before irreversible changes happen.

How to enter and exit plan mode

  • Cycle through modes using Shift-Tab at the Claude Code prompt until "Plan" mode is shown, or prefix your message with an explicit plan request.
  • Claude outputs a structured plan in Markdown format describing each proposed action.
  • When satisfied, approve it — the ExitPlanMode mechanism signals Claude to proceed to execution; Claude then executes the approved plan, tool by tool, with normal tool-use confirmation flows active.

When to use plan mode

  • Multi-file refactors where you want to see the full scope before any file is touched.
  • Database migrations or infrastructure changes where partial execution is worse than no execution.
  • Onboarding to an unfamiliar codebase where you want to understand what Claude is about to do before it does it.
  • Any task where you have a strong prior about the correct approach and want to verify Claude's plan matches it.
  • The cost is one extra round-trip to the model; the benefit is a review gate that prevents costly rollbacks.

13.9 Output styles

What output styles control

  • Named presets (concise, verbose, technical, explanatory, bullet-heavy) that adjust how Claude structures its text output without modifying CLAUDE.md. (The exact preset set is version-specific — verify current options.)
  • Distinct from capability changes (which modes and tools are active) — output styles affect presentation only.
  • Set at the session level via the /config command or through settings.

Custom styles vs named presets

  • Define custom styles in your CLAUDE.md by describing the desired output format explicitly (e.g., "always use numbered lists for multi-step instructions, never use more than 2 levels of nesting, keep code blocks short and focused").
  • Most advanced users find a well-written CLAUDE.md output format section more precise than a named style preset — you specify exactly the formatting contract you want rather than choosing from a fixed menu.
  • The named styles system is most useful for quick, temporary changes within a session.

13.10 Fast mode — when and why

What fast mode does

  • Trades reasoning depth for latency — uses a faster response configuration (typically a faster model variant or reduced thinking budget), producing responses more quickly at the cost of depth on complex tasks.
  • Not a different model selection in the sense of manually switching to Haiku; a tunable configuration within a session that adjusts the latency/quality tradeoff along a continuous axis.
  • The exact implementation — whether it swaps models, reduces context, or adjusts sampling — is subject to change; consult current documentation for the precise behavior.

When to use and when to avoid

  • Use fast mode for: interactive Q&A needing rapid back-and-forth on non-complex questions; exploration tasks where you want quick directional answers rather than exhaustive analysis; high-volume routine tasks (linting explanation, simple one-liner generation) where the full reasoning budget is overkill.
  • Avoid fast mode for: plan generation, architectural analysis, security review, complex debugging — tasks where missing a subtlety has real cost.
  • Practical heuristic: if you would be comfortable asking a junior developer with the context who answers quickly, fast mode is fine; if you want senior-level depth, use standard mode.

13.11 ScheduleWakeup, /loop, /schedule

Claude Code provides three distinct scheduling primitives that overlap in purpose but differ in mechanism and scope.

ScheduleWakeup — programmatic one-shot delay

  • A tool callable within skills and agent workflows that schedules Claude Code to wake and execute a task at a specific future time.
  • The programmatic primitive: a skill can call ScheduleWakeup to schedule follow-up work (e.g., "check if this CI job passed in 10 minutes").
  • Operates within the current machine's session lifecycle.

/loop — dynamic interval runner

  • Takes a prompt or slash command and an interval specification and repeatedly executes that command at the given cadence within a running Claude Code session.
  • Interactive — you run it in a live session and it keeps running until you stop it; use for monitoring tasks, polling a status endpoint, or repeatedly running a check while actively working.
  • The interval can be model-paced (Claude decides when enough time has passed) or fixed-clock; does not persist across session restarts.

/schedule — persistent cross-session scheduler

  • Creates "remote agents" — cron-like scheduled tasks that run on a schedule, survive session restarts, and execute even when you are not at the keyboard.
  • Configure with a cron expression or natural language schedule.
  • Right primitive for daily standup generation, nightly report emails, weekly code quality scans, or any task that should run reliably on a calendar-based cadence.
  • The three primitives form a hierarchy: /schedule for persistent recurring jobs, /loop for in-session monitoring, ScheduleWakeup for programmatic one-shot delays. (ScheduleWakeup is an internal tool name — verify it against current docs.)

13.12 Settings and permissions — .claude/settings.json

Two-file settings hierarchy

  • ~/.claude/settings.json — global, applies to all projects.
  • <project>/.claude/settings.json — project-scoped, committed to the repo; overrides global settings where they conflict.
  • Settings schema covers three major areas: permissions (what tools and shell commands Claude can run without prompting), environment variables injected into shell tool calls, and hooks.

Permissions — allowlists and denylists

  • The allowlist defines patterns of tool calls Claude can execute without asking for confirmation — e.g., allowing all Bash calls matching git *, or Read on any file under src/.
  • The denylist defines patterns that are always blocked regardless of what Claude or the user requests.
  • This architecture lets you grant broad automation for safe operations (git, test runners, linters) while maintaining strict confirmation requirements for destructive operations (production deployments, database mutations).

Example .claude/settings.json

{
  "permissions": {
    "allow": [
      "Bash(git *)",
      "Bash(npm test *)",
      "Bash(npm run lint *)",
      "Read(**)",
      "Edit(src/**)"
    ],
    "deny": [
      "Bash(rm -rf *)",
      "Bash(git push --force *)",
      "Bash(* production *)"
    ]
  },
  "env": {
    "NODE_ENV": "development",
    "LOG_LEVEL": "debug"
  },
  "hooks": {
    "SessionStart": [
      {
        "command": "echo 'Project: MyApp | Branch: '$(git branch --show-current)",
        "description": "Show project context at session start"
      }
    ]
  }
}

13.13 Memory system — auto-memory, MEMORY.md

Structure and memory types

  • Core component is a MEMORY.md index file (typically at ~/.claude/MEMORY.md) that acts as a table of contents for individual memory files — each memory is a separate Markdown file with a typed header.
  • User memories — personal preferences, communication style, and workflow patterns the agent has learned about you.
  • Feedback memories — corrections you have given ("don't add trailing commas to JSON", "always use single quotes in Python") so Claude does not repeat mistakes.
  • Project memories — project-specific knowledge discovered mid-session (e.g., "the payments module uses a non-standard event emitter pattern").
  • Reference memories — factual information you have explicitly asked Claude to remember (API keys, team member names, recurring context).

Auto-memory

  • Mechanism by which Claude Code proactively creates memory entries without being explicitly asked.
  • Triggered by signals in the conversation: explicit "remember this" instructions, corrections to Claude's behavior, and discovery of facts likely to recur.
  • At session start, Claude reads MEMORY.md and loads relevant memory files as additional context, giving it continuity across sessions.

Architecture rationale

  • Separate files + index allows selective loading rather than dumping all memories into every context window — as memory volume grows, Claude selectively loads only memories relevant to the current task.
  • Memory files are plain Markdown and fully human-readable; you can manually create and edit them.
  • The exact implementation of auto-memory, the MEMORY.md schema, and the loading strategy are subject to change across Claude Code versions — treat this as an architectural description rather than a specification.

13.14 Hands-on: configure Claude Code for your daily workflow

This walkthrough takes you from a fresh Claude Code installation to a fully configured daily workflow in six steps. (If you haven't installed it yet, do that first: npm install -g @anthropic-ai/claude-code.) Each step is independently valuable — you can stop after any step and have a meaningfully improved setup.

Walkthrough · Configure Claude Code for your daily workflow

  1. Create your global CLAUDE.md. 
    mkdir -p ~/.claude
cat > ~/.claude/CLAUDE.md << 'EOF'
# Global Claude Instructions

## Identity context
- My name is [Your Name]. I'm a [role] working primarily in [language/stack].
- My email is [email].

## Response preferences
- Be concise. Skip preamble. Lead with the answer.
- Use numbered lists for multi-step processes.
- When in doubt, ask one clarifying question rather than guessing.

## Constraints (always active)
- Never use `git push --force` without explicit confirmation.
- Never commit secrets or credentials.
- Always run tests before declaring a task complete.
EOF
    This file will be active in every Claude Code session from this point forward. Edit it whenever you notice a preference that should be universal.
  2. Initialize your project CLAUDE.md. Navigate to your project root and run: 
    cd /path/to/your-project
claude /init
    The /init command analyzes your codebase (package.json, directory structure, README, test files) and generates a starter CLAUDE.md. Review it, remove anything inaccurate, and add your project's specific conventions. Commit it: git add CLAUDE.md && git commit -m "chore: add Claude Code project config".
  3. Configure project permissions. Create .claude/settings.json in the project root: 
    mkdir -p .claude
cat > .claude/settings.json << 'EOF'
{
  "permissions": {
    "allow": [
      "Bash(git *)",
      "Bash(npm test *)",
      "Bash(npm run *)",
      "Read(**)",
      "Edit(src/**)",
      "Edit(tests/**)"
    ],
    "deny": [
      "Bash(rm -rf *)",
      "Bash(git push --force *)"
    ]
  }
}
EOF
    This eliminates confirmation prompts for safe operations while keeping hard blocks on destructive commands. Commit alongside CLAUDE.md.
  4. Write a custom slash command. Create a standup command: 
    mkdir -p ~/.claude/commands
cat > ~/.claude/commands/standup.md << 'EOF'
Generate a standup update for today.

1. Run `git log --oneline --since="yesterday" --author="$(git config user.email)"` to see yesterday's commits.
2. Check if there are any open PRs: `gh pr list --author "@me" --state open` (skip if gh is not available).
3. Produce a standup update in this format:
   **Yesterday:** [bullet points from commits]
   **Today:** [ask me what I plan to work on if not obvious from context]
   **Blockers:** [any blockers visible from PR comments or open issues, or "None"]
EOF
    Now /standup is available in every session. Test it: open Claude Code and type /standup.
  5. Register a marketplace, then install a plugin. Open Claude Code and run: 
    /plugin marketplace add obra/superpowers-marketplace
    Then complete the interactive installation: 
    /plugin
    Follow the prompts to confirm and activate. After installation, /help will show the new skills and commands the plugin registered. Verify the superpowers plugin name against the current marketplace — plugin identifiers may differ from skill names.
  6. Verify everything is wired up. Start a fresh Claude Code session in your project: 
    claude
    Check that: (a) Claude greets you with awareness of the project (from CLAUDE.md); (b) /standup appears in /help; (c) a git status Bash call does not prompt for confirmation (allowlisted); (d) attempting a rm -rf /tmp/test Bash call prompts for confirmation or is blocked (depending on your deny rules). If any of these fail, review the settings and CLAUDE.md for typos or scope mismatches.

Daily rhythm with this setup

  • Start each session with /standup to orient.
  • Use plan mode (Shift-Tab) for any task touching more than two files.
  • Use /schedule to set up nightly code quality scans.
  • When Claude makes a mistake, correct it explicitly and let auto-memory capture the correction — it will not repeat that mistake tomorrow.
  • The investment in this config compounds: every session is faster than the last because the agent accumulates accurate context about how you work.
← Ch 12. Kilo Code Deep Dive → Ch 14. Gemini CLI Deep Dive → Ch 15. Superpowers (the canonical skill plugin) → Ch 16. MCP Servers

14. Gemini CLI Deep Dive

What you'll learn: Gemini CLI's architecture, the free-tier story, GEMINI.md, extensions, MCP integration, how 1M context changes the calculus, and how to configure Gemini CLI for daily work.

14.1 Architecture — terminal, open-source, Google-backed

Gemini CLI is Google's official command-line interface for the Gemini family of models — a Node.js application released as open-source under the Apache 2.0 license that runs in any terminal.

Open-source advantage

  • The entire agent loop — tool dispatch, context management, hook invocation — is auditable and forkable in a way that proprietary CLI tools are not.
  • You can read exactly what gets sent to the model and patch behavior without waiting for an upstream release.

Architectural similarities and differences vs Claude Code

  • Both are terminal-first, use a REPL-style conversation loop, discover project-level instruction files automatically, and expose extension points for adding tools and skills.
  • Meaningful differences: Gemini CLI ships with GoogleSearch and WebFetch as first-class built-in tools — web retrieval is not an add-on; defaults to a dramatically larger context window (1M tokens on supported models); routes to Gemini models rather than Claude.
  • Think of them as two implementations of the same architectural pattern, optimized for different model families.

Google-backed integration

  • Vertex AI for enterprise deployments, Google account authentication for personal use, and a free tier that is genuinely generous by industry standards.
  • Roadmap pacing tracks Gemini model releases closely — when a new Gemini model ships, CLI support typically follows quickly.
  • Verify the current release cadence and version in the official GitHub repository before depending on specific behavior.

14.2 The model story — Gemini 3 Pro/Flash, free-tier economics

Primary models

  • Model routing is configured via settings.json (verify schema against current docs).
  • Gemini 3 Pro — reasoning-heavy, higher-latency option suited for complex multi-step tasks.
  • Gemini 3 Flash — speed-optimized, lower-cost option suited for fast iteration and high-volume tasks.
  • The pattern gemini-{version}-pro / gemini-{version}-flash has been stable, but verify current model identifiers in the Gemini API documentation before hardcoding them in configuration files.

Alternative model routing

  • Supports routing to alternative models via OpenAI-compatible API endpoints through the openai_compat model provider configuration — making Gemini CLI a general-purpose agentic shell, not a Gemini-only tool.
  • Can also configure direct Gemini API access for models not yet surfaced through the default routing.

Practical model selection

  • Use Flash for exploratory work, quick edits, and anything where latency matters.
  • Use Pro for code generation requiring multi-file reasoning, architectural design discussions, or long planning sessions where extra latency is irrelevant compared to the quality difference.
  • With a 1M context window available on both variants, the choice is almost never about "fitting the context" — it's about the quality-latency tradeoff for the specific task.

14.3 Authentication paths — personal Google, API key, Vertex AI

Personal Google account (free tier)

  • Entry point for individual developers: log in via OAuth and get access to the free tier.
  • As of mid-2025: approximately 1M context tokens per request and around 1,000 requests per day — verify current limits at ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/rate-limits.
  • A genuinely usable daily-driver tier, not a preview teaser — the reason Gemini CLI has low friction for new users.

API key (Gemini API)

  • Generate an API key at Google AI Studio, set it in your environment (GEMINI_API_KEY), and gain access to higher rate limits and pay-as-you-go pricing.
  • Right choice for reliable, higher-volume access for personal projects or small teams, without the full enterprise setup.
  • Also enables programmatic use in CI pipelines and scripts where OAuth browser flows are not possible.

Vertex AI (enterprise)

  • Uses Google Cloud Application Default Credentials (ADC) — typically via gcloud auth application-default login — and routes requests through your organization's Google Cloud project.
  • Provides VPC-SC controls, data residency guarantees, audit logging, and the ability to use private model endpoints.
  • The correct auth path for any production or compliance-sensitive deployment.
  • Configuration lives in settings.json under the vertexai key; exact schema should be verified against current documentation as the Vertex AI integration has expanded significantly since initial launch.
Personal Google account
Free tier via OAuth — ~1M context, ~1 000 req/day; zero-friction entry point.
API key (Gemini API)
Pay-as-you-go via AI Studio — higher rate limits, CI-friendly, no browser flow.
Vertex AI
Enterprise path via GCP ADC — VPC-SC, data residency, audit logging.

14.4 GEMINI.md — global, project, subdirectory scope

GEMINI.md is Gemini CLI's instruction-injection mechanism, directly analogous to Claude Code's CLAUDE.md. At session start, Gemini CLI discovers and loads GEMINI.md files from three scope levels, merging them into the active context.

Global scope (~/.gemini/GEMINI.md)

  • Applies to every Gemini CLI session on this machine — like a personal dotfile, changes here affect every project immediately.
  • Right place for: communication style preferences, always-on constraints, personal tooling context, and facts about you the agent should always know.
  • A minimal but effective global GEMINI.md contains: your name and role, your preferred explanation depth, your go-to languages, and any firm constraints you never want violated ("never push directly to main").

Project root scope (<project>/GEMINI.md)

  • The most important scope for team workflows — describes the project to the agent: tech stack, architecture overview, testing conventions, CI pipeline, code style rules, common commands, and known gotchas.
  • Commit it to the repository so all teammates get consistent agent behavior; a good project GEMINI.md eliminates the need to re-explain context at the start of every session.

Subdirectory scope (<project>/src/payments/GEMINI.md)

  • Applies only within that directory tree — use for modules with unusual conventions, security-sensitive areas, or domain-specific glossaries.
  • All three levels stack — the agent sees global + project root + subdirectory simultaneously.

Here is a minimal example:

# GEMINI.md — payments service
## Stack
Node.js 20, Postgres 15, Stripe SDK v12.

## Constraints
- Never log card numbers or CVV values, even in debug output.
- All DB queries must go through the query builder in src/db/index.ts.
- Tests live in __tests__/ alongside source files.

## Commands
- npm test — run unit tests
- npm run migrate -- --env staging — apply pending migrations
GEMINI.md vs CLAUDE.md — structural equivalence: The two files serve identical purposes and share the same three-scope hierarchy (global / project / subdirectory). The practical difference is tooling: /init in Claude Code generates a CLAUDE.md by analyzing your codebase; Gemini CLI has its own equivalent scaffolding command (verify current command name). If you work across both tools, maintaining parallel GEMINI.md and CLAUDE.md files with shared content is a common pattern — some teams symlink them or use a shared source of truth with a generation step.

14.5 Slash commands — built-in and custom

Gemini CLI exposes a slash command interface in the REPL. Type / at the prompt to see the current list.

Known built-in commands

  • /help — list all commands.
  • /clear — reset the conversation context.
  • /chat — manage named conversation sessions (save, resume, list).
  • /compress — summarize and compress the conversation history to reduce token usage while preserving key state.
  • /memory — manage the MemoryTool's stored facts.
  • /tools — list available tools and their status.
  • /stats — display token usage for the current session.
  • /quit (or /exit) — end the session.
  • The complete and authoritative list is in /help — these commands evolve frequently, and names or behaviors may differ in your installed version.

Custom slash commands

  • Can be added through GEMINI.md and extensions — define a command in a skill or extension manifest and it becomes available as a slash command in the session.
  • Allows teams to surface domain-specific workflows as first-class commands, e.g., a /deploy-preview command that runs your staging deployment workflow, or a /lint-fix command that runs your full linting pipeline and applies safe auto-fixes.

14.6 Skills — activate_skill and on-demand loading

Explicit pull model vs Claude Code's push model

  • In Claude Code, skills are discovered at session start from the filesystem and loaded proactively — available skills appear in the system-reminder context and can be invoked immediately.
  • In Gemini CLI, the primary activation path is the activate_skill tool: the agent calls this tool with a skill name or path, which loads the skill's SKILL.md into context and makes its capabilities available — an explicit pull model rather than an implicit push model.

Wiring skills via GEMINI.md

  • The agent needs to know what skills exist (typically via GEMINI.md listing them) and then activate the relevant ones for a task.
  • A common pattern: list available skills in project GEMINI.md with short descriptions so the model can recognize when a task maps to a skill and call activate_skill appropriately.
  • Some teams make this more explicit by writing activation instructions directly: "For deployment tasks, activate the deploy skill first."

Skill authoring format

  • Follows the same SKILL.md convention: a directory containing a SKILL.md (instructions on what the skill does, when to activate it, and how to use it) plus any supporting scripts or configuration.
  • Key difference from Claude Code: Gemini CLI skills don't automatically expose slash commands or appear in system reminders — they rely on the activate_skill invocation path, which makes activation more explicit but requires more deliberate GEMINI.md configuration.

14.7 Extensions — install, discover, share

What extensions are

  • Primary mechanism for adding tools, capabilities, and integrations beyond the built-ins.
  • An extension is a directory with a manifest file (typically gemini-extension.json) that declares the extension's name, description, tools it provides, and any GEMINI.md content to inject.
  • Extensions can provide MCP servers, custom tools implemented as shell commands, additional slash commands, or skill bundles.

Installation

  • From a remote Git repository: gemini extensions install --source <git-url>; for a local directory: --path <local-path>.
  • Verify exact flag names and command syntax with gemini extensions --help, as the CLI's extension management commands have changed across versions.
  • Installed extensions are tracked in your global Gemini CLI configuration and activated in all sessions unless scoped otherwise.

Discovery and sharing

  • Discovering community extensions currently happens through GitHub search and word of mouth — no centralized extension registry analogous to VS Code's marketplace, though Google has indicated a more structured discovery mechanism is planned.
  • Sharing an extension: publish the extension directory to a public Git repository — the --source install path accepts any valid git URL (GitHub, GitLab, or self-hosted).
  • For internal team extensions, private repositories work fine as long as the installing user has read access and appropriate SSH keys or tokens configured.

14.8 Hooks — lifecycle events

How hooks work

  • Shell commands (or scripts) configured in settings.json under the hooks key that fire at defined lifecycle events and receive context via environment variables or stdin.
  • Right place for: logging, notification, safety gates, and environmental setup that should happen consistently regardless of conversation content.

Supported events

  • Known lifecycle events: pre- and post-tool execution hooks (fire before/after any tool call), session start/end hooks, and command hooks that fire around specific slash commands.
  • The complete list is narrower than Claude Code's hook system and has grown incrementally — verify the current supported events in the official repository before writing hooks that depend on specific event types.
  • A practical minimum viable setup: a post-session hook that appends a summary to a daily log file, and a pre-shell-command hook that echoes the command to stderr for human review in high-trust environments.

Limitations vs Claude Code hooks

  • Gemini CLI hooks are currently more limited to shell-command invocations — unlike Claude Code's hooks, which are integrated into the harness and can trigger skills.
  • If migrating a Claude Code workflow that relies heavily on hooks triggering complex skill orchestration, you may need to rebuild that logic differently — typically by encoding the orchestration in the agent's instructions rather than in hook scripts.

14.9 Built-in tools — Read, Write, Shell, GoogleSearch, WebFetch, MemoryTool

Read

  • Gives the agent access to your local filesystem for reading files and directories; respects the permissions model configured in settings.json and will prompt for confirmation on paths outside the project root unless pre-approved.
  • The workhorse tool for any code-understanding or analysis task.

Write

  • Creates or overwrites files; subject to the permissions model and will prompt by default for writes to sensitive paths.
  • Used for code generation, configuration file creation, and any output that should persist beyond the session.

Shell

  • Executes arbitrary shell commands in the local environment — the most powerful and most dangerous built-in tool.
  • Gives the agent the ability to run tests, install packages, invoke build systems, and perform any action your user account can perform.
  • The permissions model and the --yolo flag (see 14.11) both affect how Shell invocations are confirmed.

GoogleSearch

  • A first-class built-in, not an optional add-on — the agent can invoke Google Search directly to retrieve current information, resolve documentation questions, check package versions, or look up error messages.
  • Results are returned as structured summaries with source URLs, not raw HTML.
  • Having search as a built-in fundamentally changes how you prompt the agent — you can ask questions that require current information without needing to pre-populate context.

WebFetch

  • Retrieves the full content of a specific URL and returns it as text for the agent to reason over.
  • Use it when GoogleSearch returns a URL you want the agent to read in full: a documentation page, a GitHub issue, a PR diff, or a specification.
  • WebFetch and GoogleSearch together give Gemini CLI a web-retrieval capability that is genuinely useful without external MCP servers.

MemoryTool

  • Provides a key-value persistence layer across sessions — the agent can store facts, decisions, and state using MemoryTool, and they will be available in future sessions.
  • Inspectable and editable via the /memory slash command, making it easy to audit and correct what the agent has stored.
  • See 14.12 for how MemoryTool and GEMINI.md work together as a complete memory system.

14.10 MCP integration in Gemini CLI

Registration and routing

  • MCP servers are registered in settings.json under the mcpServers key, using the same JSON structure becoming the de facto standard across CLI tools.
  • Each server entry specifies a name, transport type (stdio or sse), the command to launch the server (for stdio), and any environment variables it needs.
  • Once registered, MCP tools appear in the agent's tool list alongside built-ins; the agent discovers them at session start via the standard MCP tools/list call and invokes them via tools/call — from the agent's perspective, MCP tools are indistinguishable from built-in tools.

Differences from Claude Code's MCP support

  • Gemini CLI's MCP integration is evolving — MCP Resources (server-side context documents) and MCP Prompts (server-defined prompt templates) may have more limited support than MCP Tools.
  • Verify current MCP feature coverage in the Gemini CLI release notes before depending on Resource or Prompt features.
  • Claude Code's MCP integration, having been in production longer, has broader resource and prompt support at this writing.

14.11 Settings — settings.json, permissions, --yolo

Settings files

  • ~/.gemini/settings.json (global) and optionally <project>/.gemini/settings.json (project-scoped).
  • Controls: model selection, authentication configuration, permissions for tool use, MCP server registrations, hook definitions, and UI preferences.
  • Project-scoped settings file is useful for teams that want to standardize tool permissions or MCP server registrations per project without requiring each developer to configure them manually.

Permissions system

  • By default, Write, Shell, and MCP tool calls prompt for confirmation.
  • Pre-approve specific paths, commands, or tool names in the permissions configuration to reduce friction for common operations.
  • The model is intentionally conservative — the default is to ask, not to assume; when setting up a new project, expect permission prompts and add patterns to your permissions config rather than clicking through repeatedly.

The --yolo flag — scripting convenience, not a workflow setting

  • A per-invocation flag that auto-approves all tool calls for that single gemini command run — it is not a persistent mode toggle and does not modify settings.json.
  • Use it for scripted, non-interactive invocations where you trust the task completely: CI pipelines, batch processing scripts, automated one-shots.
  • Do not use it interactively as a way to skip confirmations during exploratory work — the prompts exist precisely to catch unintended side effects.

14.12 Memory and context tools

Two complementary mechanisms

  • GEMINI.md — stable, human-curated context: what you know before the agent starts working (project conventions, constraints, background context).
  • MemoryTool — dynamic, session-updated facts: what the agent learns during work (user preferences revealed over time, decisions made, corrections applied, workflow patterns observed).
  • Together they cover the two ends of the memory spectrum.

How MemoryTool works

  • Stores data as named key-value entries that persist in the Gemini CLI memory store (typically a local file in ~/.gemini/).
  • The agent can read, write, and delete entries via the MemoryTool API; entries are loaded into context at session start.
  • The /memory slash command lists current stored entries, lets you edit them, and lets you delete stale or incorrect entries — review it periodically and remove entries that are outdated or wrong.

Memory hygiene pattern

  • At the end of a significant work session, ask the agent to summarize what it learned that should be remembered, review the proposed entries, approve or edit them, then let MemoryTool store the result.
  • This intentional "close session" step prevents MemoryTool from accumulating a growing pile of stale facts.
  • For facts that should survive a full MemoryTool reset (e.g., fundamental project constraints), move them to GEMINI.md rather than relying on MemoryTool persistence.

14.13 Working with the 1M context window

A 1M-token context window is not just a quantitative improvement — it changes the qualitative nature of how you work with the agent. The scarcity model that governs most AI-assisted work (carefully curate what you include, summarize aggressively, rotate files in and out) largely dissolves.

Patterns that work well

  • Load the full repository at the start of a session when working on a non-trivial codebase.
  • Keep the full conversation history in context across a long multi-step task rather than compressing.
  • Include full specification documents and API references directly, rather than summarized excerpts.
  • Ask the model to reason about the full test suite when debugging a regression — not just the failing test.
  • The 1M window makes "just load the whole thing" a viable strategy for most real-world codebases.

Anti-patterns to avoid

  • Treating the 1M window as a dumping ground — loading everything available just because you can; irrelevant context still costs tokens and can dilute attention on what matters.
  • Using the large window to avoid writing good GEMINI.md — foundational project context should be in GEMINI.md and loaded efficiently on every session, not reconstructed by loading thousands of files each time.
  • Forgetting that long contexts increase latency: a nearly-full 1M context window will produce noticeably slower responses than a focused 10K context, even on Flash.
  • Load broadly, but load intentionally.

14.14 Hands-on: configure Gemini CLI for your daily workflow

This walkthrough takes you from a fresh Gemini CLI installation to a configured daily-driver setup. Complete it once and you will have a working foundation to iterate on.

Walkthrough · Configure Gemini CLI for your daily workflow

1. Install Gemini CLI. Gemini CLI requires Node.js 18 or later. Install globally via npm:

npm install -g @google/gemini-cli

Verify the installation: gemini --version. Check the GitHub repository for the current package name and minimum Node version, as these may have changed.

2. Authenticate with your personal Google account. The simplest starting point:

gemini auth login

This opens a browser window for OAuth authentication. After approving access, your credentials are cached locally. Verify the exact subcommand — it may be gemini auth or a direct gemini login depending on the version. Confirm you're authenticated: gemini auth status (or equivalent). You are now on the free tier.

3. Create your global GEMINI.md. Create the directory and file:

mkdir -p ~/.gemini
# Edit ~/.gemini/GEMINI.md with your preferred editor

Add at minimum: your name and role, your preferred programming languages, 3–5 always-on constraints, and a line listing where your projects live. This file is loaded on every session — keep it focused and under 500 lines.

4. Create a project GEMINI.md. Navigate to a project you work in regularly:

cd ~/projects/your-project
# Create GEMINI.md describing the project stack and conventions

Run gemini in the project directory and ask the agent: "Analyze this project and help me write a GEMINI.md that captures the key context you would want for future sessions." Review and edit the output. Commit the result to the repository.

5. Install an extension. Find an extension you want (e.g., a community tool from GitHub):

gemini extensions install --source https://github.com/example/gemini-ext-name

Verify the exact flag syntax with gemini extensions --help. After installation, restart your session and confirm the extension's tools appear in /tools.
