6. Register an MCP server. Open or create ~/.gemini/settings.json and add an MCP server. Example for a filesystem MCP server:

{
  "mcpServers": {
    "filesystem": {
      "command": "npx",
      "args": ["-y", "@modelcontextprotocol/server-filesystem", "/path/to/allow"],
      "transport": "stdio"
    }
  }
}

Start a new Gemini CLI session and verify the MCP tools appear: ask the agent "What tools do you have available?" and look for tools from the registered server.

7. Validate your setup. In a Gemini CLI session in your project directory, run through a quick smoke test: ask the agent to explain the project's main entry point (tests Read), create a scratch file (tests Write), run your project's test command (tests Shell), look up a dependency's changelog (tests GoogleSearch + WebFetch). If all five tools work without unexpected permission errors, your daily-driver config is operational.

Three threads from earlier chapters lead here, and three lead onward.

← Ch 2. Context as Working Memory ← Ch 4. Model Selection & Cost (free tier) ← Ch 13. Claude Code Deep Dive → Ch 15. Superpowers in Gemini CLI → Ch 16. MCP Servers

15. The Superpowers Methodology — Claude Code Skills, Gemini CLI Skills & Kilo Code Modes

What you'll learn: why methodology bundles exist, the portable patterns Superpowers encodes, the canonical Claude Code plugin, how to adapt to Kilo Code (modes/rules) and Gemini CLI (skills), and how to ship your own methodology bundle for your team.

15.1 What Superpowers is — a methodology bundle, not a tool

The problem it solves

  • Every experienced developer has a workflow: habits and checkpoints applied to every non-trivial task — think before you code, write the test first, get a second opinion before merging.
  • The problem with agentic coding is that the agent has no habits — left unprompted, it skips steps humans would never skip: writing code before understanding requirements, merging without verification, implementing without planning.
  • A methodology bundle is a collection of encoded workflows that teaches the agent your habits — it does not add capabilities; it shapes how the agent uses the capabilities it already has.

What Superpowers encodes

  • Superpowers is the canonical methodology bundle for Claude Code, encoding a specific, opinionated way of working: every creative task starts with brainstorming; every multi-step task gets a written plan before a single file changes; every implementation follows test-driven discipline; every completion goes through a verification gate; every significant change goes through a code review loop.
  • These habits are not invented for the bundle — they are the habits experienced software engineers apply anyway. Superpowers makes them automatic for the agent.

Tool-agnostic at heart

  • The methodology — brainstorm → spec → plan → execute → TDD → verify — is not Claude-specific.
  • The same sequence can be encoded as Kilo Code custom modes, as Gemini CLI skills, or as a structured session-opening prompt.
  • Teams that understand this can carry the methodology across tool changes, model upgrades, and organizational standardization efforts without losing their workflow investment.

15.2 The Superpowers methodology — portable patterns

The six stages

  • (1) Brainstorm — before touching code or writing specs, explore the problem space, surface hidden requirements, and consider multiple approaches.
  • (2) Spec — write down what you are building in enough detail that a collaborator could implement it without asking questions.
  • (3) Plan — decompose the spec into an ordered list of concrete steps, identifying dependencies and risks before any file changes occur.
  • (4) Execute — implement the plan, step by step, with human checkpoints at phase boundaries.
  • (5) TDD — write tests that demonstrate the implementation is correct, not after the fact but as part of the execution phase.
  • (6) Verify — confirm that the thing you built matches the thing you specified, using the agent's own verification skills before declaring done.

Not strictly sequential

  • Each stage has a corresponding skill in the Superpowers library; skills are not strictly sequential in all workflows — a small bugfix might start at Execute and skip Brainstorm and Spec entirely.
  • The methodology is a default sequence applied when task complexity warrants it, with lighter-weight shortcuts for simpler work.
  • The key insight: the methodology encodes the decision about which stages to apply, not just the execution of each stage — this is what separates a methodology bundle from a collection of isolated prompts.

The artifact-gate structure

  • Before moving from one stage to the next, produce an artifact (a brainstorm document, a spec file, a plan file, a test suite) that the human can review and approve.
  • Artifacts are checkpoints — the mechanism that keeps the agent from sprinting ahead while the human loses track of what is being built.
  • Regardless of which tool you use to implement this, the artifact-gate structure is what makes the methodology meaningful in practice.

15.3 The library tour — what each skill enforces

The Superpowers library covers the full development lifecycle. Each skill is a named, installable unit of methodology. The canonical library in order:

brainstorming
Forces exploration before any implementation begins.
writing-plans
Produces a reviewable numbered plan before code changes.
executing-plans
Steps through an approved plan with human checkpoints.
subagent-driven-development
Dispatches parallel subagents for independent plan tasks.
test-driven-development
Enforces red-green-refactor; blocks code before tests.
systematic-debugging
Hypothesis cycle: form, reproduce, verify, fix.
verification-before-completion
Runs structured checklist before any "task done" signal.
requesting-code-review
Formats change summary and decision rationale for reviewer.
receiving-code-review
Processes feedback systematically before making changes.
finishing-a-development-branch
Branch-close checklist: green, rebased, PR written, merged.
writing-skills
Meta-skill: guides creation of new methodology skills.
  • brainstorming — Forces exploration before implementation. Invokes structured divergent thinking (multiple approaches, hidden requirements, risks) before the agent produces any artifact. The mandatory pre-step for any creative work.
  • writing-plans — Takes a spec or requirements description and produces a written, numbered implementation plan. No code is written during this skill. The plan is an artifact for human review.
  • executing-plans — Works through an approved plan step by step, checking off items and pausing at defined review points. Keeps execution aligned to the plan rather than improvising.
  • subagent-driven-development — Dispatches independent plan tasks to parallel subagents, collecting results back into the main session. The parallelism skill for decomposable plans.
  • test-driven-development — Enforces the red-green-refactor cycle: write a failing test, make it pass with minimal implementation, then refactor. The agent is blocked from writing implementation before tests.
  • systematic-debugging — Structures debugging as a hypothesis cycle: form a hypothesis about root cause, write a minimal reproducer, verify, fix, confirm fix. Prevents random trial-and-error code changes.
  • verification-before-completion — Before declaring a task done, the agent runs a structured verification checklist: tests pass, no regressions, output matches spec, edge cases considered.
  • requesting-code-review — Prepares a structured review request: summarizes the change, highlights design decisions, identifies areas of uncertainty, and formats for a human or agent reviewer.
  • receiving-code-review — Processes incoming review feedback systematically: acknowledges each finding, classifies it (agree/disagree/needs-clarification), and produces a response plan before making any changes.
  • finishing-a-development-branch — The branch-close checklist: all tests green, branch rebased or merged cleanly, PR description written, review addressed, merge-ready signal sent.
  • writing-skills — The meta-skill. Guides the creation of new skills following the Superpowers format: name, description (for retrieval), body (agent instructions), references. The bootstrapping skill for methodology extension.

Extensibility

  • The library is not exhaustive by design — it covers the critical phase transitions that agents most commonly skip.
  • Teams extend it with domain-specific skills (deployment workflows, incident response runbooks, database migration procedures) using the same format.

15.4 Three implementations, one idea — Claude / Gemini / Kilo

Claude Code skills (canonical)

  • Skills are SKILL.md files discovered automatically from ~/.claude/skills/ and plugin directories; appear in system-reminder context at session start and are invoked via the Skill tool.
  • Auto-invocation via SessionStart hooks means the agent can apply skills without explicit user instruction — the methodology fires automatically when task patterns match.
  • The most seamless implementation because the harness does the wiring.

Gemini CLI skills (explicit pull model)

  • The same SKILL.md format works in Gemini CLI, but activation is explicit via the activate_skill tool rather than automatic.
  • Skills listed in GEMINI.md give the agent awareness of what's available; the agent then decides when to call activate_skill.
  • Slightly more manual but transparent — every skill activation is a visible agent action in the conversation log; the right implementation when you want audit trails of which methodology steps were applied.

Kilo Code modes/rules (mode-selected)

  • Encodes methodology as custom modes: each Superpowers skill maps to a custom mode whose system prompt contains the skill body.
  • The user (or an Orchestrator mode) selects the appropriate mode for each phase.
  • Advantage: model selection per mode — route brainstorming mode to a reasoning-heavy model and executing-plans mode to a faster, cheaper model.
  • Tradeoff: mode transitions require explicit user action; no auto-invocation equivalent. The best implementation for teams that want fine-grained model routing as part of their methodology.
Claude Code skills
Auto-invoked via SessionStart hook — methodology fires without user action.
Gemini CLI skills
Explicit pull model via activate_skill — visible in conversation audit log.
Kilo Code modes/rules
Mode-selected per phase — enables per-phase model routing for cost control.

15.5 The Claude Code plugin — installation and auto-invocation

Installation

  • Install via the /plugin interactive flow — either through the official marketplace (/plugin install superpowers@claude-plugins-official or equivalent) or via the canonical obra marketplace (/plugin marketplace add obra/superpowers-marketplace then /plugin).
  • Verify the current canonical install path against the Superpowers README at github.com/obra/superpowers before scripting against it.
  • To view installed plugins and confirm the installation succeeded, run /plugin on its own.
  • After installation, skills are available immediately in the current session and all subsequent sessions; the plugin manager handles versioning and updates.

The using-superpowers meta-skill and auto-invocation

  • The auto-invocation mechanism is the using-superpowers meta-skill, wired to the SessionStart lifecycle hook.
  • When Claude Code starts a new session, the harness fires SessionStart, which triggers using-superpowers; this meta-skill orients the agent: reminds it the methodology is active, enumerates available skills and their trigger conditions, and sets behavioral defaults for the session.
  • Result: the methodology is "on" from the first message, without the user needing to invoke anything explicitly.

Why the meta-skill matters

  • Skill discovery is passive (the skills appear in context), but behavioral adoption requires active orientation.
  • Without the meta-skill, an agent might have all the skills loaded but still skip the brainstorming phase because nothing prompted it to apply the methodology.
  • The SessionStart hook-driven orientation solves this by making the methodology's presence explicit at the start of every session, turning passive availability into active behavioral defaults.

15.6 The Claude Code skill machinery — format, discovery, Skill tool

SKILL.md format

  • A Claude Code skill is a directory containing a SKILL.md file (and optionally supporting scripts or configuration).
  • The SKILL.md format has a frontmatter YAML block at the top followed by a Markdown body.
  • Frontmatter declares at minimum: name (the kebab-case identifier), description (the when-to-invoke text used for retrieval), and optionally metadata (for type tagging).
  • Note: a trigger field appears in some community and user-authored skill formats (e.g., slash-command bindings in personal skills), but it is not part of the standard Superpowers SKILL.md convention — the standard fields are name, description, and metadata.
  • The Markdown body is the agent's instruction set — what to do when this skill is active, in whatever level of detail the skill requires.

Discovery path hierarchy

  • Claude Code scans ~/.claude/skills/ for user-level skills, plugin directories for installed plugin skills, and project-level .claude/skills/ for project-local skills.
  • All discovered skills are surfaced in the system-reminder context block that appears in the agent's context at session start and periodically during the session.
  • The agent sees skill names and descriptions, which enables it to invoke the right skill for the right task.

Invocation via the Skill tool

  • When the agent decides a task matches a skill's description, it calls Skill with the skill name and optional arguments; the tool loads the full SKILL.md body into the agent's active context and executes the skill's instructions.
  • From the user's perspective, skill invocation is transparent — you see the agent's actions, not the tool call that loaded the skill.
  • Understanding this machinery matters when writing skills or debugging why a skill is not firing: the description must be specific enough that the agent recognizes it as relevant, but not so narrow that it only fires in obvious cases.

15.7 Rigid vs flexible skills — when to enforce, when to suggest

Not all skills should behave the same way. The skill itself signals which category it belongs to through the language of its body.

Rigid skills

  • Use imperative, blocking language: "You MUST write the failing test before writing any implementation code. Do not proceed to implementation until you have a red test."
  • This language tells the agent that deviation is not appropriate, and the agent will typically respect it.
  • Appropriate for methodology steps where the discipline is the value — if you allow shortcuts, the methodology degrades to nothing.
  • Use rigid enforcement for TDD, for verification gates, and for any step where "I'll do it later" is a broken promise.

Flexible skills

  • Use advisory language: "Consider structuring your approach as... If the task is small enough to skip planning, use your judgment."
  • Provide a scaffold the agent can adapt; appropriate for tasks where the pattern has value at scale but mechanical enforcement would be counterproductive for small tasks.
  • The practical rule: if a senior engineer would be annoyed at being forced to follow the full procedure for a trivial task, the skill should be flexible; if skipping the procedure is how technical debt accumulates, the skill should be rigid.

15.8 Anatomy of a great skill

A well-written skill has four elements working together.

Name (kebab-case)

  • Must be memorable and self-describing: systematic-debugging is better than debug-helper because it communicates the approach, not just the domain.
  • Names are used in inter-skill references and slash commands — keep them stable once published.

Description (for retrieval)

  • The most performance-critical field — the agent reads descriptions to decide whether a skill is relevant.
  • A good description answers: "when should I invoke this skill?" — include trigger conditions ("Use when you encounter a bug or unexpected behavior and need to diagnose root cause"), not just a noun description of what the skill does.

Body (agent instructions)

  • Write it as instructions to the agent, not documentation for humans.
  • Use numbered steps for sequences, imperative language for requirements, and inline examples for anything where the agent might otherwise guess wrong.
  • Avoid vague instructions like "be thorough" — instead say "check the last three commits for related changes before proposing a fix."
  • Long enough to be unambiguous and short enough that the agent can hold it in working context while executing.

References (composed sub-skills via explicit tool calls)

  • Skills can call other skills — there is no special wiki-link syntax; composition is done via explicit tool calls in the agent's reasoning flow.
  • The skill body instructs the agent to invoke the Skill tool with another skill's name (e.g., "invoke the Skill tool with writing-plans, then with test-driven-development, then with verification-before-completion").
  • Note: the [[...]] double-bracket pattern is used by the auto-memory system, not by the skill runtime.
  • Use references deliberately — every reference is a potential invocation, and over-referencing creates an agent that is always switching context instead of doing work.

15.9 Adapting to Kilo Code — custom modes and rules

Mapping skills to custom modes

  • Kilo Code's custom mode system is the natural host for Superpowers skills in a Kilo Code workflow.
  • A custom mode has a name, a model selection, and a system prompt — map the skill body directly to the system prompt.
  • The mode's model selection is where Kilo Code adds unique value: route brainstorming to a reasoning-capable model (Claude Opus, Gemini Pro) and executing-plans to a speed-optimized model (Claude Haiku, Gemini Flash), matching the model to the cognitive demands of each phase.

Here is a concrete example — adapting the systematic-debugging skill to a Kilo Code custom mode. In Kilo Code's custom mode configuration (typically in .kilocode/modes/ as a YAML or JSON file):

# .kilocode/modes/systematic-debugging.yaml
name: "Systematic Debugging"
slug: systematic-debugging
model: claude-opus-4-7           # reasoning-heavy: root cause analysis benefits from depth
roleDefinition: |
  You are a systematic debugger. When diagnosing bugs, you follow the
  hypothesis cycle: (1) read the failing output and form ONE specific
  hypothesis about root cause — not a list, one hypothesis; (2) write
  the minimal reproducer that would confirm or deny that hypothesis;
  (3) run it; (4) if confirmed, fix the specific root cause; if denied,
  form a new hypothesis and repeat. You do not make random changes.
  You do not fix symptoms. You trace to root cause every time.
customInstructions: |
  Before proposing any fix, state your current hypothesis explicitly
  in the format: "Hypothesis: [root cause]. Evidence: [what led me here]."

Rules as methodology invariants

  • Kilo Code rules add a complementary layer: where modes define behavior per-conversation, rules define behavior across all modes.
  • A rule like "always run tests after any file edit" applies regardless of which mode is active.
  • Use rules for the invariants of your methodology (never commit to main, always verify before declaring done) and modes for the phase-specific behaviors (how to brainstorm, how to debug, how to write plans).
  • Together, modes plus rules give you the full Superpowers methodology in a Kilo Code deployment.

15.10 Adapting to Gemini CLI — GEMINI.md and activate_skill

Gemini CLI's skill system uses the same SKILL.md format, but the integration requires explicit wiring via GEMINI.md.

Registering skills in GEMINI.md

The first step is registering the skills so the agent knows they exist. In your project GEMINI.md (or global ~/.gemini/GEMINI.md), add a section that lists your methodology skills with short descriptions:

## Available Methodology Skills
The following skills are available for this project. Activate them with
activate_skill when the task matches.

- **brainstorming** — activate before any creative feature work
- **writing-plans** — activate when given a spec to decompose into a plan
- **systematic-debugging** — activate when diagnosing a bug or unexpected behavior
- **verification-before-completion** — activate before declaring any task done
- **test-driven-development** — activate for any new implementation task

Activation and audit trail

  • When a task matches, the agent calls activate_skill("systematic-debugging") (or the appropriate skill name), loading the full SKILL.md body into active context; from that point, the skill's instructions govern the agent's behavior.
  • Explicit activation shows up in the conversation log — useful for auditing which methodology steps were applied to which tasks.

Approximating auto-invocation

  • For teams that want a closer approximation of Claude Code's auto-invocation: add a session-opening instruction in GEMINI.md — "At the start of every session, activate the using-superpowers skill to orient yourself to this project's methodology."
  • This is a soft instruction rather than a hard hook, but in practice the model follows it reliably.
  • An alternative: use Gemini CLI's session-start hook (if supported in your version) to inject an orientation message programmatically — verify hook support in your Gemini CLI version before depending on this.

15.11 What you give up outside Claude Code

The auto-invocation gap

  • Auto-invocation via SessionStart hook is the primary feature lost outside Claude Code.
  • In Claude Code, the methodology is on by default; in Gemini CLI and Kilo Code, the methodology is opt-in per task.
  • Auto-invocation creates a floor for methodology compliance that opt-in activation does not.

The drift problem

  • In a team using Gemini CLI, an agent under time pressure will skip the activate_skill("brainstorming") call before diving into implementation, because nothing prevents it.
  • In Claude Code, the brainstorming skill is in the system-reminder context and the agent's orientation makes skipping it feel like a deliberate override rather than a silent omission.
  • Teams migrating from Claude Code to Gemini CLI often report that their methodology compliance degrades unless they add explicit enforcement via GEMINI.md instructions.

Three workarounds for non-Claude Code deployments

  • (1) Explicit mode selection (Kilo Code) — require that the user selects the appropriate mode at task start, making methodology application a visible user action.
  • (2) Explicit activate_skill calls (Gemini CLI) — add GEMINI.md instructions that name the specific skills to activate for each task type, making the default behavior skill-activation rather than skip.
  • (3) Session-start prompt listing the methodology — in GEMINI.md or at session open, enumerate the phases and tell the agent to announce which phase it is in before starting each step; the lowest-fidelity workaround but requires zero additional tooling.

15.12 Writing your first skill

Start with a daily friction point

  • A workflow you correct the agent on repeatedly — common first skills include "always read the existing implementation before answering questions about it" or "always check the latest API docs before using an unfamiliar method."

Here is a concrete example: a skill that enforces committing at the end of every completed task, because agents frequently leave work uncommitted when a session ends.

Create the skill directory and file: mkdir -p ~/.claude/skills/commit-on-complete && touch ~/.claude/skills/commit-on-complete/SKILL.md. Then write the SKILL.md:

---
name: commit-on-complete
description: >
  Use when a task is declared complete. Ensures all work is committed to
  git before closing out the task. Invoke before any "task done" declaration.
---

# Commit on Complete

Before declaring a task complete, you MUST:

1. Run `git status` to identify all modified and untracked files related
   to this task.
2. Stage the relevant files explicitly (never use `git add .` — stage by
   file or directory to avoid committing unrelated changes or secrets).
3. Write a commit message following the project's commit convention.
   If no convention is visible, use conventional commits:
   `type(scope): short description`.
4. Commit and confirm the commit was created with `git log --oneline -1`.
5. Only then declare the task complete.

Do NOT skip this if the changes seem small. Every completed task ends
with a commit.

Testing and refining

  • Restart Claude Code (or open a new session) — the skill appears in your system-reminder context.
  • Test it by completing a small code change — the agent should run through the commit checklist before saying "done."
  • The first skill you write is usually the most revealing: encoding the workflow as agent instructions forces you to articulate exactly what the agent should do, often revealing that your mental model of the workflow was fuzzier than you realized.
  • Refine the SKILL.md based on what you observe; after two or three sessions you will have a skill that reliably enforces the behavior.

15.13 Skill and mode composition — chaining methodology

Skill composition in Claude Code

  • A high-level workflow skill can orchestrate an entire development cycle by instructing the agent to invoke specialist skills in sequence via explicit Skill tool calls.
  • Example: a feature-dev skill might instruct the agent to invoke the Skill tool with brainstorming to explore the problem space, then with writing-plans, then with executing-plans, then with verification-before-completion before declaring done. The user invokes one skill; the methodology handles phase sequencing automatically.
  • The Skill tool is the composition mechanism — there is no special wiki-link syntax; the skill body contains natural-language instructions telling the agent to call Skill with another skill's name at the appropriate step.
  • Both sets of instructions are active simultaneously — good composed skills are aware of each other: the high-level skill sets the task context and delegates the how to the specialist skill, rather than both trying to specify behavior at the same level of detail.

Mode composition in Kilo Code

  • Composition works through the Orchestrator mode: "This task requires brainstorming first — switching to Brainstorming mode, then Planning mode, then Execution mode."
  • More explicit than Claude Code's transparent skill invocation, but gives the user full visibility into which methodology phase is active.
  • For teams that want methodology transparency as a feature (not a bug), Kilo Code's explicit mode-switching is the right implementation; the tradeoff is friction — every phase transition requires a user action or an Orchestrator decision rather than happening silently within a single conversation.

15.14 Testing skills — does it fire?

Failure mode 1 — description that does not trigger

  • The agent makes skill invocation decisions based on description matching — if the description does not match the task as the agent describes it to itself, the skill sits idle.
  • Diagnostic: after writing a skill, ask the agent to describe the task you intend the skill to handle, then compare that description to your skill's description field; if there is no lexical or semantic overlap, the skill will not fire.
  • Fix: rewrite the description to use the same vocabulary the agent uses when it encounters the task type.

Failure mode 2 — overruling skill (fires too broadly)

  • A skill so broadly described that it fires for tasks it should not handle, adding overhead to simple tasks and causing the agent to over-apply methodology.
  • Test: deliberately give the agent a trivial task (a one-word variable rename, a comment typo fix) and watch whether your skill fires.
  • Fix: narrow the description with specificity indicators: "Use when the task involves multiple files or requires design decisions" rather than "Use for coding tasks." The description is a classifier, not a label — write it with classification precision in mind.

Failure mode 3 — skill fires but is silently overridden

  • Surfaces as the agent acknowledging the skill ("I'll follow the systematic-debugging approach") and then immediately reverting to its default behavior.
  • Fix: replace advisory phrasing with imperative requirements in the skill body, and add explicit checkpoints ("Do not proceed to step 2 until you have stated your hypothesis aloud").
  • Test with adversarial prompts ("just quickly fix it, skip the debugging steps") — a well-written skill should survive the user's impatience.

15.15 Hands-on: ship a methodology bundle for your team

Walkthrough · Ship a methodology bundle for your team

This walkthrough encodes one workflow — the PR creation flow — as a Claude Code skill, a Kilo Code mode, and a Gemini CLI session-start prompt. By the end you will have a distributable methodology bundle that any teammate can install regardless of their tool choice.

Step 1: Define the workflow artifact. Before encoding anything, write the workflow as plain English. The PR creation flow for most teams looks like: (1) confirm all tests pass and there are no uncommitted changes; (2) write a PR description that summarizes the change, the motivation, and any testing notes; (3) identify the right reviewers; (4) create the PR and post a brief summary to the team channel. This is the ground truth that all three implementations will encode. Write it in a file called pr-creation-workflow.md — you will reference it throughout the steps.

Step 2: Claude Code skill. Create ~/.claude/skills/create-pr/SKILL.md:

---
name: create-pr
description: >
  Use when creating a pull request or when the user asks to "open a PR",
  "submit for review", or "create a pull request". Enforces the team's
  PR creation checklist before opening the PR.
---

# Create PR

Before creating a pull request, follow this checklist in order:

1. **Tests green**: run the full test suite. If any test fails, stop and
   fix the failure — do not create a PR with a red suite.
2. **Clean state**: run `git status`. All task-related changes must be
   committed. If there are uncommitted changes, commit them with an
   appropriate message before proceeding.
3. **PR description**: write a description that covers:
   - What changed (2-3 sentences)
   - Why it changed (motivation, linked issue if any)
   - How to test it (the reviewer's testing steps)
4. **Reviewer selection**: identify 1-2 appropriate reviewers from recent
   `git log` authors on the affected files.
5. **Create the PR**: use `gh pr create` with the description and reviewers.
6. **Team notification**: post a one-sentence summary to the team Slack
   channel if the change is significant (more than 10 lines changed).

Do not skip any step, even for small PRs.

Step 3: Kilo Code mode. Create .kilocode/modes/create-pr.yaml in your project repository:

name: "Create PR"
slug: create-pr
model: claude-sonnet-4-7
roleDefinition: |
  You are the team's PR creation assistant. Your job is to ensure
  every pull request meets the team's quality bar before it is opened.
  You enforce a strict checklist: tests green, clean git state, complete
  description, appropriate reviewers, PR created, team notified.
customInstructions: |
  Follow the PR creation checklist exactly. At each step, show the
  output of the verification command (test run, git status, etc.) before
  proceeding to the next step. If any step fails, stop and explain what
  needs to be fixed before the PR can be created.

Step 4: Gemini CLI session-start prompt. Add to your project's GEMINI.md:

## PR Creation Methodology
When creating a pull request (any time the user says "create a PR",
"open a PR", or "submit for review"), follow this sequence without
skipping steps:

1. Run the full test suite and confirm it is green.
2. Run `git status` and commit any uncommitted task-related changes.
3. Write a PR description: what changed, why, how to test.
4. Identify reviewers from recent `git log` on affected files.
5. Create the PR with `gh pr create`.
6. Post a summary to the team Slack channel if the change is significant.

If you have the `create-pr` skill available, activate it with
activate_skill("create-pr") before starting.

Step 5: Package and distribute. Put all three files in a repository directory methodology/. Add a README.md with installation instructions for each tool. Publish to your team's internal GitHub. For Claude Code, teammates run /plugin to point to the skill directory (or manually copy the skill to ~/.claude/skills/). For Kilo Code, teammates copy the mode YAML to their project's .kilocode/modes/. For Gemini CLI, teammates paste the GEMINI.md section into their project's GEMINI.md. The same workflow, enforced consistently, regardless of which tool each person on the team uses.

What you've built. A tool-agnostic methodology bundle that encodes one workflow as three first-class artifacts. The pattern scales: apply it to your deployment workflow, your incident response runbook, your database migration procedure. After five or six workflows, you have a complete team methodology that survives tool changes and onboards new teammates automatically — they install the bundle, and the agent already knows how your team works.

15.16 See also

← Ch 7. Plan-Driven Execution ← Ch 10. Safety, Review & Verification ← Ch 11. Multi-Agent & Parallel ← Ch 13. Claude Code Deep Dive ← Ch 14. Gemini CLI Deep Dive → Ch 16. MCP Servers

16. MCP Servers — Using and Building

What you'll learn: what MCP is and why it matters, the three primitives, how to use existing servers, how to build your own (Python canonical example), and how to ship a tiny local-notes MCP server end-to-end.

16.1 What MCP is — in plain terms

The protocol

  • The Model Context Protocol (MCP) is an open protocol, originally authored by Anthropic and now an industry standard adopted across the AI tooling ecosystem, that standardises how LLM-based agents connect to external tools and data sources.
  • Before MCP, every AI coding tool and every enterprise integration had to reinvent its own ad-hoc plugin protocol, resulting in fragmented, non-transferable integrations.
  • MCP defines a single contract: a server exposes capabilities, a client (the AI tool or harness) discovers and calls them, and the LLM receives a structured description of what's available — anyone's MCP server works with anyone's MCP-compatible agent.

Why it matters — ecosystem leverage

  • There are already hundreds of MCP servers — for databases, version control, project management, communication tools, cloud APIs, file systems, and more.
  • When you configure one in Claude Code or Kilo Code, your agent immediately gains the ability to query Jira tickets, post Slack messages, read Confluence pages, or look up GitHub issues — without a single line of integration code on your part.
  • Future-proofs your work: an MCP server built for your team's internal API today will work with whatever agent harness your team adopts next year.

MCP behind the scenes in Claude Code

  • MCP is the reason Claude Code's deferred tools list (those <system-reminder> entries) can include mcp__claude_ai_Atlassian__getJiraIssue alongside mcp__claude_ai_Slack__slack_send_message — each is a tool exposed by an MCP server registered with the harness.
  • The harness discovers the server, fetches its tool list, and injects the tool schemas into the model's context so the model knows what to call.

16.2 The three primitives — tools, resources, prompts

MCP servers expose exactly three kinds of capabilities. Understanding each primitive is essential before you build or configure a server.

PrimitiveWhat it isWhen to use it
Tools Callable functions with side effects. The agent invokes a tool with typed parameters and receives a structured result. Tools can create, update, delete, query, or send — anything that requires executing an action against an external system. Any time the agent needs to do something: create a Jira ticket, send a Slack message, run a database query, push a file to S3, trigger a CI job.
Resources Read-only, referenceable data identified by a URI. The agent (or user) requests a resource by URI and receives its content. Resources are pulled, not called — they don't execute logic, they return data. Any time there is large, stable, or reference content the agent might need to read: a Confluence page, a file from an S3 bucket, a database schema, a configuration document. Resources avoid the overhead of tool round-trips for pure reads.
Prompts Pre-built, guided workflows that the user or harness can invoke by name (typically via a slash command). A prompt template encodes the instructions and any required parameters for a specific, repeatable task. Any time there is a workflow complex enough to benefit from a curated preamble: a standup-update generator, a code-review template, an incident-response runbook. Prompts give non-technical users a safe, structured entry point into agent capabilities.
Tools
Callable functions with side effects — agent invokes to do things in external systems.
Resources
Read-only, URI-referenceable data — pull large stable content without tool round-trips.
Prompts
Guided workflow templates — curated entry points for repeatable team tasks.

When to use each primitive

  • Most MCP servers focus primarily on tools — the most broadly useful primitive for agent-driven work.
  • Resources are the right choice when content is large (several kilobytes of documentation), read-only, and likely to be reused across multiple tool calls in the same session — the agent references the URI rather than re-fetching through a tool each time.
  • Prompts are the least commonly authored but the most valuable for encoding team workflows that less experienced users should follow consistently.

16.3 Transport — stdio vs HTTP/SSE

MCP supports two transport mechanisms that determine how the client and server communicate; the choice has significant operational implications.

Propertystdio (subprocess)Streamable HTTP (remote)
MechanismClient spawns the server as a local subprocess; communicates over stdin/stdout JSON-RPC.Server runs as a persistent HTTP process; client connects via Server-Sent Events for streaming + HTTP POST for calls.
Setup complexityMinimal — just a command and args in your settings JSON.Higher — requires a running service, URL, optional auth headers.
Multi-clientNo — one process per client instance.Yes — multiple agents or users can connect to the same server simultaneously.
LatencyNear-zero IPC latency.Network latency; typically acceptable for human-paced agent tasks.
AuthProcess-level — env vars injected at spawn time.HTTP headers, Bearer tokens, OAuth; configurable per-request.
Best forLocal developer tools, personal productivity servers, anything that runs on the developer's machine.Team-shared servers, servers that need to access internal network resources, production integrations.

Which to choose

  • For the vast majority of servers you author yourself — local-productivity or team-internal servers — stdio is the right default: no networking, no authentication infrastructure, the server binary is just a script you run.
  • HTTP/SSE becomes necessary when multiple team members need to share a single server instance (e.g., a central integration with your company's internal API that requires credentials you don't want to distribute), or when the server needs to maintain state across multiple client connections.

16.4 Existing MCP server tour — Atlassian, Slack, Box, Figma, GitHub

Atlassian MCP server

  • Surfaces the full breadth of Jira and Confluence operations.
  • Jira: search issues with JQL (searchJiraIssuesUsingJql), fetch individual issues, create and edit issues, transition issues through workflows, add comments, and log work.
  • Confluence: get and search pages, create new pages, add inline and footer comments, traverse page hierarchies.
  • If your team uses Jira for backlog management, probably the highest-leverage integration you can add: agents can read ticket context before writing code and update ticket status when done.

Slack MCP server

  • Exposes channel reading, thread reading, user profile lookup, message sending (including drafts and scheduled sends), channel and user search, and canvas creation and editing.
  • Indispensable for an agent doing team-facing work — posting standup updates, sending PR notifications, surfacing deployment summaries to a channel.
  • Requires appropriate OAuth scopes for the workspace; read-only scopes suffice for most ambient-awareness use cases, while send scopes require explicit OAuth approval.

Box MCP server

  • Provides file and folder CRUD operations, content retrieval, metadata templates, search (by keyword or metadata), collaboration listing, and file commenting.
  • Gives agents the ability to retrieve reference materials, upload generated artifacts, and maintain a structured audit trail — useful if your team stores documentation, contracts, or design assets in Box.

Figma MCP server

  • Enables read access to Figma design files, component libraries, and diagrams (including FigJam boards).
  • With authentication, agents can retrieve design specs, inspect component properties, and extract style tokens.
  • Primary use case: bridging design and code — an agent implementing a UI component can fetch the exact spec from Figma rather than relying on a screenshot or human description.

GitHub MCP server

  • Wraps the GitHub REST and GraphQL APIs to expose repository operations, issue and PR management, code search, file content retrieval, and workflow status.
  • Frequently the first MCP server you should add for software development work: the agent can look up open issues, read PR comments, fetch file contents from a branch, and create or update issues without leaving the conversation.

16.5 Adding an MCP server to Kilo Code and Claude Code

Both tools use a JSON configuration file to register MCP servers. The structure is nearly identical; the differences are in where the file lives and what the top-level key is called.

Claude Code registration

Claude Code reads MCP server configuration from ~/.claude/settings.json (global) or .claude/settings.json (project-local). Add a mcpServers key:

{
  "mcpServers": {
    "local-notes": {
      "command": "python",
      "args": ["/path/to/notes_server.py"],
      "env": {
        "NOTES_DB_PATH": "/Users/you/notes.db"
      }
    },
    "github": {
      "command": "npx",
      "args": ["-y", "@modelcontextprotocol/server-github"],
      "env": {
        "GITHUB_PERSONAL_ACCESS_TOKEN": "ghp_..."
      }
    }
  }
}
  • You can also use the /mcp command interactively inside Claude Code to list, add, and inspect registered servers without editing JSON by hand; changes made via /mcp write back to the same settings file.

Kilo Code registration

Kilo Code uses a similar structure in the Kilo Code settings (accessible via the Kilo Code panel → Settings → MCP Servers). The configuration JSON looks identical to Claude Code's mcpServers block — same command, args, env keys; you can paste the same stdio snippet into Kilo's MCP settings UI and it will work without modification. For project-level configuration, Kilo Code also reads from .kilocode/mcp.json in the workspace root:

{
  "mcpServers": {
    "local-notes": {
      "command": "python",
      "args": ["${workspaceFolder}/tools/notes_server.py"],
      "env": {
        "NOTES_DB_PATH": "${workspaceFolder}/.notes.db"
      }
    }
  }
}

Server startup and troubleshooting

  • After saving, both tools automatically spawn the server subprocess on the next tool call that touches it.
  • If the server fails to start, check the MCP logs panel (Kilo Code) or run /mcp in Claude Code to see the error output from the subprocess.

16.6 Authoring an MCP server — Python or TypeScript

Anthropic provides official SDKs for both Python (mcp on PyPI) and TypeScript (@modelcontextprotocol/sdk on npm). Both expose the same protocol surface through idiomatic language patterns, and both support stdio and HTTP/SSE transports out of the box.

Python

  • Natural choice when your server needs to interact with ML models, data science libraries, internal Python-based systems, or when the team is primarily Python-fluent.
  • Install with pip install mcp; requires Python 3.10+ (verify the current package and version on PyPI).
  • Programming model uses async def handlers decorated with SDK decorators — familiar to anyone who has written FastAPI or asyncio code.
  • Because most AI-adjacent tooling (LangChain, HuggingFace, custom model wrappers) lives in the Python ecosystem, Python MCP servers tend to be the right fit for ML-adjacent integrations.

TypeScript

  • Right choice when your server needs to integrate deeply with the npm ecosystem, when the rest of your frontend or backend is TypeScript/Node, or when you want to publish to npm for easy team distribution.
  • The TS SDK mirrors the Python SDK's structure with TypeScript-idiomatic wrappers; see the official MCP quick-start at modelcontextprotocol.io/quickstart/server for both languages side by side.
  • All examples in this chapter use Python because it requires less toolchain overhead for a first server.

16.7 The server skeleton — handlers, schemas, errors

Every MCP server, regardless of what it does, has the same structural skeleton: it declares its capabilities, registers handlers for each capability type, and runs a transport loop. Here is the minimal Python skeleton using the mcp SDK:

from mcp.server import Server
from mcp.server.stdio import stdio_server
from mcp.types import Tool, TextContent
import asyncio

app = Server("my-server")

@app.list_tools()
async def list_tools() -> list[Tool]:
    return [
        Tool(
            name="hello",
            description="Returns a greeting. Call this to test the server is alive.",
            inputSchema={
                "type": "object",
                "properties": {
                    "name": {"type": "string", "description": "Name to greet"}
                },
                "required": ["name"]
            }
        )
    ]

@app.call_tool()
async def call_tool(name: str, arguments: dict) -> list[TextContent]:
    if name == "hello":
        return [TextContent(type="text", text=f"Hello, {arguments['name']}!")]
    raise ValueError(f"Unknown tool: {name}")

async def main():
    async with stdio_server() as (read, write):
        await app.run(read, write, app.create_initialization_options())

if __name__ == "__main__":
    asyncio.run(main())

Extending the skeleton

  • Adding resources: decorate async handlers with @app.list_resources() and @app.read_resource().
  • Adding prompts: @app.list_prompts() and @app.get_prompt().
  • The SDK handles protocol framing, capability negotiation, and transport plumbing — your only job is to implement the handlers.
  • TypeScript equivalent: server.setRequestHandler(ListToolsRequestSchema, ...) and server.setRequestHandler(CallToolRequestSchema, ...) — see the official TS quick-start for the verbatim equivalent.

Error handling

  • Raise a standard Python exception from call_tool — the SDK will catch it and return a proper MCP error response to the client.
  • For user-facing errors (e.g., "note not found"), return a TextContent with the error message rather than raising, so the agent can read and reason about the error in its next turn rather than treating it as a fatal fault.

16.8 Designing tools agents use well

The key insight

  • The model reads your tool descriptions — the description field of each Tool object and the description fields of each parameter in the JSON schema are injected verbatim into the model's context.
  • They are not documentation for humans; they are instructions to the model — a vague description produces unreliable tool calls; a precise description produces reliable ones.

Good tool design principles

  • Give each tool a single, clearly bounded purpose.
  • Name it with a verb that describes what it does (create_note, search_notes, not notes_operation).
  • Write the description as if instructing a junior developer: "Creates a new note with the given title and body. Returns the note ID. Call this when the user asks to save, record, or note something."
  • Keep parameter schemas narrow — use enums for fixed choices, mark only truly required fields as required, and describe each parameter's purpose and acceptable range.
  • Make tools idempotent where possible so the model can safely retry a call if it loses the result.
Anti-pattern: the kitchen-sink tool. A single tool called manage_notes with a required action parameter that accepts "create", "update", "delete", "list", or "search" is a common beginner mistake. The model has to make two decisions — which tool to call, and which action to pass — and the parameter schemas for each action are different but all stuffed into one schema. The result is frequent incorrect calls, incorrect action values, and missing required parameters. Split it: one tool per action. The agent's decision of which tool to call is made at the selection step where the model is most reliable; the "what to pass" decision then has a clean, unambiguous schema.

16.9 Authoring resources — when they fit better than tools

When resources are the right choice

  • When your server has read-only data the agent might want to reference multiple times across a session, or data large enough that streaming it as a resource (identified by URI) is preferable to embedding it in a tool call response.
  • Key distinction: a tool call is a one-off action; a resource is a named, stable thing that can be referenced by URI — the agent (or the harness) can fetch a resource once, cache it for the session, and reference it by URI in subsequent calls without re-fetching.

Concrete examples

  • A server exposing your team's architecture decision records (each ADR as a resource URI like adr://decisions/004-use-postgres).
  • A server exposing your API's OpenAPI spec (openapi://spec/v3).
  • A server exposing database schema documentation.
  • In each case, content is large, read-only, and reference material — not something that needs to be re-generated on each call.

Implementation

  • Implement @app.list_resources() to return a list of available URIs and @app.read_resource() to return content for a given URI.
  • Content can be text or binary (base64-encoded); use TextContent for text and BlobResourceContents for binary (class names track the SDK version — check the current mcp types).

16.10 Authoring prompts — guided workflows

How prompts work

  • Pre-built, parameterised message templates invokable by name via a /prompt-name command in the chat UI or programmatically via the harness's prompt invocation API.
  • The MCP server receives the invocation with any provided arguments and returns a list of messages that the harness injects into the conversation as if the user had typed them.
  • Allows you to encode multi-step, carefully worded workflows that less experienced users can trigger without knowing the exact phrasing.

Implementation and example

  • Implement @app.list_prompts() to return available prompt templates and @app.get_prompt() to return the rendered messages for a given prompt name and arguments.
  • Example: a well-designed standup generator prompt accepts a since date parameter and returns a system message instructing the agent to fetch recent commits, recent Jira completions, and current blockers, then format them in the team's standup format — the user types /standup since=yesterday rather than having to remember and type all of that — though the exact argument syntax (positional vs key=value) is host-specific.

Harness integration

  • In Claude Code, slash commands that map to MCP prompts are surfaced when the server is registered and the harness discovers the prompt list — verify with /mcp to see what prompts are available from each registered server.
  • In Kilo Code, MCP prompts appear in the prompt library accessible from the chat UI.

16.11 Auth and secrets in MCP servers

Environment variables (preferred pattern)

  • Right choice for the vast majority of MCP server authentication scenarios.
  • Inject an API key (GitHub PAT, Slack Bot Token, internal API key) as an env var in the server's registration config ("env": {"GITHUB_TOKEN": "..."} in the settings JSON); the server reads os.environ["GITHUB_TOKEN"] at startup or at call time.
  • The key lives in the settings file (the developer's home directory, not source control), never appears in the server code, never appears in conversation context, and is scoped to the local machine.

OAuth (when needed)

  • Necessary when the server needs to act on behalf of a specific user rather than a service account — user-scoped APIs like personal Google Drive, user-specific Jira permissions, or Slack DMs on behalf of a user.
  • Adds significant implementation complexity: authorization code flow, token storage and refresh, handling token expiry during long-running sessions.
  • For most team-internal MCP servers, a service account API key (env var) is the right answer and OAuth is unnecessary overhead.
  • When you do need OAuth, consult the official docs at modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/concepts/authentication for the current recommended implementation pattern, as the OAuth profile for MCP is still evolving.

Hard rules

  • Never hardcode secrets in the server script itself.
  • Never log secrets in the server's stdout — the MCP client reads stdout as the protocol stream, so any debug output containing a secret will be visible in the harness's MCP log viewer.
  • Use stderr for all debug and log output; the MCP SDK routes stderr to the harness's log panel, separate from the protocol stream.
Tool output is untrusted input. Whatever your server returns becomes part of the model's context, so a server that relays third-party data (web pages, tickets, emails, DB rows) can carry indirect prompt injection — text crafted to hijack the agent into calling other tools. Don't assume returned content is benign: scope each tool narrowly, keep destructive actions behind confirmation, and treat any server that proxies external data as a potential injection vector.

16.12 Testing your MCP server

Unit tests for handlers

  • Each handler is an async function that takes plain Python arguments and returns typed objects — test them directly without running the full transport stack.
  • Mock the database or external API calls, call handler functions directly in pytest-asyncio tests, and assert on the returned TextContent or Tool lists.
  • Covers the logic of your server without any MCP infrastructure.

Integration testing with MCP Inspector

  • The official tool: run npx @modelcontextprotocol/inspector python your_server.py — opens a browser-based UI that lets you browse the server's tool/resource/prompt list, invoke tools with arbitrary parameters, and inspect the raw JSON-RPC messages.
  • The fastest way to verify that your server's schemas are correct, tool calls return the expected structure, and error handling works.
  • Use the Inspector before registering your server in Claude Code or Kilo Code — it will catch schema errors and missing fields that would otherwise show up as cryptic "tool not found" errors in the harness.

Automated integration tests for CI

  • The Python MCP SDK includes test utilities that let you create an in-process client/server pair without a real transport: instantiate your server and a test client, run them in the same event loop, and call tools programmatically.
  • Appropriate for CI pipelines where you want to test the full MCP protocol path (capability negotiation, tool listing, tool calling, error propagation) without spawning a subprocess.

16.13 Publishing and distribution

PyPI and npm (broad distribution)

  • Standard channels for MCP servers intended for broad use.
  • Python server published to PyPI: install with pip install your-server-package and register with "command": "python", "args": ["-m", "your_server_package"].
  • TypeScript server published to npm: register with "command": "npx", "args": ["-y", "your-npm-package"] — downloads and runs the latest version automatically.
  • For internal team distribution, a private PyPI repository or npm registry is the cleanest solution; alternatively, install directly from a Git URL (pip install git+https://github.com/your-org/your-server.git).

GitHub Releases (team-specific servers)

  • Appropriate for servers too team-specific or deployment-specific to warrant a PyPI/npm package.
  • Tag a release, include installation instructions in the release notes, and have teammates clone the repo and configure the command path directly.
  • The right pattern for servers that require project-specific configuration at installation time.

MCP Registry

  • The growing community index of publicly available MCP servers at registry.modelcontextprotocol.io.
  • If you build a server that might benefit others outside your team, submit it to the registry to make it discoverable.
  • Check the registry before building a new server — there is a good chance someone has already built and published a server for the API you need.

16.14 Hands-on: build a tiny local-notes MCP server

Walkthrough · Build a tiny local-notes MCP server

Goal. Build a Python MCP server with three tools — add_note, list_notes, search_notes — backed by a SQLite database. Register it in Claude Code. Invoke it. Verify it works.

Step 1: Install the SDK.

pip install mcp

Step 2: Write notes_server.py. Save this to ~/tools/notes_server.py (or any stable path):

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Local notes MCP server — SQLite-backed."""
import os
import sqlite3
import asyncio
from datetime import datetime, timezone
from mcp.server import Server
from mcp.server.stdio import stdio_server
from mcp.types import Tool, TextContent

DB_PATH = os.environ.get("NOTES_DB_PATH", os.path.expanduser("~/.notes.db"))

def get_db() -> sqlite3.Connection:
    conn = sqlite3.connect(DB_PATH)
    conn.execute(
        "CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS notes "
        "(id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT, "
        " title TEXT NOT NULL, "
        " body TEXT NOT NULL, "
        " created_at TEXT NOT NULL)"
    )
    conn.commit()
    return conn

app = Server("local-notes")

@app.list_tools()
async def list_tools() -> list[Tool]:
    return [
        Tool(
            name="add_note",
            description=(
                "Saves a new note with a title and body to the local notes database. "
                "Call this when the user asks to save, record, remember, or note something. "
                "Returns the ID of the created note."
            ),
            inputSchema={
                "type": "object",
                "properties": {
                    "title": {"type": "string", "description": "Short title for the note"},
                    "body":  {"type": "string", "description": "Full content of the note"}
                },
                "required": ["title", "body"]
            }
        ),
        Tool(
            name="list_notes",
            description=(
                "Returns all notes in the database, newest first. "
                "Call this when the user asks to see, show, or list their notes. "
                "Returns a numbered list with IDs, titles, and creation dates."
            ),
            inputSchema={"type": "object", "properties": {}, "required": []}
        ),
        Tool(
            name="search_notes",
            description=(
                "Searches notes by keyword in title or body. Case-insensitive. "
                "Call this when the user wants to find a specific note or recalls partial content. "
                "Returns matching notes with their IDs, titles, and body previews."
            ),
            inputSchema={
                "type": "object",
                "properties": {
                    "query": {"type": "string", "description": "Search term to look for in title or body"}
                },
                "required": ["query"]
            }
        ),
    ]

@app.call_tool()
async def call_tool(name: str, arguments: dict) -> list[TextContent]:
    conn = get_db()
    try:
        if name == "add_note":
            now = datetime.now(timezone.utc).isoformat()
            cur = conn.execute(
                "INSERT INTO notes (title, body, created_at) VALUES (?, ?, ?)",
                (arguments["title"], arguments["body"], now)
            )
            conn.commit()
            return [TextContent(type="text",
                                text=f"Note saved. ID: {cur.lastrowid}")]

        elif name == "list_notes":
            rows = conn.execute(
                "SELECT id, title, created_at FROM notes ORDER BY id DESC"
            ).fetchall()
            if not rows:
                return [TextContent(type="text", text="No notes yet.")]
            lines = [f"[{r[0]}] {r[1]} ({r[2][:10]})" for r in rows]
            return [TextContent(type="text", text="\n".join(lines))]

        elif name == "search_notes":
            q = f"%{arguments['query']}%"
            rows = conn.execute(
                "SELECT id, title, body FROM notes "
                "WHERE title LIKE ? OR body LIKE ? ORDER BY id DESC",
                (q, q)
            ).fetchall()
            if not rows:
                return [TextContent(type="text", text="No matching notes.")]
            lines = [f"[{r[0]}] {r[1]}: {r[2][:80]}..." for r in rows]
            return [TextContent(type="text", text="\n".join(lines))]

        else:
            return [TextContent(type="text", text=f"Unknown tool: {name}")]
    finally:
        conn.close()

async def main():
    async with stdio_server() as (read, write):
        await app.run(read, write, app.create_initialization_options())

if __name__ == "__main__":
    asyncio.run(main())

Step 3: Test with the MCP Inspector. Before registering with Claude Code, verify the server works:

npx @modelcontextprotocol/inspector python ~/tools/notes_server.py

Open the Inspector URL in your browser. In the Tools panel, you should see add_note, list_notes, and search_notes. Call add_note with {"title": "test", "body": "hello from inspector"} and confirm you get back a note ID. Call list_notes and verify the note appears.

Step 4: Register with Claude Code. Add to ~/.claude/settings.json:

{
  "mcpServers": {
    "local-notes": {
      "command": "python",
      "args": ["/Users/you/tools/notes_server.py"],
      "env": {
        "NOTES_DB_PATH": "/Users/you/.notes.db"
      }
    }
  }
}

Replace /Users/you with your actual home directory path.

Step 5: Invoke and verify. Start a new Claude Code session. Run /mcp — you should see local-notes listed as a connected server with 3 tools. Then say:

Please add a note titled "MCP chapter done" with body "Finished Ch 16 of Hero to Architect".

Claude Code will call add_note and return the note ID. Then say:

List all my notes.

You should see the note you just created. Finally:

Search my notes for "architect".

The note appears in search results. Your local-notes MCP server is live and working end-to-end.

What to extend next. Add an update_note tool and a delete_note tool. Add a resource that exposes the full notes list as a URI (notes://all) so the agent can reference it without calling a tool. Add a daily-summary prompt that renders a standup-style summary of notes created today. Each of these extensions follows exactly the same SDK patterns shown above.

16.15 See also

← Ch 3. Tools, Side Effects & the Agent's World ← Ch 12. Kilo Code Deep Dive (MCP in Kilo) ← Ch 13. Claude Code Deep Dive (MCP in Claude Code) ← Ch 14. Gemini CLI Deep Dive (MCP in Gemini) → Ch 17. Worked Example end-to-end

17. Worked Example: Ship gitlog-report End-to-End

What you'll learn: how all the methodology layers and tools combine into a single ship-able feature, shown three ways — once in Kilo Code (full), once in Claude Code (full), once in Gemini CLI (highlights). Cost, time, and ease compared side by side with concrete numbers.

17.1 The brief — what we're building (gitlog-report)

Across Ch 6–11 you have seen gitlog-report appear in six mini-walkthroughs: specced in Ch 6, planned in Ch 7, test-driven in Ch 8, debugged in Ch 9, reviewed in Ch 10, and polished in parallel in Ch 11. This chapter runs all three passes of the full development lifecycle on the same codebase, from first commit to merged PR, so you can see exactly how the methodology layers nest.

The seven acceptance criteria from the spec produced in Ch 6.11 (AC1–AC7), restated here — and extended with two delivery criteria (performance and test coverage) — as the authoritative contract all three passes must satisfy:

  • Reads git log from the current directory or --path <dir>
  • Per-author summary: commit_count, files_changed, insertions, deletions
  • Top 5 most-touched files per author by total line delta (top_files)
  • JSON output by default; Markdown report via --format md
  • --since <ISO-date> and --until <ISO-date> date range filters
  • Merge commits: counted in commit total, excluded from line counts by default (opt-in via --include-merges)
  • Handles empty repository gracefully — zero-row output, exit code 0
  • Completes under 5 seconds on a 10,000-commit repository
  • Tested: at minimum the four test cases from Ch 8.11 (empty repo, single author, multi-author, date filter)

Why written criteria matter

  • These nine bullets — the seven criteria from Ch 6.11 plus the performance and test-coverage criteria — are the acceptance contract; all three passes below must satisfy all nine.
  • Any pass that ships without satisfying them is incomplete — that is the point of writing them down before touching code.

17.2 Why multiple passes — different surfaces, different lessons

Pass 1 · Kilo Code
IDE, mode-driven (Architect → Code → Debug), full pipeline.
Pass 2 · Claude Code
Terminal, parallel subagents + worktrees, /ultrareview.
Pass 3 · Gemini CLI
Free tier, 1M context window, highlights vs Pass 2.

What each tool uniquely exposes

  • Kilo Code is IDE-embedded, mode-switching, and optimised for tight edit-test loops inside a project that is already open.
  • Claude Code is terminal-first, operates on the whole repository without an IDE host, and has first-class multi-agent dispatch.
  • Gemini CLI has a free-tier cost model and a 1M-token context window that changes how you think about compaction and context strategy.
  • No tool dominates on all three dimensions — cost, speed, and ergonomics. That is the lesson the comparison table in §17.13 is designed to make concrete.

Why the same feature in all three environments

  • Real engineering teams are not monolithic — some teammates live in their IDE; some live in the terminal; some are on free-tier accounts.
  • Showing the same feature end-to-end in each environment means this chapter is relevant regardless of which tool you reach for.
  • The methodology — spec first, plan before coding, TDD as the control rod, review before merge, parallel only when truly independent — is identical in all three passes. Only the surface syntax changes.
1
Brainstorm + Spec

Write the acceptance criteria before touching code. Architect mode or superpowers:brainstorming → OpenSpec / SpecKit output.

2
Plan

Decompose into TDD-ordered tasks naming exact files and verification commands. Review with the five-point checklist from Ch 7.6.

3
TDD

Write failing test → confirm red → implement → verify green → commit. Repeat per task. The test suite is the control rod.

4
Debug

Inject or encounter a real bug. Use Debug mode / /systematic-debugging to isolate root cause before touching code.

5
Review

Agent review, /security-review, or /ultrareview. Block on real findings; every fix is a quality investment, not a delay.

6
Ship

Open PR with gh pr create, confirm CI green, merge. Capture wall-clock time and token cost for the pass comparison.

17.3 Pass 1 (Kilo Code) — brainstorm + spec + plan

Setup and mode context

  • Open your project in VS Code with the Kilo Code extension active.
  • The acceptance criteria from §17.1 are already written — they came from the Ch 6.11 walkthrough, which produced openspec/specs/gitlog-report.md.
  • Switch Kilo Code to Architect mode — the mode designed for design-level work without direct file edits.
  • The relevant mode mechanics are covered in Ch 12; the short version: Architect mode can read all files but will not write them without your approval.

Paste the brief:

You are in Architect mode. Read openspec/specs/gitlog-report.md.
Produce a numbered implementation plan (PLAN.md) for the gitlog-report CLI.
Decompose into tasks suitable for TDD order:
  - write the failing test → run it (confirm it fails) → implement → verify → commit.
Each task should name the exact files touched and the verification command.
Do not write any implementation code yet.

Reviewing the Architect output

  • Kilo Code's Architect agent reads the spec, reads the existing repo structure, and produces PLAN.md.
  • Review it using the five-point checklist from Ch 7.6: right files? right order? complete code in steps? no placeholders? verification steps?
  • The typical Architect output will have 6–8 tasks, roughly: (1) scaffold gitlog_report/ package, (2) implement parse_gitlog() with the four Ch 8.11 tests, (3) implement the CLI entry point with argparse, (4) JSON + Markdown formatter, (5) --include-merges flag, (6) performance validation.

Spec vs plan — keeping them separate

  • Once you approve the plan (close Architect, open Code mode), the agent will not re-read the spec — it will read PLAN.md.
  • This is intentional: the spec is the contract; the plan is the recipe. The agent executes the recipe. Keep them separate.
# openspec/changes/add-gitlog-report/ already exists from Ch 6.11 workflow
# Accept and archive the change so the spec is canonical:
openspec accept add-gitlog-report
openspec archive add-gitlog-report
# → openspec/specs/gitlog-report.md is now the live spec

17.4 Pass 1 — TDD in Code mode

Switching to Code mode

  • Switch Kilo Code to Code mode and feed it the plan.
  • Code mode can read and write files — this is where the implementation happens.
  • The agent works through PLAN.md task by task; your job is to watch, gate each task's output, and intervene if it deviates from the plan.

Task 2 — confirm tests fail first

  • Task 2 in the plan is the TDD task.
  • The four tests from Ch 8.11 are the scaffold — they already exist in tests/test_gitlog.py from the walkthrough.
  • The agent should confirm they fail before writing any implementation:
$ pytest tests/test_gitlog.py -v
FAILED tests/test_gitlog.py::test_empty_repo_returns_empty_dict - ImportError: No module named 'gitlog_report'
FAILED tests/test_gitlog.py::test_single_author_commit_count - ImportError
FAILED tests/test_gitlog.py::test_multi_author_counts - ImportError
FAILED tests/test_gitlog.py::test_date_filter_excludes_commits_before_start_date - ImportError
4 failed in 0.12s

Implementation and green confirmation

  • All four failing — correct.
  • The agent implements gitlog_report/parser.py with parse_gitlog() using subprocess to call git log --numstat, aggregating per-author stats into a dict.
  • After implementation, the test run should show all green:
$ pytest tests/test_gitlog.py -v
PASSED tests/test_gitlog.py::test_empty_repo_returns_empty_dict
PASSED tests/test_gitlog.py::test_single_author_commit_count
PASSED tests/test_gitlog.py::test_multi_author_counts
PASSED tests/test_gitlog.py::test_date_filter_excludes_commits_before_start_date
4 passed in 0.83s

Remaining tasks and token cost

  • The agent continues through the remaining plan tasks: CLI entry point (gitlog_report/__main__.py), JSON and Markdown formatters (gitlog_report/formatters.py), the --include-merges flag, and the performance test.
  • Commit SHA after Task 6 completes: a3f82c1.
  • Token usage at this point: approximately 88,000 tokens consumed across the Code-mode session.

17.5 Pass 1 — inject a bug, debug it in Debug mode

Why inject a real bug here

  • This step mirrors Ch 9.11 exactly — the point is to practice the debugging loop in the context of a real ship cycle, not just as an isolated exercise.

Inject the off-by-one bug from Ch 9.11 into parser.py:

# BEFORE (correct)
if commit_date >= start_date:

# AFTER (injected bug — off-by-one, boundary commit excluded)
if commit_date > start_date:

What the bug triggers

  • Run the tests — three pass; one fails.
  • The failure is exactly the date-filter boundary test from Ch 8.11, because it was written specifically on the boundary condition:
FAILED tests/test_gitlog.py::test_date_filter_excludes_commits_before_start_date
AssertionError: Expected author Alice with 2 commits in window [2024-01-10, None].
Got: {'Alice': {'commit_count': 1, ...}}

Debug mode walkthrough and resolution

  • Switch Kilo Code to Debug mode — a read-only-first mode that analyses before proposing edits, matching the systematic debugging loop.
  • Feed it the failing test output and walk it through the five stages from Ch 9: Reproduce → Isolate → Root Cause → Fix → Verify.
  • The agent identifies the root cause (single character difference) without needing more than two turns.
  • The fix is one character: > to >=. Verify all four tests pass.
  • Commit SHA: b7d14e2. This debug cycle consumed approximately 18,000 tokens in Kilo's Debug mode.

17.6 Pass 1 — review pass

Review mode setup

  • Switch to Architect mode — or a dedicated Reviewer mode if your Kilo Code configuration exposes one; both work for read-only review.
  • The review methodology from Ch 7.6's five-point plan-review checklist applies here too, extended by the three-layer review stack from Ch 10: automated static analysis, agent review, and manual spot-check.

Run the review with the Ch 10.11 three-layer approach:

# Layer 1: automated
ruff check gitlog_report/ tests/
mypy gitlog_report/ --strict

# Layer 2: agent review prompt
"Review the diff from HEAD~6..HEAD for gitlog-report.
Check: (1) spec compliance — does each acceptance criterion from
openspec/specs/gitlog-report.md have a test and an implementation?
(2) error handling — what happens if git is not installed, or the path
is not a git repo? (3) performance — will this handle 10,000 commits
under 5 seconds? (4) security — any shell injection risks in the
subprocess calls? (5) completeness — any missing edge cases?"

Agent review finding and fix

  • The agent review surfaces one real finding: the subprocess.run(["git", "log", ...], shell=False) call correctly avoids shell injection, but the --path argument is not validated before being passed to git.
  • A path with special characters will produce an unhelpful git error rather than a clean user-facing message.
  • Fix: add a Path(args.path).is_dir() check before the subprocess call.
  • Commit SHA after fix: c9a3d05. Total Pass 1 token count: approximately 148,000 tokens.

17.7 Pass 1 — ship the PR

With the implementation complete, tests green, and the review finding addressed, ship the PR:

git checkout -b feat/gitlog-report
git add gitlog_report/ tests/ PLAN.md openspec/
git commit -m "feat: add gitlog-report CLI (Pass 1 — Kilo Code)"

gh pr create \
  --title "feat: add gitlog-report CLI" \
  --body "$(cat <<'EOF'
## Summary
- Adds \`gitlog-report\` CLI: per-author git contribution summary
- JSON (default) and Markdown output via \`--format md\`
- Date range filtering: \`--since\` / \`--until\`
- Merge commits opt-in via \`--include-merges\`
- Empty repo handled gracefully (exit 0, zero-row output)

## Test plan
- [x] 4 core tests from Ch 8.11 (empty, single author, multi-author, date filter)
- [x] Performance smoke: 10K commit mock completes in <5s
- [x] Ruff + mypy clean
- [x] Manual smoke: \`gitlog-report --path . --format md\`

## Reviewers
Addresses all 9 acceptance criteria in openspec/specs/gitlog-report.md
EOF
)"

# After approval:
gh pr merge --squash

Merge SHA: d1f6b38. Pass 1 is complete. Wall-clock time from blank branch to merged PR: approximately 47 minutes, including the deliberate debug cycle.

17.8 Pass 2 (Claude Code) — brainstorm + spec + plan

Pass 2 setup and methodology difference

  • Reset to a fresh branch — Pass 2 uses Claude Code exclusively: terminal-first, no IDE.
  • The methodology difference from Pass 1 is the front end: instead of Kilo Code's Architect mode for the brainstorm, we use the superpowers:brainstorming skill explicitly, then SpecKit's /specify for the spec, then Claude Code's plan mode for the plan.
git checkout main
git checkout -b feat/gitlog-report-pass2
claude  # open Claude Code session

In the Claude Code session, invoke the brainstorming skill:

/brainstorming gitlog-report: a CLI that reads git log and produces
per-author contribution summaries (commit_count, files_changed,
insertions, deletions, top_files). JSON + Markdown output.
Date range filters. Handles empty repo. Tested.

What the brainstorm skill surfaces

  • The superpowers:brainstorming skill runs a structured exploration — it identifies ambiguities (how do we handle author email deduplication? what is "top files" sorted by?).
  • It proposes design options: subprocess vs. Python's gitpython library — recommends subprocess for zero-dependency portability.
  • It surfaces three edge cases not in the initial brief: binary files, unborn HEAD, unicode in author names.

After the brainstorm settles, run SpecKit:

/specify gitlog-report

SpecKit output and transition to plan mode

  • SpecKit produces specs/001-gitlog-report/spec.md — identical in structure to the Ch 6.11 output because the acceptance criteria are the same contract.
  • The difference: the brainstorm added the three edge cases to the spec's edge-cases section.

Now switch to plan mode:

/plan implement gitlog-report according to specs/001-gitlog-report/spec.md

Plan review and approval

  • Claude Code's plan mode produces a numbered task list.
  • Apply the Ch 7.6 five-point review. Approve.

17.9 Pass 2 — execute via parallel subagents

Why parallel subagents for initial implementation

  • Pass 2's implementation phase uses parallel subagents from the outset — not as a polish pass (as in Ch 11.11), but for the initial implementation itself.
  • This is appropriate because the three major deliverables (core library, test suite, CLI + docs) are genuinely independent: they touch different files, have no shared mutable state during development, and can be integrated cleanly at the end.

Create three worktrees using the superpowers:using-git-worktrees skill:

git worktree add ../gitlog-feat   -b pass2/feat
git worktree add ../gitlog-tests  -b pass2/tests
git worktree add ../gitlog-docs   -b pass2/docs

Dispatch three subagents in a single orchestrator message — the same pattern as Ch 11.11 but for initial implementation rather than polish:

"Dispatch 3 subagents in parallel using the Agent tool:

Agent A — worktree ../gitlog-feat (branch pass2/feat):
  Implement gitlog_report/parser.py with parse_gitlog() per the spec at
  specs/001-gitlog-report/spec.md. Use subprocess to call git log --numstat.
  Return a dict keyed by author name with keys: commit_count, files_changed,
