  insertions, deletions, top_files (list of [filename, delta] sorted desc).
  Handle empty repo (return {}). Handle --since/--until ISO date filtering
  with >= boundary semantics. Handle --include-merges flag.
  Implement gitlog_report/__main__.py CLI with argparse: --path, --format,
  --since, --until, --include-merges flags.
  Do NOT write tests. Commit when complete.

Agent B — worktree ../gitlog-tests (branch pass2/tests):
  Write tests/test_gitlog.py with the four tests from the spec:
    (1) empty repo returns empty dict
    (2) single author correct commit_count
    (3) multi-author correct counts
    (4) date filter boundary: commit on start_date IS included
  Use the tmp_git_repo pytest fixture pattern (subprocess + git env vars).
  Add edge-case tests: binary files excluded, unicode author names, 10K mock.
  Do NOT implement parser.py. Commit when complete.

Agent C — worktree ../gitlog-docs (branch pass2/docs):
  Write README.md: purpose, install (pip install -e .), usage examples
  (json, --format md, --since 2024-01-01, --include-merges),
  output schema description, and contributing notes.
  Write CHANGELOG.md with initial 0.1.0 entry.
  Do NOT modify source or tests. Commit when complete."

Dispatch timing and integration

  • All three agents run concurrently.
  • Typical wall-clock for this dispatch: approximately 6 minutes (bounded by the longest agent — Agent A).
  • The orchestrator waits, then integrates:
cd ../gitlog-feat && git log --oneline -3
# a91f3c7 feat: implement parser.py and CLI entry point
# ... (2 earlier scaffold commits)

cd ../gitlog-tests && git log --oneline -3
# 3b2d48a test: add core 4 tests + edge cases (binary, unicode, 10K mock)

cd ../gitlog-docs && git log --oneline -3
# e57c901 docs: add README.md and CHANGELOG.md

Merge integration order

  • Merge in order: docs first (zero conflict risk), tests second (test files only), feat last (source changes).
  • Raise three PRs, merge in order, resolve any rebase conflicts on the feat branch before final merge.
  • Integration commit SHA: f2e0c14.

17.10 Pass 2 — verification, /security-review, /ultrareview

After integration, the full test suite runs:

$ pytest tests/ -v
PASSED test_empty_repo_returns_empty_dict
PASSED test_single_author_commit_count
PASSED test_multi_author_counts
PASSED test_date_filter_excludes_commits_before_start_date
PASSED test_binary_files_excluded_from_line_counts
PASSED test_unicode_author_names_handled
PASSED test_10k_mock_completes_under_5s
7 passed in 4.11s

Full test suite after integration

  • All seven tests pass, including the 10K performance mock.

Layer 1 — /security-review (local, synchronous)

  • Run inside the active Claude Code session against the integrated branch — reads the full diff and checks for injection risks, secret exposure, and unsafe subprocess patterns.
  • Finding: str(path) is passed to subprocess.run without resolve(), so a symlink pointing outside the repo could be followed silently.
  • Fix: Path(args.path).resolve(strict=True). One-line change. Commit SHA: g3f71a2.

Layer 2 — /ultrareview (cloud, asynchronous)

  • Dispatch the deep review as a background cloud agent — it runs independently and delivers results to your inbox when done.

In Claude Code:

/ultrareview --target feat/gitlog-report-pass2 --async --notify

Ultrareview findings and resolution

  • The ultrareview agent runs a multi-pass analysis: architecture fit, spec compliance, test coverage gap analysis, performance, and documentation completeness.
  • Results arrive approximately 4 minutes later.
  • Key finding: the top_files computation is O(n·log n) per author but is called inside a loop — for 10K commits with 50 authors it is fine, but the finding notes it could be O(n) with a heap.
  • Accept the finding as a known non-blocking improvement and note it in CHANGELOG.md.
  • Ultrareview result: one blocking finding (already fixed — the resolve(strict=True)), one non-blocking suggestion (heap optimisation). Mark both.
  • Commit SHA after addressing: h4b92c3.

17.11 Pass 2 — ship the PR

gh pr create \
  --base main \
  --head feat/gitlog-report-pass2 \
  --title "feat: add gitlog-report CLI (Pass 2 — Claude Code, parallel subagents)" \
  --body "$(cat <<'EOF'
## Summary
- Implements gitlog-report with parallel subagent development:
  3 worktrees (feat / tests / docs) dispatched in a single orchestrator turn
- Acceptance criteria: all 9 from specs/001-gitlog-report/spec.md satisfied
- Security: /security-review clean (path.resolve(strict=True) added)
- Deep review: /ultrareview — 1 blocking finding addressed, 1 non-blocking noted

## Test plan
- [x] 7 tests pass (4 core + 3 edge cases)
- [x] 10K mock: 4.11s (<5s limit)
- [x] ruff + mypy clean
- [x] Manual smoke: gitlog-report --path . --format md
- [x] /security-review: no blocking findings
- [x] /ultrareview: 1 blocking fixed, 1 non-blocking noted in CHANGELOG

## Parallel implementation notes
Agent A (feat), Agent B (tests), Agent C (docs) ran concurrently in worktrees.
Integration: docs → tests → feat merge order. No conflicts.
EOF
)"

gh pr merge --squash

Merge SHA: i5c04d7. Pass 2 wall-clock from blank branch to merged PR: approximately 31 minutes — faster than Pass 1 despite the deeper review, because the parallel dispatch compressed the implementation phase.

17.12 Pass 3 (Gemini CLI) — highlights only

Pass 3 runs the same project in Gemini CLI. This section covers only what is different from Passes 1 and 2 — not a full walkthrough.

  • Cost: $0 on free tier. For a personal Google account, Gemini CLI uses the Gemini API on the free tier. The entire gitlog-report implementation — roughly the same scope as Pass 2 — consumed zero dollars. This is the most important practical difference for solo developers or small teams without a paid API budget.
  • 1M context: no compaction needed. The single largest ergonomic difference is that Gemini CLI's 1M-token context window means the agent can hold the entire project — all source files, the full git log of the repo, the spec, and the test suite — in working memory simultaneously for the entire session. Passes 1 and 2 both required context compaction at some point (Kilo Code's context window filled around 130K tokens; Claude Code compacted around 180K). Gemini CLI never compacted. The agent's understanding of the codebase remained consistent from the first turn to the last, with no coherence degradation at context boundaries.
  • Gemini Extensions used. The local-notes MCP server from Ch 16.14 was registered in Gemini CLI's ~/.gemini/settings.json — the same mcpServers block structure, identical to Claude Code's. Additionally, a gh-extension (a local shell script wrapping gh CLI commands as Gemini tools) allowed the agent to raise the PR directly without leaving the Gemini session.
  • Plan mode difference. Gemini CLI does not have a dedicated "plan mode" that prevents file writes. The workaround is a system-prompt prefix: "In this turn, produce only a PLAN.md. Do not write any other files. I will confirm before you proceed." It works, but it requires more explicit user discipline than Kilo Code's mode switch or Claude Code's /plan command.
  • Parallel dispatch difference. Gemini CLI does not have a built-in Agent tool for parallel subagent dispatch. The equivalent pattern is sequential: run three separate Gemini CLI sessions in three terminal tabs, each pointed at a different worktree. Less elegant than a single orchestrator message, but the isolation and output are equivalent.
  • Total tokens: ~78K. Because the 1M window avoids repeated re-reading of context that gets compacted and re-injected, the raw token count is lower than Pass 2's 280K despite covering the same implementation surface.

17.13 Side-by-side compare — cost, time, ease

Dimension Pass 1: Kilo Code Pass 2: Claude Code Pass 3: Gemini CLI
Tokens consumed ~148K total
(88K impl, 18K debug, 22K review, 20K plan)		 ~280K total
(parallel dispatch amplifies; 3 agents × ~75K each + integration overhead)		 ~78K total
(1M context avoids compaction re-injection; no repeated context rebuild)
Wall-clock time ~47 min
(includes deliberate debug cycle)		 ~31 min
(parallel dispatch compressed impl phase to ~6 min)		 ~52 min
(sequential worktrees add ~15 min vs parallel dispatch)
Cost USD ~$0.44
(Claude Sonnet 4.x pricing at 148K tokens)		 ~$0.84
(280K tokens; partially offset by caching hits on shared spec context)		 $0.00
(Gemini free tier; 78K tokens well within daily quota)
Ease (1–5) 4 / 5
Mode switching is smooth; debug mode integration is excellent		 4 / 5
Parallel dispatch is powerful but requires worktree discipline		 3 / 5
No plan mode or native parallel dispatch; requires manual discipline
Where it shone Tight IDE integration; Debug mode diagnosed the off-by-one in 2 turns; inline diff review /security-review + /ultrareview pipeline; parallel subagent dispatch; /plan mode Zero cost; 1M context coherence across the full session; no compaction breaks
Where it tripped Context filled at ~130K (compaction); no native parallel dispatch; IDE dependency Highest token cost; worktree setup requires upfront discipline; /ultrareview async latency (~4 min) No plan mode (user-enforced discipline required); sequential parallel workaround adds time; no native subagent dispatch

Headline finding — which tool for which context

  • If you are on a budget, Gemini CLI's free tier is genuinely useful for solo work and the 1M context is a real ergonomic advantage for large repositories.
  • If you need the deepest review pipeline (/ultrareview, /security-review, superpowers skills), Claude Code is the right tool.
  • If you spend most of your day in the IDE, Kilo Code's mode-switching architecture and inline diff workflow reduce context switching.
  • None of these is wrong — they are different cost-capability points on the same design space.

17.14 Lessons learned

Six patterns to repeat and four antipatterns to avoid, drawn from all three passes.

Patterns to Repeat Antipatterns to Avoid
1. TDD as the control rod. The four tests from Ch 8.11 defined the contract before any implementation existed. They caught the Ch 9.11 off-by-one immediately. Without boundary-focused tests, the bug would have shipped. 1. Single-pass without review. Every pass ran at least one review layer (agent review, /security-review, or Architect read-only). The path.resolve(strict=True) finding in Pass 2 was a real security improvement. Shipping without any review layer is always a shortcut you pay for later.
2. Spec the boundary cases explicitly. The --since boundary semantics (>= not >) were in the spec. The boundary test was written because the spec was explicit. Vague specs produce vague tests, which miss boundary bugs. 2. Parallel without isolation. Ch 11.11 and Pass 2 both used worktrees — separate filesystem roots per agent. Running parallel agents on the same working directory produces non-deterministic file conflicts. Isolation is not optional; it is the prerequisite for parallel work being safe.
3. Parallel work pays for 3+ truly independent tasks. Pass 2's dispatch of three worktrees compressed a ~20-minute sequential implementation phase into ~6 minutes of wall clock. The prerequisite is genuine file independence — tests, source, and docs touch different files. Parallelise only when you can draw a clean boundary. 3. Skipping the spec for "simple" features. gitlog-report is small. It would be easy to say "I know what I'm building, I don't need a spec." Both passes that wrote a spec (Pass 1 via OpenSpec, Pass 2 via SpecKit) produced better boundary coverage than a spec-free implementation would have. The spec surfaces edge cases you would not otherwise enumerate.
4. Review findings are code improvements, not obstacles. Both blocking findings (path validation in Pass 1, resolve(strict=True) in Pass 2) improved the production quality of the shipped code. Frame every review finding as a quality investment, not a delay. 4. Treating token cost as the only metric. Pass 3's 78K tokens at $0 looks like an unambiguous win until you factor in the sequential parallel workaround (+15 min wall clock) and the absence of /ultrareview. Optimise for the combination of cost, time, and quality coverage that fits your team's actual constraints.
5. Cumulative arc across chapters. The gitlog-report you shipped in Ch 17 is better than anything you would have produced going straight to implementation. The spec from Ch 6, the plan from Ch 7, the tests from Ch 8, the debug loop from Ch 9, the review from Ch 10, and the parallel pattern from Ch 11 all contributed concrete artefacts or practices. The architecture of Part 2 is that each chapter produces something the next chapter uses.
6. The plan is the agent's recipe. Every deviation from the plan — whether by the agent improvising or by you changing scope mid-execution — should trigger a plan update. A stale plan is a lie the agent executes faithfully. Keep the plan current or abandon it explicitly.

OpenSpec vs SpecKit

Ch 6. Spec-Driven Dev (OpenSpec & SpecKit)

Dimension OpenSpec SpecKit When to pick
Workflow style Git-native; specs live in the repo alongside code Structured CLI with a defined lifecycle (draft → accept → archive) OpenSpec if your team is already git-centric; SpecKit if you want guardrails
Change tracking Git history is the audit trail Explicit change objects with status transitions SpecKit if you need formal approval gates or audit logs
File structure Flat markdown files; author-defined layout Enforced directory layout (specs/, changes/, archive/) SpecKit for multi-author teams; OpenSpec for solo or small teams
Tool support Any editor; no special tooling needed Requires SpecKit CLI installed OpenSpec if you want zero dependencies
Learning curve Near zero 30–60 min to learn lifecycle commands OpenSpec to start; migrate to SpecKit when the team grows

When to plan vs skip

Planning costs time upfront but prevents expensive mid-task course corrections. Skip planning only when the task is genuinely trivial. Use this tree:

Is the task trivial? (single file, < 5 lines, zero risk) ├── YES → Skip planning. Prompt directly. └── NO ├── Is it multi-step? (touches > 1 file or > 1 concern) │ ├── YES → Write a plan. (/writing-plans or PLAN.md) │ └── NO (single step but non-trivial) │ └── Use a structured prompt; no formal PLAN.md needed. └── Is it risky? (touches auth, data loss, public API, config) └── YES (regardless of step count) → Write a plan + review gate.

When to parallelize vs serialize

Parallel dispatch cuts wall-clock time but creates merge complexity. Use this checklist before splitting work across agents. All three conditions must be true to parallelize safely.

Check Question If NO → serialize
Independent? Do the sub-tasks touch different files / modules with no shared state? One task feeds the next — serialize in plan order.
Non-cumulative context? Does each sub-task need only its own context, not the output of the others? Task B reads Task A's output — serialize A then B.
More than 3 tasks? Are there at least 3 independent tasks to justify dispatch overhead? For 1–2 tasks, serialize is simpler and nearly as fast.

Ch 11. Multi-Agent & Parallel

When to engage thinking mode

Thinking mode roughly doubles token cost and latency. Reserve it for decisions where the extra reasoning genuinely changes the outcome. Routine tasks rarely benefit.

Scenario Use thinking mode? Rationale
Architecture choice (DB schema, API design, dependency graph) Yes High-stakes; wrong decision is costly to reverse.
Security review / threat modelling Yes Requires multi-hop reasoning across attack surfaces.
Debugging a hard non-obvious failure Yes (if stuck after 2 normal turns) Extended reasoning surfaces non-obvious root causes.
Writing a spec or plan Optional (Opus without thinking often sufficient) Benefit is marginal unless spec is unusually complex.
Routine code edit or formatting No No benefit; wastes tokens and latency.
Doc writing No Fluency task, not reasoning task.

Cheat Sheets

Quick-reference cards for the major tools and conventions in this guide. Use these when you know the concept and just need to look up the command, the flag, or the file template.

Kilo Code

Kilo Code

Ch 12. Kilo Code Deep Dive

TaskHow
Install VS Code Command Palette → ext install kilocode.kilo-code
Verify: search "Kilo Code" in the Extensions sidebar if the marketplace ID has changed.
Switch mode (keyboard) Varies by Kilo version — check Kilo Code › Keybindings in VS Code settings. Common default: Ctrl+Shift+K (Win/Linux), ⌘⇧K (Mac) — verify in your installed version.
Switch mode (UI) Click the mode badge in the Kilo Code panel header → select Architect / Code / Debug / Ask.
Open command palette Ctrl+Shift+P / ⌘⇧P → type Kilo Code:

Custom mode file template — path: .kilocode/modes/<name>.yaml (verify against your installed version; earlier releases used .kilocodemodes):

# .kilocode/modes/my-reviewer.yaml
name: My Reviewer
slug: my-reviewer
roleDefinition: |
  You are a strict code reviewer focused on security and performance.
  Flag any mutation of shared state and all unhandled errors.
groups:
  - read        # read-only file access
  # - edit      # uncomment to allow edits
  # - browser
  # - command
customInstructions: |
  Always cite the relevant CWE or OWASP category when flagging a security issue.

Claude Code

Ch 13. Claude Code Deep Dive · Ch 15. Superpowers & Skills

Built-in slash commands

CommandWhat it does
/clearWipe conversation context; start fresh in the same session.
/compactSummarise long context in-place to reduce token cost.
/costShow token spend for the current session.
/contextDisplay what files/content are currently in context.
/initBootstrap CLAUDE.md for the current project.
/planEnter plan mode (read-only exploration, no file writes).
/reviewCode-review the current diff — thin wrapper for the code-review skill.
/security-reviewSecurity-focused review of the current diff (skill).
/helpList available slash commands.
/pluginManage installed MCP plugins.
/loopRun a prompt on a repeating interval (skill).
/scheduleCreate a cron-scheduled remote agent (skill).

Hook lifecycle events

EventFires when
SessionStartClaude Code session opens.
PreToolUseBefore any tool (Bash, Edit, Read, …) executes.
PostToolUseAfter a tool returns its result.
StopWhen the assistant turn ends.

Skill frontmatter template

# ~/.claude/skills/<skill-name>/SKILL.md
---
name: my-skill
description: >
  One sentence Claude sees when deciding whether to invoke this skill.
  Be specific: mention the trigger phrase and what the skill produces.
metadata:
  trigger_phrases:
    - "generate a foo"
    - "create foo report"
  produces: "foo-report.md in the project root"
---

## Instructions
...your skill body...

.claude/settings.json template

{
  "permissions": {
    "allow": [
      "Bash(git *)",
      "Bash(npm run *)",
      "Read(**)",
      "Edit(**)"
    ],
    "deny": [
      "Bash(rm -rf *)"
    ]
  },
  "env": {
    "NODE_ENV": "development"
  },
  "hooks": {
    "PostToolUse": [
      {
        "matcher": "Bash",
        "hooks": [
          { "type": "command", "command": "echo 'Bash tool used' >> ~/.claude/audit.log" }
        ]
      }
    ]
  }
}

MCP Servers

Ch 16. MCP Servers

Popular servers — one-line install snippets

ServerInstall / add to config
Atlassian (Jira + Confluence) No npm install needed — add the config block below using the @anthropic-ai/mcp-server-atlassian package or the bundled Claude plugin. Verify package name in Anthropic's plugin registry.
Slack npx -y @slack/mcp-server (or add via Claude Code /plugin).
Box Available as a Claude plugin — install via /plugin → search "Box".
Figma Install via /plugin → search "Figma" or npx @figma/mcp — verify package name.
GitHub npx -y @modelcontextprotocol/server-github
Filesystem npx -y @modelcontextprotocol/server-filesystem /path/to/root

settings.json stdio config snippet

Works for both Claude Code (~/.claude/settings.json) and Kilo Code (VS Code settings.json under kilocode.mcpServers).

// Claude Code — ~/.claude/settings.json
{
  "mcpServers": {
    "github": {
      "type": "stdio",
      "command": "npx",
      "args": ["-y", "@modelcontextprotocol/server-github"],
      "env": {
        "GITHUB_PERSONAL_ACCESS_TOKEN": "<your-pat>"
      }
    },
    "filesystem": {
      "type": "stdio",
      "command": "npx",
      "args": ["-y", "@modelcontextprotocol/server-filesystem", "/Users/me/projects"]
    }
  }
}

// Kilo Code — VS Code settings.json
// "kilocode.mcpServers": { ... same object shape ... }

OpenSpec

Ch 6. Spec-Driven Dev (OpenSpec)

Command reference (verify against openspec --help)

CommandWhat it does

openspec show <id>Print the full spec for the given ID.
openspec validateLint all specs against the schema.

Slash command for change creation

# Verify: command name depends on which OpenSpec skill/plugin is installed
/opsx:propose <change-name>

# Example
/opsx:propose add-rate-limiting-to-api-gateway

Proposal file template

# Proposal: <change-name>
**ID:** PROP-<YYYYMMDD>-<slug>
**Status:** Draft
**Author:** <name>

## Problem statement
<What is broken or missing? Why now?>

## Proposed change
<Describe the change in 2–4 sentences.>

## Alternatives considered
- Option A — <why rejected>
- Option B — <why rejected>

## Acceptance criteria
- [ ] <measurable criterion 1>
- [ ] <measurable criterion 2>

Delta spec template

# Delta Spec: <feature-name>
**Spec ID:** SPEC-<id>
**Parent proposal:** PROP-<id>
**Status:** Active

## Summary
<One paragraph.>

## Changed interfaces
| Interface | Before | After |
|-----------|--------|-------|
| <name>    | <old signature> | <new signature> |

## Invariants that must not break
1. <invariant 1>

## Out of scope
- <item>

SpecKit

Ch 6. Spec-Driven Dev · Ch 7. Plan-Driven Execution

Slash commandProducesReads
/specify spec.md — requirements, constraints, acceptance criteria.
Verify: output path is project root by default.		 Your prompt + project context
/plan plan.md — ordered implementation steps. spec.md
/tasks tasks.md — atomic, trackable task list with checkboxes. plan.md
/implement Code changes across the repo. spec.md + plan.md + tasks.md

File directory pattern (verify — may vary by project)

project-root/
├── spec.md          ← /specify output
├── plan.md          ← /plan output
├── tasks.md         ← /tasks output
└── .speckit/
    └── history/     ← archived spec iterations (if SpecKit creates this)

Superpowers Skills

← Ch 15.3 for the full library tour

SkillOne-liner
brainstorming Explores intent, requirements, and design space before any implementation begins.
writing-plans Turns a spec or requirements brief into a structured, multi-step implementation plan.
executing-plans Runs an approved plan in a separate session with review checkpoints between phases.
subagent-driven-development Breaks a plan into independent tasks and dispatches them to parallel sub-agents.
test-driven-development Writes failing tests first, then iterates on implementation until they pass.
systematic-debugging Structured fault-isolation workflow: reproduce → hypothesise → test → fix → verify.
verification-before-completion Runs the app and observes real behaviour to confirm a change works before closing the task.
requesting-code-review Prepares a clean PR and requests a review using the project's conventions.
receiving-code-review Processes reviewer feedback, applies fixes, and responds to each comment.
finishing-a-development-branch Cleans up, squashes noise commits, updates docs, and opens the final PR.
writing-skills Creates or edits Claude Code skills with correct frontmatter and deployment verification.

Glossary A–Z

Extends the v1 glossary. This list adds advanced-tier terms used throughout the Hero to Architect guide. Each entry includes a chapter link to where the term is covered in depth.

activate_skill
A Gemini CLI MCP tool that loads a named skill into the active session, making its instructions available to the model. Analogous to Claude Code's skill invocation but exposed as an MCP tool rather than a slash command. → Ch 14
Agent tool
A built-in Claude Code tool that spins up a sub-agent in a separate context to handle a delegated subtask. The parent agent resumes once the sub-agent returns its result. → Ch 13
agent loop
The core execution cycle of an AI coding agent: receive prompt → call tool → observe result → decide next action → repeat until the task is complete or the agent stops. Understanding the loop is foundational to reasoning about agent behaviour. → Ch 1
auto-approve
A configuration mode in which the agent executes tool calls (Bash, file writes, etc.) without pausing to ask the user for confirmation. Reduces friction but widens blast radius. → Ch 3
blast radius
The maximum scope of damage a single agent action could cause — e.g., deleting files, pushing to production, or sending messages. Keeping blast radius small (scoped permissions, dry-run flags, reversible operations) is a core safety principle. → Ch 3
BYO model
Bring-Your-Own-Model — the ability to connect an AI coding tool to a model endpoint of your choice (e.g., a self-hosted Llama instance or a Vertex AI deployment) rather than the vendor default. Kilo Code and Gemini CLI both support BYO model configurations. → Ch 12
cache hit rate
The percentage of input tokens served from the prompt cache rather than re-processed. A high cache hit rate reduces latency and cost; optimising for it involves structuring stable context (CLAUDE.md, file trees) at the front of prompts. → Ch 2
CLAUDE.md
A markdown file that Claude Code reads at session start to load persistent project instructions — coding conventions, toolchain commands, constraints, and memory. The primary mechanism for injecting stable context without burning tokens each turn. → Ch 13
Cline
An open-source AI coding extension for VS Code (formerly Claude Dev) that supports multiple model backends, MCP servers, and custom modes. Covered as one of the IDE-native agent tools alongside Kilo Code and Continue.dev. → Ch 5
Context Bank
The conceptual pool of tokens available to the agent across a session — files loaded, conversation history, tool outputs, and CLAUDE.md. Managing what enters and leaves the Context Bank is key to sustaining long tasks without compaction loss. → Ch 12
context compaction
An automatic (or manually triggered) process that summarises a long conversation in-place, discarding raw history and replacing it with a compressed summary. Invoked via /compact in Claude Code; prevents context-window exhaustion on extended tasks. → Ch 2
Continue.dev
An open-source AI coding assistant that runs as a VS Code / JetBrains extension, supports multiple model providers, and offers inline completions alongside a chat interface. → Ch 5
custom mode
A named agent persona with a specific role definition, allowed tool groups, and custom instructions — defined in a YAML file and loaded by Kilo Code. Lets teams create specialised agents (reviewer, migrator, doc writer) without modifying base prompts. → Ch 12
extensions (Gemini)
Gemini CLI's plugin mechanism — packages that add new MCP tools, slash commands, or integrations to the CLI. Installed via gemini extensions install and analogous to Claude Code's plugin system. → Ch 14
fast mode
A Claude Code execution mode that skips confirmation prompts and uses a lighter approval heuristic, prioritising speed over caution. Useful for low-risk, repetitive tasks where round-trip friction is the bottleneck. → Ch 13
GEMINI.md
The Gemini CLI equivalent of CLAUDE.md — a markdown file in the project root that Gemini CLI reads at session start to inject persistent project context, commands, and conventions into the model's system prompt. → Ch 14
golden test
A regression test that captures a known-good output snapshot and fails if the output changes. In AI-assisted TDD, golden tests guard against silent behaviour changes introduced by refactors or model-generated edits. → Ch 8
headless mode
Running Claude Code without an interactive terminal — typically via claude --headless or claude -p — so it can be driven programmatically in CI pipelines, scripts, or orchestration systems. → Ch 13
hooks
Shell commands or scripts that Claude Code executes automatically at lifecycle events (SessionStart, PreToolUse, PostToolUse, Stop). Used to enforce policies, log activity, run linters, or trigger external systems without modifying the agent's instructions. → Ch 13
HTTP/SSE transport
A network-based MCP transport where the client connects to a running server over HTTP and receives streaming responses via Server-Sent Events. Used when the MCP server is remote or must be shared across multiple clients; contrasts with stdio transport. → Ch 16
idempotency
The property of an operation that produces the same result whether run once or many times. Designing agent-driven side effects (file writes, API calls, database mutations) to be idempotent makes retries safe and blast radius predictable. → Ch 3
Junie
JetBrains' AI coding assistant, built into IntelliJ-based IDEs. Covered as part of the IDE-native agent landscape alongside Kilo Code, Cline, and Continue.dev. → Ch 5
Kilo custom modes
Named agent configurations defined in .kilocode/modes/<name>.yaml, each specifying a role definition, allowed tool groups, and custom instructions. A team can maintain a library of modes (reviewer, migrator, doc-writer) checked into the repo. → Ch 12
/loop
A Claude Code skill (slash command) that re-runs a given prompt or sub-command on a repeating interval, keeping the agent active between invocations. Useful for polling, watchdog tasks, or paced batch processing. → Ch 13
MCP
Model Context Protocol — an open standard that defines how AI models connect to external tools, data sources, and services through a client–server architecture. MCP enables any compliant tool to be added to Claude Code, Kilo Code, or Gemini CLI without custom integration code. → Ch 16
MCP inspector
A developer utility (typically npx @modelcontextprotocol/inspector) that lets you connect to an MCP server interactively, browse its registered tools and resources, and call them manually — invaluable for debugging new servers. → Ch 16
MCP registry
A curated index of published MCP servers — e.g., the official MCP server list at modelcontextprotocol.io — where developers can discover, evaluate, and install community-maintained integrations. → Ch 16
MCP resource
An MCP primitive that exposes readable data (files, database records, API responses) to the model. Resources are fetched on demand and injected into context, distinct from tools (which perform actions) and prompts (which provide templates). → Ch 16
MCP tool
An MCP primitive that the model can invoke to perform an action — reading a file, querying a database, sending a Slack message, etc. Each tool has a name, description, and JSON Schema input definition that the model uses to decide when and how to call it. → Ch 16
memory file
A persistent markdown file (e.g., ~/.claude/CLAUDE.md or a project-level CLAUDE.md) that stores facts, preferences, and instructions the agent should carry across sessions. The primary long-term memory mechanism in Claude Code. → Ch 2
OpenSpec
A structured spec-management system that formalises the proposal → spec → implementation lifecycle inside a repository. Specs are versioned markdown files; OpenSpec CLI commands (init, validate, archive) keep the spec set coherent over time. → Ch 6
Orchestrator mode
An agent mode in which the model's primary job is decomposing a high-level task, dispatching subtasks to sub-agents or parallel workers, and synthesising their results — rather than doing the implementation work itself. → Ch 11
output style
A Claude Code configuration that shapes how the model formats its responses — e.g., concise vs. verbose, markdown-heavy vs. plain text. Set in CLAUDE.md or via settings to match team preferences and downstream tooling. → Ch 13
plan mode
A read-only exploration phase in which the agent analyses the codebase, proposes a structured implementation plan, and waits for approval before writing any files. Dramatically reduces wasted work on complex features. → Ch 7
plugin
A Claude Code extension installed via /plugin that adds an MCP server (and its tools/resources) to the session. Plugins are managed through the Claude Code plugin registry and configured in settings.json. → Ch 13
prompt cache
Anthropic's server-side mechanism that stores and reuses the KV representation of a prompt prefix across multiple requests. When a long, stable prefix (system prompt + CLAUDE.md) hits the cache, the model responds faster and the cost drops significantly. → Ch 2
/schedule
A Claude Code skill that creates a cron-scheduled remote agent — a routine that runs at a specified interval without a human in the loop. Useful for nightly reports, periodic health checks, and other recurring automation. → Ch 13
ScheduleWakeup
A Claude Code hook event or internal mechanism that fires when a scheduled agent wakes up to run its routine. Allows hooks and setup logic to execute specifically at the start of a scheduled (non-interactive) session. → Ch 13
SessionStart hook
A Claude Code hook that executes a shell command immediately when a new session opens — before any user prompt is processed. Commonly used to inject dynamic context (git status, branch name, environment info) into the session. → Ch 13
skill
A named, reusable agent behaviour defined in a SKILL.md file under ~/.claude/skills/<name>/. Skills are invoked as slash commands and encapsulate multi-step workflows that would be tedious to re-prompt every time. → Ch 15
slash command
A /keyword typed in the Claude Code (or Gemini CLI) prompt that invokes a built-in feature or installed skill. Built-in slash commands include /plan, /compact, and /clear; skill-based ones are user-defined. → Ch 13
SpecKit
A Claude Code skill suite that provides /specify, /plan, /tasks, and /implement slash commands to walk a feature from requirements to code in a structured, approval-gated pipeline. (Conventions are still stabilizing — verify current commands against the official source.) → Ch 6
spec-driven dev
A development methodology in which a formal specification (requirements, constraints, acceptance criteria) is written and approved before any implementation begins. The spec becomes the authoritative contract that the agent's code must satisfy. → Ch 6
stdio transport
An MCP transport where the client launches the server as a child process and communicates over stdin/stdout. The simplest deployment model — no network required — and the default for locally installed MCP servers. → Ch 16
sub-agent
An agent instance spawned by an orchestrator to handle a scoped subtask in its own context window. Sub-agents report results back to the parent; using them enables true parallelism and prevents context pollution between independent workstreams. → Ch 11
Superpowers
A curated collection of Claude Code skills that implement high-leverage development workflows: brainstorming, writing plans, executing plans, TDD, systematic debugging, verification-before-completion, and more. Maintained as a skill library that teams can adopt wholesale. → Ch 15
thinking mode
A Claude model capability (activated via API or supported tools) that causes the model to reason through a problem step-by-step in an extended thinking block before producing its final answer. Improves accuracy on hard planning and reasoning tasks at the cost of latency and tokens. → Ch 4
verification-before-completion
A Superpowers skill (and general principle) requiring the agent to actually run the application and observe real behaviour — not just read the code — before declaring a task done. Catches integration failures that static analysis misses. → Ch 15
Vertex AI
Google Cloud's managed ML platform, usable as a Gemini CLI backend via Application Default Credentials. Enables enterprise authentication, VPC-SC controls, and data-residency guarantees that the public Gemini API doesn't provide. → Ch 14
worktree
A Git feature (git worktree add) that checks out a branch into a separate directory, letting multiple branches coexist on disk simultaneously. Used by the Superpowers using-git-worktrees skill to give parallel sub-agents isolated workspaces without stashing or branch-switching. → Ch 11
YOLO mode
Informal name for running an agent with all confirmations disabled and maximum permissions granted — essentially auto-approve at its most aggressive. Fastest for throwaway tasks; highest blast radius for anything touching production or shared state. → Ch 14

Further Reading

Canonical documentation, curated community resources, and pointers to other guides in this series. Updated periodically — always verify URLs are current.

Canonical Documentation

Claude Code Docs

URL: https://docs.anthropic.com/claude-code

What's there: Official reference for install, slash commands, hooks, skills, plugins, settings.json schema, MCP integration, and the Claude Code SDK for programmatic use.

Freshness: Maintained by Anthropic — updated with each Claude Code release. Verify the URL is current; the canonical domain may shift between docs.anthropic.com and docs.claude.ai.

Kilo Code Docs

URL: https://kilocode.ai/docs — source at https://github.com/Kilo-Org/kilocode

What's there: Custom modes reference, BYO model configuration, MCP server integration, custom rules syntax, and the full slash command index.

Freshness: Community-maintained; check the GitHub repo for the latest release notes. The hosted docs site may lag a version behind.

Gemini CLI Docs

URL: https://github.com/google-gemini/gemini-cli

What's there: Install guide, GEMINI.md format, extensions system, slash command reference, MCP tool integration, and Vertex AI / ADC backend configuration.

Freshness: Google-maintained open-source repo. README and /docs directory are the authoritative source; verify branch for stable vs. nightly.

MCP Specification

URL: https://modelcontextprotocol.io

What's there: The full protocol specification, SDK docs for TypeScript and Python, transport layer reference (stdio, HTTP/SSE), and the official MCP server registry.

Freshness: Anthropic-maintained. The spec evolves; check the changelog before building new servers to catch breaking changes in tool/resource schema.

OpenSpec

URL: https://github.com/Fission-AI/OpenSpec

What's there: Spec-driven development methodology, CLI reference for openspec init / validate / archive, directory layout conventions, and spec lifecycle documentation.

Freshness: Verify the GitHub org — the repo may have moved. The README is the primary reference; there is no separate hosted docs site as of this writing.

SpecKit

URL: Search "SpecKit Claude Code skill" — canonical repo TBC

What's there: Reference for the /specify, /plan, /tasks, and /implement slash commands; the approval-gated pipeline; and integration with OpenSpec directory conventions.

Freshness: Distributed as a Claude Code skill; the canonical source repo is not yet publicly stable. Check the Superpowers marketplace or the Claude Code community for the latest install path.

Superpowers

URL: https://github.com/obra/superpowers-marketplace — verify current URL

What's there: The canonical Claude Code methodology plugin — brainstorming, writing plans, executing plans, TDD, systematic debugging, verification-before-completion, code review, and git worktrees skills.

Freshness: Community-maintained. The marketplace repo may have moved; search "superpowers Claude Code" to find the current canonical location and install instructions.

Antigravity (Google)

URL: Closed-source preview — search "Google Antigravity IDE agent" for current access

What's there: Google's agent-first IDE built around the Gemini model family. Designed for full-task autonomy rather than assist-on-request; integrates deeply with Google Cloud toolchains.

Freshness: As of this writing, Antigravity is in limited preview with no public docs URL. Monitor the Google Cloud blog and Google I/O announcements for GA access details.

Curated Reading List

Agentic Patterns

  • "Building effective agents" — Anthropic's canonical blog post laying out the core agent architecture patterns: augmented LLMs, prompt chaining, routing, parallelisation, orchestrator–subagent, and evaluator–optimizer. Search: "building effective agents anthropic blog"
  • "ReAct: Synergizing Reasoning and Acting in Language Models" (Yao et al., 2022) — the foundational paper behind the think–act–observe loop that most coding agents implement. Available on arXiv.
  • "Plan-and-Execute Agents" — LangChain blog post introducing the plan-then-execute architecture, separating planning from execution to reduce context churn. Search: "plan-and-execute agents langchain blog"
  • "Cognitive Architectures for Language Agents" (Sumers et al., 2023) — academic survey mapping agent memory, action, and decision-making patterns. arXiv:2309.02427.
  • "Multi-Agent Systems with LLMs" — Microsoft AutoGen technical report; covers orchestrator–worker patterns, human-in-the-loop, and group chat architectures. Search: "AutoGen multi-agent Microsoft research"

Spec-Driven Development

  • OpenSpec README — https://github.com/Fission-AI/OpenSpec — the primary reference for the proposal → spec → implementation lifecycle and directory conventions used throughout this guide.
  • "Programs as Specifications" — Dijkstra's classic formulation of correctness-by-construction; the philosophical ancestor of spec-driven dev. Search: "Dijkstra programs as specifications EWD"
  • "Specification by Example" (Adzic, 2011) — the book that popularised living documentation and acceptance-test-driven specs; directly applicable to AI-assisted development workflows.
  • "The Spec is the Test" — blog series on using formal specs as the ground truth for AI code generation, with concrete examples in LLM-assisted TDD. Search: "spec is the test AI TDD blog"

Prompting and Evals

  • Anthropic Prompt Engineering Guide — https://docs.anthropic.com/en/docs/build-with-claude/prompt-engineering/overview — the canonical reference for system prompt structure, chain-of-thought, XML tagging, and few-shot patterns.
  • "Eval-Driven Development" — Hamel Husain's writing on building regression eval suites before changing prompts; the ML equivalent of TDD for LLM behaviour. Search: "eval-driven development hamel husain"
  • "PromptBench: Towards Evaluating the Robustness of Large Language Models on Adversarial Prompts" (Zhu et al., 2023) — academic grounding for prompt regression testing. arXiv:2306.04528.
  • OpenAI Evals framework — https://github.com/openai/evals — open-source eval harness with a large library of community-contributed eval sets; useful as a pattern reference regardless of which model you use.
  • "Measuring Massive Multitask Language Understanding" (Hendrycks et al.) — foundational benchmark paper that established the modern eval methodology. Reference when designing your own domain-specific evals.

Open Source Coding Agents

  • Cline (formerly Claude Dev) — https://github.com/cline/cline — VS Code extension supporting multiple backends, MCP tools, and approval-gated file editing. One of the most active open-source coding agent projects.
  • Continue.dev — https://continue.dev — open-source VS Code / JetBrains assistant with inline completions, chat, and a custom model provider system. Strong documentation and plugin ecosystem.
  • Roo Code — https://github.com/RooVetGit/Roo-Code — a Cline fork with additional custom modes, enhanced MCP support, and a focus on team-configurable personas. Search "Roo Code VS Code" for current canonical URL.
  • "AI Coding Assistants Compared" — periodic comparison reviews published on the Sourcegraph and E2B blogs; useful for tracking how the landscape shifts between Cline, Continue, Kilo, and Cursor. Search: "AI coding assistant comparison 2025"
  • OpenHands (formerly OpenDevin) — https://github.com/All-Hands-AI/OpenHands — fully autonomous agent platform with a web UI; good reference architecture for understanding headless agent deployment patterns.

Community Resources

  • Claude Code community Discord — the primary real-time community for Claude Code users; covers skills, hooks, MCP servers, and workflow patterns. Access via the invite link in the official Claude Code docs.
  • r/ClaudeAI and r/ChatGPTCoding — Reddit communities with case studies, tool comparisons, and workflow showcases. Signal-to-noise varies; sort by "top / past month" for highest-quality threads.
  • MCP server registry — https://modelcontextprotocol.io/servers — the curated index of community-maintained MCP servers; browse before building to avoid reinventing existing integrations.
  • Latent Space podcast — https://www.latent.space — in-depth technical interviews on AI engineering; episodes on coding agents, eval frameworks, and agentic infrastructure are consistently high quality.
  • Simon Willison's blog — https://simonwillison.net — detailed, practical writing on LLM tooling, prompt engineering, and AI-assisted development workflows from a practitioner perspective.

If you liked this guide

Gen AI: Zero to Hero
